**National Institutional Ranking Framework (NIRF): Transforming Indian Higher Education**

**A Comprehensive Analysis of Methodology, Parameters, and Impact**

**Dr Venkata Rama Krishna Tottempudi**

**Sai Saran Tottempudi**

Our sincere thanks also go to the National Board of Accreditation (NBA) for serving as the ranking agency and ensuring that the framework is implemented with rigor, transparency, and accountability.

We acknowledge the contributions of regulatory bodies such as UGC, AICTE, NAAC, PCI, COA, and other councils, whose policies and quality benchmarks align closely with NIRF's objectives and support the broader vision of academic excellence. We also appreciate the role of global bibliometric databases such as Scopus, Web of Science, and PubMed, whose data provide reliable foundations for research and innovation metrics under the framework.

Special recognition is extended to the Institutional Innovation Councils (IICs), Internal Quality Assurance Cells (IQACs), and faculty members across institutions, whose active engagement in data collection, reporting, and compliance has made NIRF a practical tool for transformation. **Sai Saran Tottempudi**

**Abstract**

The National Institutional Ranking Framework (NIRF), launched in 2015 by the Ministry of Education, Government of India, has emerged as one of the most important policy instruments in higher education. It was designed to address the absence of a standardized and transparent system for evaluating universities and colleges across the nation. NIRF provides a holistic, evidence-based methodology for ranking institutions based on teaching quality, research, student outcomes, inclusivity, and reputation. By introducing a uniform framework, it has created a benchmark for academic excellence and accountability, aligning Indian higher education with both national priorities and global standards.

NIRF operates on five parameters: Teaching, Learning and Resources (TLR); Research and Professional Practice (RP); Graduation Outcomes (GO); Outreach and Inclusivity (OI); and Perception (PR). Each parameter is weighted differently depending on the type of institution—engineering, management, law, medical, or multidisciplinary university—to reflect their distinct missions. For example, RP carries greater weight in technical institutions where research drives performance, while OI and PR are more significant in general universities where diversity and perception matter. This balanced design ensures fairness and comparability across diverse institutions.

The methodology is data-driven and verification-based. Institutions upload detailed data via the NIRF portal, which is then validated using external sources such as Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, and regulatory records (UGC, AICTE, NAAC, NBA, PCI, COA). This dual-layer approach enhances credibility, minimizes data manipulation, and provides confidence to stakeholders. Scores are normalized and aggregated into an overall score out of 100, determining each institution's rank. Institutions are also required to publish their submitted data on their websites, reinforcing transparency and accountability.

Beyond ranking, NIRF has become a policy and benchmarking tool. Institutions use its metrics for internal audits, faculty development, and strategic research investments, while policymakers rely on its outcomes for initiatives such as Institutions of Eminence (IoE) and Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA) funding. For students and parents, NIRF provides a credible, government-recognized guide to selecting universities and colleges, making it a central decision-making resource.

NIRF also plays a crucial role in promoting equity, innovation, and entrepreneurship. The OI parameter pushes institutions to enhance diversity by including women, socio-economically disadvantaged groups, and differently-abled students. At the same time, RP incentivizes research, patents, and startup incubation, encouraging institutions to engage with industry and society. These directions strongly align with the National Education Policy (NEP) 2020, which emphasizes inclusion, innovation, and global competitiveness.

The National Board of Accreditation (NBA) manages the ranking exercise annually, with results published each April, widely covered in national media. Over the years, NIRF has contributed to elevating India's higher education profile on the global stage by encouraging institutions to compete on measurable outcomes. To further support institutions, several ready-to-use data collection and reporting formats have been developed and shared by educators and experts. A notable example is the open-source repository NIRF Formats on GitHub (https://github.com/tvrk1122-tech/NIRF_Formats-), which provides structured templates for institutions to systematically record teaching, research, and outcome-related data in line with NIRF requirements. Such resources bridge the gap between policy frameworks and institutional implementation, making compliance and reporting more efficient.

In essence, NIRF is more than a ranking system—it is a comprehensive framework for continuous quality improvement in Indian higher education. By combining transparency, inclusivity, and innovation, it has become an essential tool for institutions, policymakers, and students, ensuring that Indian universities and colleges are better positioned to achieve excellence, accountability, and global recognition.

**1. Introduction**

The National Institutional Ranking Framework (NIRF) was launched in 2015 by the Ministry of Education, Government of India, to provide a structured and transparent methodology for ranking higher education institutions. Prior to its introduction, India lacked a standardized national system for evaluating universities and colleges, resulting in fragmented perceptions of quality. NIRF was designed to fill this gap by adopting a holistic approach that integrates teaching quality, research performance, inclusivity, student success, and reputation into a single measurable framework. Its introduction marked a paradigm shift, aligning India's higher education evaluation system with international practices while contextualizing it to national priorities.

NIRF evaluates institutions under five core parameters: Teaching, Learning and Resources (TLR); Research and Professional Practice (RP); Graduation Outcomes (GO); Outreach and Inclusivity (OI); and Perception (PR). Each parameter carries specific weightages that vary across categories such as universities, engineering institutions, management schools, law colleges, and medical colleges. For example, research productivity and patents are weighted more heavily for technical and research-focused institutions, while inclusivity and perception carry greater importance for colleges and multidisciplinary universities. This differential weighting ensures fairness while recognizing the diversity of Indian higher education institutions.

The framework relies on a combination of institution-submitted primary data and externally verified third-party sources. Institutions provide inputs on faculty strength, financial resources, research projects, placements, and inclusivity initiatives, which are validated against records from regulatory bodies (UGC, AICTE, PCI, COA, NAAC, NBA) and global bibliometric databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed). This hybrid verification approach strengthens the reliability of the rankings and prevents inflation or manipulation of claims. Additionally, NIRF emphasizes data transparency by making institutional submissions publicly available for stakeholders to cross-check.

Over the years, NIRF has moved beyond its initial purpose of ranking to become a benchmarking and policy tool. Institutions now actively use NIRF parameters as performance indicators for internal audits, faculty recruitment policies, and research investments. Policymakers, on the other hand, employ NIRF outcomes to inform funding allocations, policy interventions, and institutional recognition schemes such as the Institutions of Eminence (IoE) and Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA) initiatives. For students and parents, NIRF provides a credible reference point for making admission choices, particularly in a country with thousands of institutions offering similar programs.

Another significant impact of NIRF lies in its role in promoting equity and inclusivity. Through its Outreach and Inclusivity (OI) parameter, NIRF encourages institutions to diversify their student and faculty demographics across gender, socio-economic background, geography, and disability status. This aspect resonates with the goals of the National Education Policy (NEP) 2020, which emphasizes inclusivity, accessibility, and holistic learning. Moreover, the emphasis on innovation, entrepreneurship, patents, and industry partnerships under the Research and Professional Practice (RP) category has motivated institutions to go beyond traditional teaching and move towards contributing to India's innovation and startup ecosystem.

In conclusion, the National Institutional Ranking Framework is not merely a tool for ranking institutions but a comprehensive quality assurance mechanism. By focusing on teaching, research, outcomes, inclusivity, and perception, NIRF addresses the multidimensional nature of higher education. It fosters competition, drives institutions towards continuous improvement, and enhances India's global educational visibility. As it continues to evolve in alignment with NEP 2020 and global benchmarks, NIRF is poised to become an indispensable pillar of India's higher education reforms, shaping institutions to deliver academic excellence, societal impact, and global competitiveness.

**2. Salient Features**

The National Institutional Ranking Framework (NIRF) represents a nationally accepted, transparent, and data-driven methodology for assessing and ranking higher education institutions in India. Its design emphasizes credibility, objectivity, and fairness through the use of five broad parameters—Teaching, Learning and Resources (TLR), Research and Professional Practices (RP), Graduation Outcomes (GO), Outreach and Inclusivity (OI), and Perception (PR). Each of these parameters carries a predefined weightage, further divided into sub-parameters to ensure a balanced and fine-grained evaluation of institutional performance. Data for these indicators is sourced both from institutions themselves and third-party repositories such as Scopus, Web of Science, and government records, with a strong emphasis on verifiability and ease of access. Scores are calculated for each sub-parameter using standardized metrics, aggregated with weightages, and normalized to an overall score out of 100, which is then used to rank institutions in specific categories such as universities, engineering, management, pharmacy, law, and medical, alongside an overall ranking. The rankings are published annually by the Ministry of Education, offering a reliable benchmark for students, parents, policymakers, and institutions, while also encouraging continuous improvement, transparency, and alignment with both national priorities and global standards.

**NIRF Methodology**

· **Core Idea**

> o The methodology is built on a set of well-defined metrics to evaluate and rank higher education institutions in India.
>
> o These metrics are standardized, quantifiable, and transparent, ensuring fairness across institutions of different types.

· **Role of the Core Committee**

> o Parameters and metrics were finalized by the NIRF Core Committee after extensive consultation with academia, policymakers, and industry.
>
> o Ensures that chosen parameters reflect the overall quality and performance of institutions.

· **Five Broad Parameters**

> o **Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) –** assessing faculty strength, infrastructure, and student–teacher ratio.
>
> o **Research & Professional Practices (RP) –** measuring publications, patents, research funding, and quality of outputs.
>
> o **Graduation Outcomes (GO) –** focusing on placements, higher studies, competitive exams, and entrepreneurship.
>
> o **Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) –** evaluating diversity, gender balance, and inclusion of socially/economically disadvantaged groups.
>
> o **Perception (PR) –** capturing employer, peer, and public reputation.

· **Sub-Parameters & Weightage**

> o Each broad parameter is broken into sub-parameters, e.g., faculty qualifications, research publications, placements.
>
> o Appropriate weightage is assigned both at parameter and sub-parameter levels.
>
> o This ensures balanced evaluation across teaching, research, outcomes, inclusivity, and reputation.

· **Data Sources**

> o Institutions provide structured data through the NIRF portal.
>
> o External third-party sources (e.g., Scopus, Web of Science, government databases) are used for validation.
>
> o Emphasis on easily verifiable data to maintain credibility.

· **Scoring System**

> o Each sub-parameter is computed into a score using standard formulas.
>
> o Sub-scores → aggregated into parameter score.
>
> o Parameter scores → weighted sum → overall institutional score (out of 100).

· **Ranking Outcome**

> o Institutions are rank-ordered based on total score.
>
> o Separate rankings for categories such as universities, engineering, management, pharmacy, law, medical, etc.
>
> o Enables students, parents, policymakers, and institutions to make informed decisions.

**NIRF Parameters → Broad Heads → Sub-Heads with Weightage Distribution**

· **Five Broad Heads (Main Parameters)**

1\. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)

2\. Research & Professional Practices (RP)

3\. Graduation Outcomes (GO)

4\. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)

5\. Perception (PR)

· **Weightage Assigned to Each Head**

1\. TLR: 30%

2\. RP: 30%

3\. GO: 20%

4\. OI: 10%

5\. PR: 10%

6\. **Total = 100%**

· **Sub-Heads Under Each Head**

> **1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (30%)**

1\. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) with emphasis on permanent faculty → 30% of TLR

2\. Faculty with Ph.D. and Experience (FQE) → 20% of TLR

3\. Financial Resources and their Utilization (FRU) → 30% of TLR

4\. Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS) → 20% of TLR

> **2. Research & Professional Practices (30%)**

5\. Publications (PU) → 35% of RP

6\. Quality of Publications (QP, indexed in Scopus/WoS, citation impact) → 35% of RP

7\. IPR & Patents (IPR) → 15% of RP

8\. Footprint of Projects, Professional Practice & Executive Development Programs (FPPP) → 15% of RP

> **3. Graduation Outcomes (20%)**

9\. Placement, Higher Studies & Entrepreneurship (GPH) → 40% of GO

10\. University Exams: Median Salary, Graduate Employment (GUE) → 25% of GO

11\. Higher Studies (HES) → 15% of GO

12\. Other Outcomes: Ph.D. Graduations, Professional Exams, Entrepreneurship (GPHD, GPHE) → 20% of GO

> **4. Outreach & Inclusivity (10%)**

13\. Regional Diversity (RD) → 30% of OI

14\. Women Diversity (WD) → 30% of OI

15\. Economically & Socially Disadvantaged Students (ESDS) → 20% of OI

16\. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) → 20% of OI

> **5. Perception (10%)**

17\. Academic & Employer Perception (PR) → 100% of this head

· **Overall Score Calculation**

1\. Sub-head scores → combined as per assigned percentages → parameter score.

2\. Parameter scores → multiplied by overall weightage → total institutional score (max 100).

3\. Institutions then rank-ordered based on this total.

**NIRF methodological feature**

· **Purpose of Data Identification**

> o Ensures that every sub-head under NIRF parameters is backed by reliable and measurable data.
>
> o Avoids subjective judgments and focuses on objective, verifiable evidence.

· **Types of Data Considered**

> o Institution-provided data: Admission numbers, student strength, faculty details, exam results, placement records, financial reports.
>
> o Third-party sourced data: Publications from Scopus/Web of Science, citation metrics, patent databases, government portals (AICTE, UGC, MHRD, AISHE, etc.).
>
> o Survey-based inputs: Employer/peer perception, alumni feedback.

· **Criteria for Data Selection**

> o Easily available: Institutions already maintain most of this data for regulatory/accreditation purposes.
>
> o Easily verifiable: Cross-checked through government/industry databases and third-party repositories.
>
> o Minimal reporting burden: Avoids demanding complicated or rare data that institutions struggle to compile.

· **Importance of Verifiability**

> o Prevents data manipulation or inflation by institutions.
>
> o Maintains credibility and trust in the ranking system.
>
> o Verification ensures rankings are transparent and evidence-based.

· **Transparency Objective**

> o Students, parents, and policymakers can trust NIRF results as the data is authentic.
>
> o Institutions gain confidence since the evaluation is based on fair, uniform, and unbiased information.
>
> o Encourages institutions to improve record-keeping and adopt data-driven management practices.

**NIRF scoring and computation process**

· **Step 1: Metric Development**

> o For each sub-head, a specific metric/formula is designed to quantify performance.
>
> o Example: ***Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR)*** metric = (Number of Permanent Faculty ÷ Total Students), capped at an optimal level.
>
> o These formulas ensure uniformity and comparability across institutions.

· **Step 2: Sub-Head Scoring**

> o Raw data collected → processed using the defined metric → converted into a normalized score (usually 0–100).
>
> o Ensures that institutions with different sizes/scales are judged fairly.

· **Step 3: Head-Level Score Aggregation**

> o All sub-head scores under a parameter are added together using their predefined sub-weightages.
>
> o Example: TLR (30%) = Weighted sum of (Faculty Ratio + Faculty with Ph.D. + Financial Resources + Student Strength).

· **Step 4: Overall Score Computation**

> o Each head score is multiplied by its overall weight (TLR 30%, RP 30%, GO 20%, OI 10%, PR 10%).
>
> o Weighted head scores are added together to give the final institutional score.

· **Step 5: Maximum Score**

> o The overall score is capped at 100.
>
> o This uniform scale allows ranking across categories (universities, engineering, management, etc.).

· **Step 6: Rank Ordering**

> o Institutions are ordered highest to lowest based on overall score.
>
> o Final NIRF Ranking List is published annually by category.

· **Salient Features of the Scoring System**

> o Normalization: Ensures fairness regardless of institutional size.
>
> o Weight Distribution: Balances teaching, research, outcomes, inclusivity, and perception.
>
> o Transparency: Formulas and weightages are published openly.
>
> o Comparability: Allows benchmarking between institutions of similar or different categories.

**NIRF methodology – Rank Ordering**

· **Conversion of Scores into Ranks**

> o Once the overall score (out of 100) is computed for every institution, they are arranged in descending order.
>
> o The institution with the highest score is ranked \#1, followed by others sequentially.

· **Category-wise Ranking**

> o Institutions are not compared across all types together.
>
> o Rankings are published separately for categories such as:
>
> ▪ Universities
>
> ▪ Engineering
>
> ▪ Management
>
> ▪ Pharmacy
>
> ▪ Medical
>
> ▪ Law
>
> ▪ Architecture & Planning
>
> ▪ Dental, Agriculture, etc.
>
> o Additionally, an Overall Ranking list is also published.

· **Handling of Ties**

> o If two or more institutions obtain the same score, they are given the same rank.
>
> o The next rank is skipped (e.g., if two institutions tie at Rank 5, the next one gets Rank 7).

· **Annual Publication**

> o The Ministry of Education, Government of India, releases the NIRF rankings once every year.
>
> o Results are made public on the official NIRF website for transparency.

· **Impact of Rank Ordering**

> o Benchmarking: Institutions know where they stand nationally.
>
> o Decision-making tool: Helps students and parents choose the right institutions.
>
> o Policy-making: Guides government and regulators in planning support and incentives.
>
> o Reputation & Competition: Higher ranks enhance visibility and brand value of institutions.

**3. Eligibility for Overall and Discipline Specific Rankings**

· **(i) Overall Ranking for Eligible Institutions**

> o All institutions, regardless of discipline or type (comprehensive, specialized, multidisciplinary), will be considered for an overall ranking list.
>
> o Eligibility criterion → the institution must have a minimum intake/enrollment of 1000 students across UG and PG programs.
>
> o Ensures that only reasonably large institutions with diverse academic activity are compared at the national level.

· **(ii) Discipline-Specific Ranking**

> o In addition to overall ranking, institutions will also receive a discipline-wise ranking (e.g., engineering, management, pharmacy).
>
> o Helps students compare institutions within a field rather than only by overall score.

· **(iii) Ranking for Focused Institutions (\<1000 intake)**

> o Institutions with a single major discipline (Engineering, Medical, Law, Management, Pharmacy, Architecture, UG degree colleges in Arts/Science/Commerce) and less than 1000 students will not appear in overall ranking.
>
> o Instead, they will only receive a discipline-specific ranking, ensuring fairness for smaller but specialized institutions.

· **(iv) Separate Registration for Schools/Departments**

> o Faculties or Schools within larger universities (e.g., Faculty of Law, School of Engineering, Department of Management, etc.) must register separately and provide discipline-specific data if they wish to be ranked independently.
>
> o Encourages universities to highlight the strength of their specialized departments at the national level.
>
> o Only those that opt-in through the registration portal will be considered.

· **(v) Participation of Undergraduate Teaching Institutions**

> o UG teaching colleges (including degree colleges affiliated to universities) are invited to participate.
>
> o Expands the ranking exercise beyond central universities and IITs/IIMs to include state colleges, private colleges, and other teaching-focused institutions.

· **(vi) Minimum Threshold for Discipline Rankings**

> o NIRF will publish discipline-specific rankings only if a significant number of institutions participate.
>
> o Rankings will also require the presence of well-known institutions in that field and acceptable quality scores.
>
> o Final decision lies with NIRF, ensuring rankings remain meaningful and competitive.

· **(vii) Exclusion of Open & Affiliating Universities**

> o Open universities (distance/online education) and affiliating universities (that oversee many colleges without significant teaching/research of their own) are not eligible.
>
> o However, if such universities have a physical teaching or research campus, they may participate using data only from that campus.
>
> o Data from their affiliating or distance-learning functions cannot be included.

· **(viii) Graduation Requirement for Eligibility**

> o To ensure reliability, only institutions that have graduated at least three batches of students in full-time UG or PG programs are considered.
>
> o Minimum program duration: 3 years (UG) and 2 years (PG).
>
> o This avoids ranking newly established institutions that do not yet have proven academic outcomes.

**NIRF Ranking Eligibility & Type of Ranking**

| **Condition**                                                                                                                                                        | **Eligibility**                                                                       | **Type of Ranking**                     |
|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|
| Institution with ≥1000 student intake/enrolled (UG + PG)                                                                                                             | Eligible                                                                              | Overall Rank + Discipline-Specific Rank |
| Institution with \<1000 student intake, focused on a single main discipline (Engineering, Medical, Law, Management, Pharmacy, Architecture, Arts, Science, Commerce) | Eligible                                                                              | Discipline-Specific Rank Only           |
| University Schools/Departments (e.g., Faculty of Law, School of Engineering, Management Dept., Pharmacy)                                                             | Eligible if they register separately and provide discipline-specific data             | Discipline-Specific Rank                |
| Undergraduate teaching institutions (including degree colleges affiliated to universities)                                                                           | Eligible to participate                                                               | Discipline-Specific Rank                |
| Disciplines with insufficient participation (few institutions, weak representation)                                                                                  | Not eligible                                                                          | No Ranking Published                    |
| Open Universities / Affiliating Universities (State or Central)                                                                                                      | Not eligible as affiliators; only physical teaching/research campuses may submit data | Campus-specific Ranking (if applicable) |
| Institutions with \<3 batches of graduates in UG (≥3 years) or PG (≥2 years)                                                                                         | Not eligible                                                                          | No Ranking Considered                   |

· **Similarity in Scoring**

> o Both overall rankings and discipline-specific rankings follow the same basic methodology:
>
> ▪ Data collection → Metric application → Sub-head scoring → Parameter scoring → Overall score.
>
> o Institutions are assessed under the same five broad heads:
>
> ▪ Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)
>
> ▪ Research & Professional Practices (RP)
>
> ▪ Graduation Outcomes (GO)
>
> ▪ Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)
>
> ▪ Perception (PR)

· **Differences in Weights**

> o While the framework remains the same, weightage distribution is adjusted for discipline-specific rankings.
>
> o This is done to account for variations across disciplines and to ensure fairness.

· **Examples of Discipline-Specific Adjustments**

> o Engineering/Management/Pharmacy: More weight given to Research & Professional Practices (RP) and Graduation Outcomes (GO), as research, placements, and professional practice are critical.
>
> o Medical/Health Sciences: Greater emphasis on Research Publications (RP) and Graduation Outcomes, since quality healthcare research and graduate competence are paramount.
>
> o Law/Architecture/Arts & Humanities: More emphasis on Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) and Perception (PR), reflecting diversity, access, and professional reputation.
>
> o UG Teaching Colleges: Higher focus on Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) and Graduation Outcomes (GO) rather than research, since their mission is primarily teaching-focused.

· **Rationale for Adjustments**

> o Prevents smaller or discipline-focused institutions from being disadvantaged in comparisons with larger, multidisciplinary universities.
>
> o Makes the ranking context-sensitive so that institutions are evaluated according to their core strengths and missions.

· **Outcome**

> o Both overall rankings and discipline-specific rankings are comparable in terms of methodology, but discipline-specific lists better capture field-level performance.
>
> o This ensures the credibility and acceptance of rankings among stakeholders such as students, parents, employers, and policymakers.

**Overall vs. Discipline-Specific Ranking in NIRF**

| **Parameter**                          | **Overall Ranking (Weight %)** | **Discipline-Specific Ranking (Weight %)**                                                                        | **Justification for Adjustment**                                                                                                                       |
|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)   | 30                             | 30–40 (higher for UG colleges, lower for research-heavy disciplines)                                              | UG-focused institutions rely primarily on teaching quality, so TLR weight is emphasized in discipline-specific rankings (Arts, Commerce, UG Colleges). |
| Research & Professional Practices (RP) | 30                             | 25–40 (higher for Engineering, Medical, Pharmacy, Management)                                                     | Research output, patents, and projects are more critical in professional and research-intensive fields; weight adjusted upwards in these disciplines.  |
| Graduation Outcomes (GO)               | 20                             | 20–30 (higher for Engineering, Management, Medical; moderate for others)                                          | Graduate employability, placements, median salary, and higher studies matter more in professional fields; hence more emphasis.                         |
| Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)            | 10                             | 10–15 (higher in disciplines like Arts, Law, Humanities)                                                          | Diversity and inclusivity are important in social sciences, law, and liberal arts; thus weight adjusted upwards.                                       |
| Perception (PR)                        | 10                             | 5–15 (higher in fields where reputation strongly influences admission/employment, e.g., Law, Management, Medical) | Employer/peer reputation is more decisive in certain disciplines; hence perception carries more weight in discipline-specific rankings.                |
| **Total**                              | **100**                        | **100**                                                                                                           | **Adjustments ensure rankings remain fair, discipline-sensitive, and contextually relevant.**                                                          |

**4. Data Collection**

· **Challenge of Data Availability**

> o At present, there is no single third-party database in India that comprehensively provides all the information required for NIRF scoring (e.g., teaching resources, research output, placements, inclusivity measures).
>
> o While some data points can be verified through sources like Scopus, Web of Science, UGC, AICTE, MHRD, and patent databases, many institution-specific details (faculty strength, infrastructure, financial resources, inclusivity support) are not centrally available.

· **Requirement for Institutional Data Submission**

> o Institutions wishing to participate in NIRF rankings must submit data directly through the NIRF portal.
>
> o Data must be filled strictly in the prescribed format to ensure standardization across all institutions.
>
> o This includes information on:
>
> ▪ Faculty and student strength
>
> ▪ Research projects and publications
>
> ▪ Placement records and higher studies
>
> ▪ Financial resources and utilization
>
> ▪ Inclusivity and diversity indicators
>
> ▪ Infrastructure and facilities

· **Verification Process**

> o Submitted data is cross-checked against third-party databases wherever available.
>
> o Verification ensures transparency, prevents manipulation, and maintains credibility of the rankings.

· **Deadlines and Compliance**

> o NIRF sets a strict deadline each year for data submission.
>
> o Institutions failing to submit data before the deadline will not be included in that year's ranking cycle.
>
> o Deadlines and instructions are published on the official NIRF portal.

· **Importance for Institutions**

> o Participation allows institutions to showcase their strengths at a national level.
>
> o Submitting accurate and timely data is crucial, since rankings are used by students, parents, policymakers, and funding agencies for decision-making.

**NIRF Data Submission Checklist**

| **Data Category** | **Examples**                                                                 | **Source (Institution / External)**             | **Verification Method**                           |
|-------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| Student Strength  | UG & PG enrollment, doctoral students, intake capacity                       | Institution's admission records, AISHE database | Cross-check with AISHE, UGC, AICTE reports        |
| Faculty Details   | Faculty count, permanent vs. contractual, qualifications (Ph.D., experience) | HR/Administration records, Institution MIS      | Verified through UGC/AICTE faculty approval lists |

| **Data Category**         | **Examples**                                                                                               | **Source (Institution / External)**                                    | **Verification Method**                                            |
|---------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Teaching Resources        | Infrastructure, classrooms, labs, libraries, ICT facilities                                                | Institution facilities database                                        | On-site audits, photographs, uploaded documents                    |
| Financial Resources       | Annual budget, expenditure on academic infrastructure, salaries, R&D spending                              | Institution audited financial statements                               | Audit reports, submitted financial statements                      |
| Research & Publications   | Journal papers, conference papers, citations, patents                                                      | Faculty research records, Scopus, Web of Science, Indian Patent Office | Scopus/WoS indexing, citation reports, patent filings              |
| Projects & Consultancy    | Sponsored research projects, consultancy revenue, professional practice programs                           | Institution R&D cell, MoUs with industry                               | Project sanction letters, funding agency databases                 |
| Graduation Outcomes       | Placement numbers, salary packages, higher studies, exam results (GATE, NET, UPSC)                         | Placement cell, Alumni records                                         | Employer feedback, alumni surveys, offer letters                   |
| Inclusivity & Diversity   | Gender ratio, regional diversity, representation of disadvantaged groups, facilities for differently-abled | Student records, scholarship lists                                     | Government quota records, institutional facilities reports         |
| Perception                | Employer/peer reviews, academic reputation surveys                                                         | NIRF survey process (external)                                         | NIRF-administered perception surveys                               |
| Institutional Eligibility | Age of institution, number of graduating batches (≥3)                                                      | Institution history, registrar office                                  | Verified by accreditation agencies, affiliating university records |

**Data transparency and hosting**

· **Mandatory Data Hosting**

> o Institutions must upload all submitted NIRF data on their own official website.
>
> o The data must be accessible via a prominent link named “NIRF” placed clearly on the homepage.
>
> o This ensures that stakeholders (students, parents, faculty, policymakers, and the public) can directly access the same data used for rankings.

· **Timeline Requirement**

> o Data for India Rankings 2024 must be hosted immediately after final submission to the NIRF portal.
>
> o The hosted data must remain available for a minimum of three years, maintaining continuity and accountability.

· **Feedback Mechanism**

> o Institutions must provide a dedicated email address on the NIRF webpage to receive comments and feedback.
>
> o This encourages external stakeholders to highlight errors, discrepancies, or missing information.

· **Corrective Action**

> o Institutions are required to proactively review feedback in an objective manner.
>
> o Corrections must be made within the official correction window announced by NIRF on its website.
>
> o This step strengthens accuracy, reliability, and trust in institutional data.

· **Purpose and Benefits**

> o Transparency: Enhances credibility of both the institution and the NIRF ranking system.
>
> o Accountability: Prevents data manipulation and builds public trust.
>
> o Engagement: Allows stakeholders to actively participate in validating institutional claims.
>
> o Compliance: Mandatory for continued eligibility in rankings; non-compliance can lead to exclusion.

>

>

**Correction Policy**

· All feedback will be objectively examined by the institution's NIRF committee.

· Corrections, if warranted, will be made within the time slot announced by NIRF.

· Place a prominent “NIRF” link on the homepage menu or header that redirects to this disclosure page.

· Ensure all downloadable files are in PDF format for consistency and easy access.

· Maintain version control of uploaded files (v1, v2 if corrections are made).

**Physical verification and ethical compliance**

· **Authority of NIRF**

> o NIRF has the mandate to conduct physical checks at institutions when necessary.
>
> o These checks may include:
>
> ▪ Reviewing original institutional records (student enrollment, faculty details, placements, etc.).
>
> ▪ Inspecting audited accounts to verify financial data submitted.
>
> ▪ Examining supporting evidence for research, publications, patents, and projects.

· **Purpose of Physical Checks**

> o To ensure that data submitted aligns with reality and has not been manipulated.
>
> o To uphold the principles of ethical behaviour in data reporting.
>
> o To build trust and credibility in the NIRF ranking process.

· **Expectations from Institutions**

> o Institutions must fully co-operate with NIRF officials during physical inspections.
>
> o They must provide unhindered access to requested records, facilities, and staff.
>
> o Cooperation demonstrates transparency and accountability.

· **Consequences of Non-Compliance**

> o Refusal or failure to cooperate during checks may result in:
>
> ▪ Disqualification from the current ranking cycle.
>
> ▪ Possible debarment from future participation in NIRF rankings.
>
> o Such penalties protect the integrity of the framework by discouraging unethical practices.

· **Importance for Institutions**

> o Promotes a culture of honest reporting and ethical governance.
>
> o Prevents reputational damage caused by exposure of false claims.
>
> o Encourages institutions to maintain accurate, verifiable records at all times.

**NIRF Physical Verification Compliance Checklist**

| **What to Keep Ready**           | **Examples of Documents**                                                                      | **Verification Method**                                                                |
|----------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Student Enrollment Records       | Admission registers, student ID lists, fee receipts, enrollment statistics                     | Cross-check with AISHE data, UGC/AICTE approvals, random student ID verification       |
| Faculty Records                  | Appointment letters, faculty qualification certificates, service registers, salary slips       | Verify against UGC/AICTE faculty approval lists, interviews with faculty               |
| Infrastructure & Resources       | Inventory of classrooms, labs, library resources, ICT facilities, timetables                   | Physical inspection, photographs, campus visit validation                              |
| Financial Accounts               | Audited financial statements, annual reports, budget allocation and utilization records        | Review of auditor's certification, tally with expenditure proofs                       |
| Research & Publications          | Copies of published papers, Scopus/WoS indexing, patent certificates, project sanction letters | Verify via Scopus, Web of Science, Indian Patent Office databases                      |
| Sponsored Projects & Consultancy | MoUs with industry, consultancy agreements, project completion reports                         | Confirm with funding agencies, industry partners                                       |
| Placement Records                | Offer letters, placement reports, salary packages, higher studies confirmation letters         | Random checks with employers, alumni surveys                                           |
| Inclusivity & Diversity Data     | Scholarship records, student category-wise admissions, disability support facilities           | Cross-check with government scholarship databases, physical verification of facilities |
| Perception Inputs                | Employer feedback forms, peer review records                                                   | Validation through NIRF-administered surveys                                           |
| Institutional Eligibility Proof  | Accreditation certificates, affiliation letters, records of graduating batches (≥3)            | Verified with NAAC/UGC/AICTE databases                                                 |

**NIRF guideline on data sourcing for parameters like Research and Patents**

· **Use of International Databases**

> o For parameters such as Research & Professional Practices (RP), NIRF relies on internationally recognized databases.
>
> o Examples include:
>
> ▪ Scopus and Web of Science (WoS): For publications, citations, h-index, journal quality.
>
> ▪ Indian Patent Office (IPO) and World Intellectual Property Organization (WIPO): For patents filed, published, or granted.
>
> ▪ Other global citation databases and indexing services for validation.

· **Flexibility in Source Selection**

> o NIRF reserves the right to exclude a data source if it finds inaccuracies, gaps, or bias.
>
> o It may also include additional databases to broaden coverage and ensure fairness.
>
> o This flexibility prevents institutions from gaming the system by over-relying on one database.

· **Direct Access to Data**

> o NIRF will directly extract the relevant data from these databases to minimize dependence on institutional self-reporting.
>
> o This ensures authenticity, reliability, and uniformity in evaluating research and patent outputs.

· **Collaboration with Resource Publishers**

> o If necessary, NIRF may work directly with database publishers (e.g., Elsevier for Scopus, Clarivate for WoS, IPO/WIPO for patents).
>
> o Collaboration ensures accurate integration of institutional records into the ranking framework.

· **Implications for Institutions**

> o Institutions must ensure their research outputs are properly indexed in international databases.
>
> o Faculty and researchers should consistently update their affiliation details to avoid data mismatches.
>
> o Patent filings must be properly recorded with national/international offices for recognition in rankings.

· **Significance of this Approach**

> o Enhances credibility by using trusted, globally recognized data.
>
> o Minimizes chances of false reporting or inflation of achievements.
>
> o Encourages institutions to strengthen their research visibility and patent activities in standard international platforms.

**NIRF – Data Sources for Research & Patents**

| **Parameter**                          | **Preferred Databases**                                                            | **Example Metrics Used**                                                    | **Notes**                                                              |
|----------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Research Publications (PU)             | Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed (Medical), Indian Citation Index              | Number of publications, indexed journals, publication per faculty           | Ensures use of peer-reviewed, globally recognized journals             |
| Quality of Publications (QP)           | Scopus (Citations), Web of Science (Impact Factor, h-index), SNIP, SJR             | Citation counts, Field-Weighted Citation Impact, journal quality indicators | Validates not just quantity but also impact of research                |
| Patents (IPR)                          | Indian Patent Office (IPO), WIPO, USPTO, EPO                                       | Number of patents filed, published, granted; commercialization data         | Institutions must ensure proper recording with official patent offices |
| Research Funding & Projects (FPPP)     | Government databases (DST, DBT, ICSSR, AICTE, UGC), International funding agencies | Sponsored projects, consultancy revenue, funded research outcomes           | Data verified with sanction letters and funding databases              |
| Graduate Outcomes (linked to research) | Institutional records, Alumni tracer studies, employer validation                  | Ph.D. completions, higher studies in research domains                       | Cross-checked with graduation data submitted to NIRF                   |

· **Policy intent**

> o NIRF may revise, add, drop, or re-define metrics/weights when data realities or methodological improvements demand it.
>
> o Objective is rationalisation: preserving fairness, validity, and comparability across institutions and disciplines.

· **Triggers for modification**

> o Data quality issues (missing, noisy, unverifiable, or non-standardised submissions).
>
> o Discipline shifts (e.g., new research norms, altered placement ecosystems).
>
> o Gaming detection (unintended incentives that distort behaviour).
>
> o Source dependency risks (over-reliance on a single database; coverage gaps).
>
> o Policy/Regulatory updates (national priorities, inclusion mandates, NEP-aligned indicators).

· **Scope of changes**

> o Metric definitions (formulae, caps, normalisation, thresholds).
>
> o Weight redistributions (between/within TLR, RP, GO, OI, PR).
>
> o Data sources (add/remove external databases; alter verification layers).
>
> o Eligibility filters & aggregation rules (e.g., treatment of outliers, tie-handling, cohort windows).

· **Governance & transparency**

> o Changes are formally notified at the time of ranking announcement.
>
> o Ensures a single, authoritative version is used for that year's computation.
>
> o Institutions should not assume year-to-year continuity; always review official notices.

· **Implications for institutions**

> o Maintain metric-agnostic, auditable data pipelines (clear provenance, versioned snapshots).
>
> o Stress-test internal scores under plausible weight/metric shifts (sensitivity analysis).
>
> o Keep evidence repositories current (faculty, research, placements, finance, inclusivity).
>
> o Monitor data coverage in external indices (Scopus/WoS, IPO/WIPO, funding portals).

· **Good-practice readiness checklist (internal)**

> o Data dictionary aligned to current NIRF specs (fields, units, sampling window).
>
> o Change log documenting any institutional recalculations after NIRF updates.
>
> o Scenario workbook to simulate score impact for ± changes in weights/limits.
>
> o Source parity audit (cross-verify institutional counts vs. external databases).
>
> o Ethics & QA sign-off before submission (prevents last-minute corrections).

· **Ready-to-use change-log table (embed in your book/portal)**

> o Fields: Year → Parameter → Metric/Sub-metric → Old Definition/Weight → New Definition/Weight → Rationale → Data Impact → Action Required → Owner → Deadline.
>
> o Usage: Populate upon NIRF notification; drive campus-wide updates and communications.

· **Key takeaway**

> o Metric flexibility protects the integrity of rankings as contexts evolve; proactive, well-documented data management ensures institutions remain resilient to such changes.

**NIRF Metric Change-Log Table**

*The following records are presented as cards because the source table has more than five columns (Year, Parameter, Metric/Sub-metric, Old Definition/Weight, New Definition/Weight, Rationale, Data Impact, Action Required, Owner, Deadline).*

| **Label**                   | **Value**                                                   |
|-----------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Old Definition / Weight** | Count of Scopus-indexed publications only (Weight = 35%)    |
| **New Definition / Weight** | Count of Scopus + WoS-indexed publications (Weight = 30%)   |
| **Rationale**               | To broaden database coverage and ensure fairness            |
| **Data Impact**             | Slight increase in publication counts for some institutions |
| **Action Required**         | Update publication records, re-verify faculty profiles      |
| **Owner**                   | Research Cell / Dean (R&D)                                  |
| **Deadline**                | March 2024                                                  |

| **Label**                   | **Value**                                                    |
|-----------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Old Definition / Weight** | Median Salary considered only (Weight = 40%)                 |
| **New Definition / Weight** | Median Salary + Placement Percentage combined (Weight = 35%) |
| **Rationale**               | To better capture employability                              |
| **Data Impact**             | Changes graduate outcome calculation                         |
| **Action Required**         | Placement cell to recompile placement reports                |
| **Owner**                   | Training & Placement Officer                                 |
| **Deadline**                | March 2024                                                   |

**2025 – Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – Gender Diversity (WD)**

| **Label**                   | **Value**                                         |
|-----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| **Old Definition / Weight** | Female student ratio (Weight = 30%)               |
| **New Definition / Weight** | Female + Transgender student ratio (Weight = 30%) |

| **Action Required**         | Update student admission datasets                 |

| **Label**                   | **Value**                                               |
|-----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|
| **Old Definition / Weight** | Count included all faculty (Weight = 30%)               |
| **New Definition / Weight** | Count includes only permanent faculty (Weight = 30%)    |
| **Rationale**               | To discourage contractual-only appointments             |
| **Data Impact**             | Drop in ratio for contract-heavy institutions           |
| **Action Required**         | Revise HR records and mark permanent faculty separately |
| **Owner**                   | HR & Academic Audit Cell                                |
| **Deadline**                | Feb 2025                                                |

· **How to use this table:**

· Update rows every year when NIRF publishes metric changes.

· Assign owners (responsible departments/persons) for quick action.

· Use the deadline field to align with NIRF's submission cut-off dates.

· Keep this table online and accessible for transparency and tracking.

**NIRF guideline regarding promotional/canvassing emails during the ranking exercise**

· **Context of the Rule**

> o NIRF rankings include a Perception (PR) parameter, which is partly based on surveys of peers, academics, and employers.
>
> o Institutions may be tempted to influence perception surveys by sending bulk promotional or canvassing emails asking stakeholders to “vote” for them.

· **Definition of Canvassing**

> o Any activity where an institution directly contacts survey participants (employers, academics, peers) with the intent to bias their responses.
>
> o Examples include:
>
> ▪ Mass emails requesting positive survey ratings.
>
> ▪ Advertising campaigns specifically urging stakeholders to support them in NIRF surveys.
>
> ▪ Incentivizing or pressuring students, alumni, or employers to cast favourable opinions.

· **NIRF's Position**

> o Such practices are strictly prohibited, as they compromise the objectivity and integrity of the survey process.
>
> o NIRF aims to ensure that perception scores are based on genuine professional experience and reputation, not artificial influence or marketing tactics.

· **Penalty for Violation**

> o If an institution is found guilty of canvassing:
>
> ▪ All votes received in the survey will be nullified (i.e., their perception score will be set to zero).
>
> ▪ This can significantly reduce their overall NIRF score, since Perception carries 10% weight in the ranking framework.
>
> ▪ Repeat violations may also damage institutional reputation and credibility.

· **How NIRF Detects Violations**

> o Monitoring of email traffic and reports from survey respondents.
>
> o Use of spam detection tools to identify bulk promotional emails.
>
> o Complaints raised by stakeholders who receive such canvassing communications.

· **Implications for Institutions**

> o Institutions must maintain a code of ethical conduct during the ranking cycle.
>
> o They should not engage in canvassing, but instead focus on genuine quality improvements that naturally build reputation.
>
> o Institutions are encouraged to improve perception scores through:
>
> ▪ Strong academic-industry partnerships.
>
> ▪ High-quality research output.
>
> ▪ Alumni success and recognition.
>
> ▪ Participation in national and international academic networks.

· **Key Takeaway**

> o Perception must be earned, not manipulated.
>
> o Promotional emails during the ranking process not only fail to improve scores but can backfire drastically, leading to a zero perception score.

**Do's and Don'ts Checklist for institutions during the NIRF survey phase**

**Do's for Institutions**

· **Provide Accurate Data**

> o Submit institutional data in the prescribed format without exaggeration or omission.

· **Maintain Transparency**

> o Host the submitted data on your institution's official website under a clear NIRF link.

· **Encourage Genuine Engagement**

> o Build long-term goodwill with alumni, industry, and academic peers through collaborations, not canvassing.

· **Ensure Ethical Practices**

> o Train staff and committees about ethical guidelines in ranking participation.

· **Prepare Evidence Repository**

> o Keep audited records, research outputs, placement reports, and diversity data ready for possible NIRF verification.

· **Monitor Communications**

> o Ensure no faculty, staff, or students send messages that can be interpreted as canvassing.

· **Promote Quality through Action**

> o Focus on academic achievements, research contributions, and community service to naturally improve perception.

**Don'ts for Institutions**

· **No Canvassing or Promotional Emails**

> o Do not send bulk emails or messages asking stakeholders to vote positively in perception surveys.

· **No Misrepresentation of Data**

> o Avoid inflating publication counts, placements, faculty strength, or infrastructure claims.

· **No Incentivizing Stakeholders**

> o Do not offer benefits or rewards to alumni, students, or employers for favourable survey responses.

· **No Pressurizing Students or Faculty**

> o Discourage internal campaigns urging staff or students to influence survey outcomes.

· **No Last-minute Cosmetic Changes**

> o Avoid temporary measures (like short-term faculty appointments) solely for boosting rankings.

· **No Withholding Negative Feedback**

> o Accept and address stakeholder comments transparently instead of suppressing or ignoring them.

**Key Reminder**

· Violations (especially canvassing) may result in Perception scores being set to zero and can even damage institutional credibility in future ranking cycles.

**5. Implementation Details**

**National Board of Accreditation (NBA) as the Ranking Agency**

· **Role of NBA in NIRF**

> o The National Board of Accreditation (NBA) has been entrusted to act as the Ranking Agency for NIRF.
>
> o NBA will continue this responsibility for India Rankings 2024, just as it did in the previous year.

· **Why NBA?**

> o NBA is an autonomous body under the Ministry of Education (MoE), Government of India.
>
> o It already has expertise in evaluating academic institutions, quality assurance, and accreditation processes.
>
> o Its experience ensures that ranking procedures are conducted with professionalism, transparency, and fairness.

· **Functions of NBA as Ranking Agency**

> o Manage the end-to-end ranking process on behalf of NIRF.
>
> o Collect institutional data via the official NIRF portal.
>
> o Validate submitted data (cross-check with third-party databases like Scopus, WoS, Patent offices, AISHE).
>
> o Administer perception surveys (employers, academics, peers).
>
> o Compute scores using approved NIRF methodology and weightages.
>
> o Ensure adherence to ethical guidelines (no canvassing, manipulation, or false reporting).
>
> o Announce results in coordination with the Ministry of Education.

· **Benefits of NBA as Ranking Agency**

> o Continuity: Institutions are already familiar with NBA's procedures from previous cycles.
>
> o Expertise: NBA brings a strong background in accreditation and evaluation aligned with outcome-based education.
>
> o Trustworthiness: NBA's independent functioning enhances credibility of rankings.
>
> o Accountability: Institutions can appeal or clarify with a recognized and established body.

· **Implications for Institutions**

> o Institutions must follow the standardized formats and procedures prescribed by NBA for NIRF rankings.
>
> o Data must be prepared in alignment with NBA's accreditation-style evidence requirements (audits, documentation, verifiable proofs).
>
> o Compliance with NBA-led processes is mandatory for participation in NIRF 2024 rankings.

**Registration and data submission for NIRF**

· **Registration Process**

> o Institutions interested in participating in the NIRF ranking exercise must register on the official NIRF portal.
>
> o Registration involves providing institutional details such as:
>
> ▪ Name of institution and type (University, Engineering, Management, Medical, etc.)
>
> ▪ Location and contact details
>
> ▪ Accreditation/affiliation status (UGC, AICTE, NAAC, NBA, etc.)
>
> ▪ Nodal officer details for communication with NIRF.

· **Data Submission**

> o After registration, institutions are required to submit their data online using the structured format provided on the NIRF portal.
>
> o The data submission covers all five broad parameters:
>
> ▪ Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)
>
> ▪ Research & Professional Practices (RP)
>
> ▪ Graduation Outcomes (GO)
>
> ▪ Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)
>
> ▪ Perception (PR)
>
> o Sub-parameters and metrics within each category must also be filled accurately.

· **Online Facility**

> o NIRF provides a dedicated online data submission facility to ensure standardization.
>
> o The system includes:
>
> ▪ Data input forms for each parameter.
>
> ▪ Drop-down menus and fixed formats to avoid misreporting.
>
> ▪ Upload options for supporting documents (PDFs, scanned reports, audited statements).
>
> ▪ Validation checks to ensure data completeness.

· **Advantages of Online Submission**

> o Standardization: Ensures uniformity across all institutions.
>
> o Transparency: Institutions know exactly what data is required and how it will be used.
>
> o Efficiency: Eliminates paperwork, manual errors, and delays.
>
> o Traceability: Data submissions are logged with timestamps for accountability.

· **Institutional Responsibility**

> o Data submitted must be accurate, verifiable, and free of exaggeration.
>
> o Institutions are required to host the same submitted data on their official website (as per transparency norms).
>
> o Failure to register or submit data within the deadline results in exclusion from that year's ranking exercise.

**Key takeaway:** Participation in NIRF rankings begins with registration on the NIRF portal followed by structured online data submission, ensuring uniformity, transparency, and fairness across all institutions.

**NIRF guideline on data authentication**

· **Need for Authentication**

> o To maintain the credibility and integrity of rankings, NIRF cannot rely solely on self-reported institutional data.
>
> o Authentication ensures that institutions are not inflating numbers or misrepresenting achievements.

· **Who Conducts Authentication?**

> o NIRF itself: Through internal validation checks on submitted data.
>
> o Partner agencies: Third-party organizations, government bodies, or recognized data providers may assist in verification.
>
> ▪ Examples: Scopus, Web of Science, Indian Patent Office, UGC, AICTE, NAAC, NBA, DST, DBT.

· **Scope of Authentication**

> o Research & Publications: Verified through citation databases (Scopus, WoS, PubMed).
>
> o Patents & IPR: Verified via Indian Patent Office (IPO), WIPO, or other patent registries.
>
> o Faculty & Student Strength: Cross-checked with AISHE, UGC, AICTE databases.
>
> o Financial Data: Reviewed through audited reports and government funding records.
>
> o Graduation Outcomes: Placement claims checked with employer feedback, alumni records, or industry surveys.
>
> o Inclusivity Data: Cross-referenced with government scholarships and demographic records.

· **When Authentication is Applied**

> o Wherever necessary: If data looks inconsistent, suspicious, or unusually high/low.
>
> o Where feasible: If reliable external sources exist to validate the information.
>
> o Ensures flexibility so that NIRF can adapt verification methods depending on the type of data.

· **Benefits of Authentication**

> o Transparency: Institutions are held accountable for accuracy.
>
> o Fairness: Prevents manipulation, ensuring all institutions are judged equally.
>
> o Reliability: Stakeholders (students, parents, policymakers) can trust the ranking outcomes.
>
> o Continuous Improvement: Encourages institutions to maintain accurate and standardized data systems.

· **Institutional Implications**

> o Institutions must keep supporting documents and evidence repositories ready (faculty lists, financial audits, placement records).
>
> o They should ensure their research and patents are properly indexed in international/national databases.
>
> o Failure in authentication may lead to downgraded scores or even exclusion from the rankings.

**NIRF Data Authentication Responsibility Matrix**

| **Parameter**                          | **Data to be Authenticated**                                                                           | **Agency / Source**                                                     | **Mode of Verification**                                                    |
|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)   | Student enrollment, faculty count, qualifications, financial resources                                 | AISHE, UGC, AICTE, Institutional audited reports                        | Cross-check with official databases, verify documents, review audit reports |
| Research & Professional Practices (RP) | Publications, citations, research quality                                                              | Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed (Medical), Indian Citation Index   | Direct database queries, citation reports, h-index validation               |
| Patents & IPR (RP sub-metric)          | Patents filed, published, granted, commercialized                                                      | Indian Patent Office (IPO), WIPO, USPTO, EPO                            | Patent number lookup, verification from patent registries                   |
| Projects & Consultancy (RP sub-metric) | Sponsored projects, consultancy revenue, executive programs                                            | DST, DBT, ICSSR, AICTE, UGC, industry partners                          | Project sanction letters, funding agency databases, MoU verification        |
| Graduation Outcomes (GO)               | Placements, higher studies, median salary, exam results, Ph.D. completions                             | Employer feedback, Alumni tracer studies, Placement cell records, AICTE | Employer surveys, alumni confirmation, placement reports                    |
| Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)            | Gender ratio, regional diversity, disadvantaged group representation, facilities for differently-abled | Government scholarship databases, Institutional records                 | Scholarship award verification, on-site facility audits                     |
| Perception (PR)                        | Academic/peer reputation, employer reputation                                                          | NIRF-administered perception survey                                     | Independent surveys conducted by NIRF (no institutional intervention)       |

**Usage:**

· Institutions can use this matrix as a compliance guide to prepare evidence for each parameter.

· Helps NIRF and partner agencies streamline data validation responsibilities.

· Prevents last-minute discrepancies by keeping authenticated data sources ready in advance.

**NIRF's data processing and ranking timeline**

· **Automated Data Processing**

> o Once institutions submit their data on the NIRF portal, the system extracts relevant information using standardized software tools.
>
> o The software applies predefined metrics and formulas to compute sub-parameter scores, aggregate parameter scores, and overall scores (out of 100).
>
> o This automation ensures objectivity, speed, and consistency in processing.

· **Computation of Metrics**

> o Data is mapped to the five NIRF parameters (TLR, RP, GO, OI, PR).
>
> o Each parameter's sub-metrics are scored based on institutional submissions and third-party authenticated data.
>
> o Weighted aggregation produces final scores, ensuring comparability across institutions.

· **Timeline for Completion**

> o The entire data extraction, computation, verification, and ranking exercise is expected to take about 3 months.
>
> o This period includes:
>
> ▪ Data cleaning and preprocessing
>
> ▪ Authentication and validation (with external agencies if needed)
>
> ▪ Metric computation and normalization
>
> ▪ Draft report review and quality assurance
>
> ▪ Final approval from NIRF governing committee

· **Publication Date**

> o The final rankings are released annually on the first Monday of April.
>
> o For India Rankings 2024, this corresponds to 1st April 2024.
>
> o Rankings are made available on the official NIRF website, covering Overall and Discipline-specific categories (Universities, Engineering, Management, Medical, Law, etc.).

· **Significance of Timeline**

> o The fixed publication date ensures predictability for institutions, students, and policymakers.
>
> o Aligns with admission cycles, allowing prospective students to use the rankings as a decision-making tool for choosing institutions.
>
> o Provides institutions with a benchmark window to review and improve their performance annually.

**Key takeaway:** NIRF's ranking process is a software-driven, metric-based exercise completed in about 3 months, with the final rankings published annually on the first Monday of April, ensuring transparency, reliability, and timeliness.

**6. Errors and Correction Policy**

**NIRF guideline on data display, cross-checking, and correction policy**

· **Raw Data Display on NIRF Website**

> o After institutions submit their data and NIRF completes its initial processing and validation, the raw processed data is displayed on the official NIRF portal.
>
> o This transparency step allows institutions to verify the exact dataset that will be used for final score computation and ranking.

· **Institutional Responsibility**

> o Institutions must carefully cross-check the displayed data against their own submissions.
>
> o If any discrepancies are noticed (e.g., missing records, incorrect third-party data, errors in extraction), it is the institution's duty to highlight them.
>
> o Responsibility for accuracy rests with the institution, not with NIRF.

· **Inclusion of Third-Party Data**

> o Apart from self-reported data, NIRF also uses third-party data sources (e.g., Scopus, WoS, Patent databases, government records).
>
> o Institutions are invited to review this extracted third-party data to confirm that it matches their actual output.

· **Feedback & Correction Window**

> o NIRF specifies a limited time period (correction window) during which institutions can submit comments, corrections, or objections.
>
> o Feedback must be objective, evidence-based, and timely.
>
> o After the deadline expires, no further corrections will be entertained.

· **Finality of Rankings**

> o Once rankings are published, they are final and binding.
>
> o No petitions, complaints, or appeals for corrections will be accepted after:

1\. The declared correction window closes, or

2\. The rankings are officially announced.

· **Implications for Institutions**

> o Institutions must adopt a proactive review mechanism when NIRF data is displayed.
>
> o Delays or negligence in cross-checking may result in errors being permanently reflected in the rankings.
>
> o Institutions should assign a dedicated NIRF Data Committee to verify the displayed data promptly within the given time frame.

· **Key Takeaway**

> o This rule enforces accountability on institutions to ensure the integrity of their own data, while NIRF ensures transparency by publicly displaying it before final scoring.
>
> o The system prevents last-minute disputes and ensures that rankings are published on time with reliable data.

**NIRF Data Monitoring Checklist**

| **Stage**                              | **Institution's Task**                                                                                 | **Responsible Team**                              | **Deadline**                          |
|----------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------|
| 1\. Data Submission                    | Upload institutional data on NIRF portal in prescribed format                                          | NIRF Nodal Officer + Institutional Data Committee | Before official submission deadline   |
| 2\. Data Hosting on Website            | Publish submitted data on institution's website with “NIRF” homepage link                              | IT/Web Team + Registrar Office                    | Immediately after submission          |
| 3\. Data Display by NIRF               | Download and review raw data displayed on NIRF website                                                 | NIRF Core Committee + Departmental Coordinators   | Within 24–48 hours of display         |
| 4\. Verification of Self-Reported Data | Match displayed data with internal submissions to ensure accuracy                                      | Institutional Data Committee                      | During official verification window   |
| 5\. Verification of Third-Party Data   | Cross-check publications, patents, placements, etc. with external sources (Scopus, WoS, Patent Office) | Research Cell + Placement Cell                    | During official verification window   |
| 6\. Feedback Submission to NIRF        | Report discrepancies with evidence via official feedback channel                                       | NIRF Nodal Officer + Registrar Office             | Before correction window closes       |
| 7\. Confirmation                       | Provide “no objection” if data matches accurately                                                      | Head of Institution / NIRF Coordinator            | Before end of correction window       |
| 8\. Post-Ranking Compliance            | Archive verified data and retain for 3 years as per NIRF rule                                          | Institutional Data Committee + Records Office     | Immediately after ranking publication |

**How to use this table:**

· Assign a dedicated team for each stage (Data Committee, Research Cell, Placement Office, Registrar).

· Track deadlines closely—missing the correction window means errors cannot be corrected.

· Maintain evidence files and audit trails to support any corrections submitted.

**Role of the Nodal Officer during NIRF data verification**

· **Role of the Nodal Officer**

> o Every participating institution must appoint a NIRF Nodal Officer as the single point of contact with NIRF.
>
> o The Nodal Officer is responsible for coordinating data submission, verification, corrections, and communication with NIRF.

· **Responsibility During Data Verification**

> o When NIRF displays the processed data for cross-checking, the Nodal Officer must:
>
> ▪ Carefully review all updated data for accuracy.
>
> ▪ Cross-verify against institutional submissions and supporting documents.
>
> ▪ Validate corrections, if any, during the official correction window.

· **Action in Case of Errors**

> o If the Nodal Officer finds that incorrect data has been updated (either by mistake or due to system/database mismatch), they must:
>
> ▪ Immediately notify NIRF via email before the rankings are officially announced.
>
> ▪ Provide evidence-based justification for the correction request.
>
> o This ensures that final scores are based on accurate data.

· **No Complaints After Rankings**

> o Once the rankings are officially released, no further complaints, appeals, or correction requests will be accepted.
>
> o Institutions cannot challenge rankings on grounds of incorrect data if they fail to raise the issue within the verification window.

· **Implications for Institutions**

> o The Nodal Officer's vigilance directly affects the institution's ranking outcome.
>
> o Any negligence or delay may result in incorrect data being locked in, impacting the institution's national ranking.
>
> o Institutions must empower the Nodal Officer with access to all data and supporting documents for effective review.

· **Best Practices for Nodal Officers**

> o Maintain a checklist of all submitted data and documents.
>
> o Conduct an internal review meeting with research cell, placement office, registrar, and finance before confirming data.
>
> o Keep a log of communications with NIRF for accountability.
>
> o Use the correction window proactively rather than waiting until the deadline.

**NIRF Nodal Officer Responsibility Checklist**

| **Task**                                                                  | **Timeline**                        | **Evidence Required**                                       | **Status**          |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------|
| Register institution on NIRF portal                                       | Before data submission deadline     | Registration confirmation from NIRF portal                  | Pending / Completed |
| Coordinate collection of data from all departments (TLR, RP, GO, OI, PR)  | During preparation phase            | Departmental submissions, signed verification notes         | Pending / Completed |
| Ensure data is uploaded in the prescribed online format                   | Before submission deadline          | Screenshot of portal submission, acknowledgment receipt     | Pending / Completed |
| Publish submitted data on institution website (NIRF homepage link)        | Immediately after portal submission | Active webpage link, uploaded files                         | Pending / Completed |
| Review processed data displayed by NIRF                                   | Within 24–48 hours of display       | Cross-check sheet (institutional vs. NIRF data)             | Pending / Completed |
| Verify third-party data (publications, patents, placements, funding)      | During correction window            | Scopus/WoS reports, patent numbers, placement records       | Pending / Completed |
| Submit corrections/comments if discrepancies found                        | Within official correction window   | Official email to NIRF with evidence attachments            | Pending / Completed |
| Confirm “No Objection” if data is accurate                                | Before correction window closes     | Confirmation email/file from institution head/Nodal officer | Pending / Completed |
| Archive all data submitted and verified for record-keeping                | After correction phase              | Institutional repository of submitted files                 | Pending / Completed |
| Monitor final ranking publication and prepare institutional communication | First Monday of April (release day) | Official NIRF ranking list                                  | Pending / Completed |

**How to use this checklist:**

· Add it to an Excel tracker so the Nodal Officer can tick off each stage.

· Use the Status column to monitor progress (Pending/Completed/In Review).

· Helps ensure no deadlines are missed and all evidence is well-documented.

**NIRF Overall Ranking Parameters & Weightages (2024)**
