| **Sr. No.** | **Parameter**                          | **Max Marks** | **Weightage** | **Contribution to Overall Score (Out of 100)** |
|-------------|----------------------------------------|---------------|---------------|------------------------------------------------|
| 1           | Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)   | 100           | 0.30          | 30                                             |
| 2           | Research & Professional Practices (RP) | 100           | 0.30          | 30                                             |
| 3           | Graduation Outcomes (GO)               | 100           | 0.20          | 20                                             |
| 4           | Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)            | 100           | 0.10          | 10                                             |
| 5           | Perception (PR)                        | 100           | 0.10          | 10                                             |
| **Total**   | **–**                                  | **500**       | **–**         | **100**                                        |

**Key Notes:**

· Each parameter is scored on 100 marks internally, but weighted differently.

· Final institutional score = Weighted sum of all five parameters (max 100).

· TLR and RP together account for 60%, making them the most critical factors.

· GO, OI, and PR collectively contribute 40%, balancing outcomes, inclusivity, and reputation.

**7. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)**

· Overall Weightage: 30% in NIRF Rankings

· Total Marks: 100 (internally), scaled to contribute 30 marks to the final score

**A. Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS) – 20 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Total student enrollment at UG, PG, and Ph.D. levels.
>
> o Proportion of doctoral students, as they contribute to research ecosystem.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Reflects institutional capacity and diversity of academic programs.
>
> o Higher doctoral enrollments strengthen the research culture.

**B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) with emphasis on permanent faculty – 25 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Ratio of full-time permanent faculty to students.
>
> o Greater emphasis is placed on permanent faculty over temporary/contractual faculty.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Lower FSR indicates better academic attention and mentoring.
>
> o Permanent faculty ensures stability, continuity, and quality in teaching-learning.

**C. Faculty with PhD (or equivalent) and Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Percentage of faculty with doctoral degrees or equivalent qualifications.
>
> o Average teaching/research experience of faculty.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Qualified and experienced faculty contribute to strong academic foundations.
>
> o Ensures high-quality supervision, mentoring, and knowledge dissemination.

**D. Financial Resources and their Utilisation (FRU) – 20 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Total annual budget and expenditure on academic and research infrastructure.
>
> o Effective utilization of financial resources for faculty salaries, labs, libraries, ICT, and student support.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Well-funded and properly utilized resources create a strong learning environment.
>
> o Prevents underfunding issues that directly affect student and faculty performance.

**E. Online Education (OE): Completion of Syllabus, Exams & SWAYAM – 10 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Adoption of online teaching and learning methods.
>
> o Completion of syllabus and exams through digital modes.
>
> o Participation in national digital initiatives like SWAYAM MOOCs.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Reflects digital readiness of institutions.
>
> o Ensures continuity of education during disruptions (e.g., pandemic).
>
> o Encourages integration of modern e-learning tools.

**F. Multiple Entry/Exit, Indian Knowledge System & Regional Languages (MIR) – 5 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Implementation of NEP 2020 reforms like multiple entry/exit options.
>
> o Incorporation of Indian Knowledge Systems (IKS) in curricula.
>
> o Promotion of regional languages in higher education.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Aligns institutions with national education policy priorities.
>
> o Encourages inclusivity, flexibility, and preservation of cultural knowledge.
>
> o Strengthens accessibility for students from diverse backgrounds.

**Overall Significance of TLR**

· TLR collectively assesses the foundation of teaching and learning quality in an institution.

· It goes beyond counting students and teachers—it emphasizes:

> ▪ Quality of faculty (PhDs, experience)
>
> ▪ Availability of resources (funding, labs, ICT, libraries)
>
> ▪ Digital education practices (SWAYAM, online exams)
>
> ▪ Policy adoption (NEP reforms, IKS, regional language integration)

· As the heaviest-weighted parameter (30%), strong TLR performance directly influences institutional rank.

**NIRF 2024 – Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) Breakdown**

| **S. No.** | **Sub-Parameter**                                                                              | **Marks** | **Remarks**                                                                                         |
|------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 1A         | Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS)                                              | 20        | Captures total student strength and balance between UG/PG/Ph.D. students                            |
| 1B         | Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) with emphasis on permanent faculty                                 | 25        | Measures adequacy of faculty, quality of teaching environment                                       |
| 1C         | Combined Metric for Faculty with PhD (or equivalent) and Experience (FQE)                      | 20        | Rewards institutions with higher proportion of qualified and experienced faculty                    |
| 1D         | Financial Resources and their Utilisation (FRU)                                                | 20        | Evaluates budgetary support, spending on academics, infrastructure, and research                    |
| 1E         | Online Education (OE): Completion of syllabus & exams, SWAYAM                                  | 10        | Accounts for adoption of online teaching-learning, MOOCs, digital assessments                       |
| 1F         | Combined Metric for Multiple Entry/Exit, Indian Knowledge System, and Regional Languages (MIR) | 5         | Encourages NEP 2020 reforms, inclusion of Indian Knowledge Systems, and regional language promotion |
| **Total**  | **–**                                                                                          | **100**   | **Weighted overall contribution = 30% of NIRF Score**                                               |

**8. Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS)**

**Formula**

· SS = f(NT, NE) × 15 + f(NP) × 5

> o f(NT, NE): Function of approved intake (NT) and actual enrolled students (NE).
>
> o f(NP): Function of doctoral (Ph.D.) enrollment.

**Components**

1\. **NT – Total Sanctioned Approved Intake**

> o Sum of all approved seats in UG + PG programs.
>
> o Approved by regulatory agencies (AICTE, MCI, UGC, etc.).

2\. **NE – Total Number of Enrolled Students**

> o Actual students admitted against NT.
>
> o Reflects utilization efficiency of intake capacity.
>
> o Institutions with higher enrollment-to-intake ratios score better.

3\. **NP – Number of Ph.D. Students Enrolled**

> o Captures strength of doctoral research programs.
>
> o Weight = 5 Marks, highlighting research culture.

**Data Source**

· Primary Data → To be provided in prescribed NIRF format.

· Verified through:

> o Admission registers, enrollment lists.
>
> o Regulatory body approvals.
>
> o Student records audited by IQAC/NBA/NAAC.

**Scoring Logic**

· **15 Marks for NT & NE**

> o Higher marks if institution maintains high enrollment relative to sanctioned intake.
>
> o Penalized if actual enrollment is significantly below capacity.

· **5 Marks for NP (Ph.D. Students)**

> o More Ph.D. students = better research environment.
>
> o Institutions with strong doctoral programs approach full 5 marks.

· Maximum = 20 Marks

**Why It Matters**

· Reflects institutional scale (student capacity utilization).

· Rewards research-driven institutions (Ph.D. enrollment).

· Balances quantity (UG/PG strength) with quality (doctoral research).

**Primary Data (Institution-Submitted)**

· Institutions must provide data in the prescribed NIRF format.

· Covers details like:

> ▪ Program-wise intake capacity (UG, PG, Ph.D.).
>
> ▪ Actual enrollment (domestic + international).
>
> ▪ Year-wise student strength.
>
> ▪ Category-wise data (Gender, State-wise, Country-wise, etc.).

**Verification Sources**

· **Admission Registers**

> o Maintained by the admission cell or registrar.
>
> o Contains official details of admitted students (name, roll number, category, domicile).
>
> o Used to confirm reported intake vs. actual enrollments.

· **Enrollment Lists**

> o Department-wise or program-wise enrollment data.
>
> o Includes active student roll numbers and enrollment IDs.
>
> o Helps NIRF validate real-time student strength.

· **Regulatory Body Approvals**

> o AICTE/UGC/PCI/INC/COA/NMC/MCI approvals for sanctioned student intake.
>
> o Ensures institutions are not over-reporting intake or admitting beyond approved limits.
>
> o Cross-verified with annual approval letters.

· **Student Records Audited by IQAC/NBA/NAAC**

> o Internal audits by IQAC ensure accuracy of enrollment data.
>
> o NBA/NAAC accreditation audits also verify student enrollment and progression.
>
> o Adds third-party credibility to institutional submissions.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Student enrollment is foundational for:

> o Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR).
>
> o Graduation Outcomes (GO).
>
> o Outreach & Inclusivity (OI).

· Ensures institutions do not inflate intake/enrollment numbers.

· Cross-verification guarantees transparency and authenticity.

**9. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR)**

**Formula**

· FSR = 25 × \[15 × (F / N)\]

> o F = Number of full-time regular faculty (counting only permanent or full-time contract faculty who taught both semesters in the previous academic year).
>
> o N = NT + NP
>
> ▪ NT = Total sanctioned approved intake (UG + PG).
>
> ▪ NP = Number of doctoral students enrolled.

· **Benchmark:**

> o Expected faculty–student ratio = 1:15.
>
> o If F/N ≥ 1:15 → full 25 marks.
>
> o If F/N \< 1:50 → score set to zero.

**What It Captures**

· **Faculty Adequacy:**

> o Ensures there are enough faculty members to maintain quality teaching and supervision.

· **Faculty Permanency:**

> o Prioritizes permanent and full-time faculty for scoring.
>
> o Temporary/ad-hoc faculty only counted if they taught across both semesters.

· **Research-Oriented Disciplines:**

> o In PG & Ph.D. programs, faculty availability strongly impacts research guidance and output.

**Data Requirements**

· **Primary Data:**

> o Faculty list with details (name, qualification, appointment type, permanent/contract, teaching load).

· **Eligibility Criteria:**

> o Only Ph.D. or Master's qualified faculty are counted.
>
> o Faculty with only Bachelor's degrees are excluded.

**Scoring Logic**

· F/N ≥ 1:15 → Full 25 Marks.

· F/N lower than 1:15 → proportionally reduced score.

· F/N below 1:50 → Score = 0.

**Example:**

· NT = 1200 (UG + PG intake)

· NP = 200 (Doctoral students)

· N = 1400

· F = 95 full-time permanent faculty

· Ratio = 95/1400 = 1:14.7 ≈ meets benchmark → Full 25 Marks

**Why It Matters**

· Teaching Quality: Good FSR ensures smaller class sizes, better student–faculty engagement.

· Research Supervision: Critical for doctoral programs where faculty guide Ph.D. scholars.

· Accreditation Alignment: FSR is a key metric in NBA, NAAC, and Washington Accord quality standards.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Faculty List with Details**

Institution must submit a comprehensive faculty database including:

> ▪ Name of Faculty
>
> ▪ Highest Qualification (Ph.D. / Master's / Bachelor's)
>
> ▪ Appointment Type (Permanent, Contract, Visiting/Adjunct)
>
> ▪ Employment Status (Full-time, Part-time)
>
> ▪ Teaching Load (hours per week or number of courses taught per semester)
>
> ▪ Department/Discipline
>
> ▪ Date of Appointment / Service Record

· **Purpose**

> o Used to compute:
>
> ▪ Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – only full-time permanent faculty considered.
>
> ▪ Faculty Quality & Experience (FQE) – only Ph.D.-qualified faculty considered.
>
> ▪ Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – diversity among faculty.

**Eligibility Criteria for Counting in NIRF**

· **Included Faculty**

> o Faculty with Ph.D. or equivalent degree.
>
> o Faculty with Master's degree (M.Tech, MBA, M.Sc., etc.) if not Ph.D.-qualified.
>
> o Counted towards FSR metric.
>
> o Only Ph.D. faculty are counted for FQE metric.

· **Excluded Faculty**

> o Faculty with only Bachelor's degree.
>
> o Adjunct, visiting, and part-time faculty (not full-time regular).
>
> o Non-teaching staff, research fellows, and teaching assistants.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Faculty profile forms a major part of Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR).

· Ensures institutions are not inflating numbers by adding non-qualified or temporary staff.

· Rewards institutions with higher proportions of Ph.D.-qualified, permanent, experienced faculty.

**10. Faculty with PhD (or equivalent) and Experience (FQE)**

**Formula**

· FQE = FQ + FE

> o **FQ (PhD-qualified Faculty Score)**
>
> ▪ If FRA ≥ 95% → FQ = 10
>
> ▪ Else → FQ = 10 × (FRA / 95)
>
> ▪ FRA = % of Faculty with PhD (or equivalent) relative to faculty required or available (whichever is higher).
>
> o **FE (Faculty Experience Score)**
>
> ▪ F1 = Fraction of faculty with experience up to 8 years.
>
> ▪ F2 = Fraction with experience between 8+ and 15 years.
>
> ▪ F3 = Fraction with experience \> 15 years.
>
> ▪ Formula: FE = 3 × min(3F1, 1) + 3 × min(3F2, 1) + 4 × min(3F3, 1)
>
> ▪ Rationale: Full marks when faculty experience is distributed in a balanced 1:1:1 ratio across three bands.

**What It Captures**

· **Research Depth (PhDs):**

> o Rewards institutions with higher proportions of PhD-qualified faculty.
>
> o PhD-qualified faculty bring advanced research capability, supervision strength, and international exposure.

· **Experience Diversity:**

> o Recognizes institutions with a balanced mix of young, mid-career, and senior faculty.
>
> o Encourages mentoring pipelines and sustainable teaching–research balance.

**Scoring Logic**

1\. **PhD-Qualified Faculty (FQ):**

> o Example:
>
> ▪ Total faculty required = 100
>
> ▪ PhD-qualified = 90 → FRA = 90%
>
> ▪ FQ = (90/95) × 10 ≈ 9.47 Marks

2\. **Experience Mix (FE):**

> o Example:
>
> ▪ F1 (≤ 8 yrs) = 0.33, F2 (8–15 yrs) = 0.34, F3 (\>15 yrs) = 0.33
>
> ▪ FE = 3×min(3×0.33,1) + 3×min(3×0.34,1) + 4×min(3×0.33,1) = 3+3+4 = 10 Marks

3\. **Final FQE = FQ + FE → Max 20 Marks**

**Data Requirements**

· Faculty List with Qualifications & Experience (in years).

· Categories: PhD/Equivalent, Master's, Bachelor's (only PhD/Master's count for FSR; PhD used here).

· Faculty Experience grouped as:

> o F1: ≤ 8 years
>
> o F2: 8–15 years
>
> o F3: \> 15 years

**Why It Matters**

· Ensures teaching quality (experienced faculty) + research excellence (PhDs).

· Encourages institutions to hire and retain PhD-qualified faculty.

· Balances academic innovation from young faculty with stability from senior faculty.

**Faculty List with Qualifications & Experience (in years)**

· **What it means:**

> o Institution must provide a complete list of faculty members working on a regular/full-time basis.
>
> o Details required:
>
> ▪ Faculty Name
>
> ▪ Designation (Assistant/Associate/Professor)
>
> ▪ Department
>
> ▪ Highest Qualification (PhD / Master's / Bachelor's)
>
> ▪ Years of Teaching/Research/Industry Experience
>
> ▪ Date of Joining Institution
>
> o Visiting/Adjunct faculty are not considered for this metric.

· **Purpose:**

> o To evaluate Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) and Faculty Quality & Experience (FQE) metrics in NIRF.
>
> o Higher number of qualified and experienced faculty positively impacts rankings.

**Categories: PhD / Equivalent, Master's, Bachelor's**

· **PhD / Equivalent:**

> o Faculty with Doctoral Degree (PhD, DSc, or equivalent).
>
> o Considered the highest weightage category in NIRF scoring.

· **Master's Degree:**

> o Faculty holding only a Master's degree (M.Tech, M.E., M.Sc., MBA, etc.).
>
> o Counted for FSR, but not weighted as strongly as PhD for FQE.

· **Bachelor's Degree:**

> o Faculty with only Bachelor's degree are not counted in FSR or FQE.
>
> o Included in the list for completeness, but excluded in scoring.

· **Important:**

> o For FSR calculation: Only PhD + Master's faculty count.
>
> o For Faculty Quality (FQE): Only PhD-qualified faculty are considered.

**Faculty Experience Grouping**

· Faculty are classified into 3 experience bands:

> o **F1: ≤ 8 years**
>
> ▪ Early-career faculty with limited experience.
>
> ▪ Usually Assistant Professors or new recruits.
>
> ▪ Important for showing young talent but scores lower in experience weightage.
>
> o **F2: 8–15 years**
>
> ▪ Mid-career faculty with moderate experience.
>
> ▪ Often Associate Professors or senior Assistant Professors.
>
> ▪ Provides balance between teaching and research output.
>
> o **F3: \> 15 years**
>
> ▪ Highly experienced faculty (often Professors, senior academic leaders).
>
> ▪ Contributes significantly to research, mentoring PhDs, and institutional leadership.
>
> ▪ Highest weightage in NIRF's Faculty Experience (FQE) score.

· **Purpose of grouping:**

> o To measure the distribution of faculty across different career stages.
>
> o Balanced distribution (not too many in F1, healthy representation in F2 & F3) indicates institutional maturity and stability.

**Why NIRF Collects This Data**

· Helps evaluate faculty adequacy and quality (critical for Teaching, Learning & Resources – TLR).

· Ensures institutions don't inflate numbers with inexperienced/adjunct staff.

· Rewards institutions with higher PhD-qualified and experienced faculty.

· Directly impacts FSR (30 marks) and FQE (40 marks) sub-metrics in NIRF scoring.

**11. Financial Resources and their Utilisation (FRU)**

**Formula**

· FRU = 5 × f(BC) + 15 × f(BO)

> o **BC = Average Annual Capital Expenditure per Student (3 years)**
>
> ▪ Excluding costs for construction of new buildings.
>
> ▪ Covers labs, equipment, IT resources, learning resources.
>
> o **BO = Average Annual Operational (Recurring) Expenditure per Student (3 years)**
>
> ▪ Excluding maintenance of hostels and allied services.
>
> ▪ Covers faculty salaries, library, consumables, academic services.

**Components**

1\. **Capital Expenditure (BC, 5 Marks)**

> o Investments in academic infrastructure:
>
> ▪ Laboratories, workshops, ICT facilities, digital classrooms, libraries.
>
> o Shows whether the institution upgrades resources regularly.
>
> o Per-student normalization ensures fairness across institutions of different sizes.

2\. **Operational Expenditure (BO, 15 Marks)**

> o Running costs directly contributing to academic delivery.
>
> o Includes:
>
> ▪ Faculty and staff salaries.
>
> ▪ Consumables (lab supplies, teaching aids).
>
> ▪ Library subscriptions, e-resources.
>
> ▪ Student support services.
>
> o Excludes: hostels, transport, non-academic amenities.
>
> o Higher share ensures sustained teaching–learning quality.

**Data Source**

· Primary Data (institution-submitted):

> o Audited accounts for past 3 years.
>
> o Student strength each year.

· Verification:

> o Cross-check with finance department records, audited reports.

**Scoring Logic**

· **Capital Expenditure (Max 5 Marks):**

> o Full marks if average annual capex per student is above benchmark (say, ≥ ₹50,000).
>
> o Scaled down proportionally if lower.

· **Operational Expenditure (Max 15 Marks):**

> o Full marks if per-student recurring expenditure is above benchmark (say, ≥ ₹1,50,000).
>
> o Proportional scaling for lower spends.

· Total FRU = Max 20 Marks.

**Why It Matters**

· Ensures that institutions:

> o Invest continuously in upgrading infrastructure.
>
> o Sustain faculty and academic services with adequate funding.
>
> o Demonstrate financial transparency and accountability.

· Balances short-term operational stability with long-term infrastructure growth.

**Primary Data (Institution-Submitted)**

· **Audited Accounts for Past 3 Years**

> o Institution must provide audited financial statements for the last three financial years.
>
> o Key components:
>
> ▪ Income & Expenditure statements.
>
> ▪ Balance sheets.
>
> ▪ Audit certificates from Chartered Accountant / Finance Controller.
>
> o Purpose:
>
> ▪ Ensures transparency in institutional income, funding, and expenditure.
>
> ▪ Forms the basis for NIRF's financial resource normalization.

· **Student Strength Each Year**

> o Institution must submit year-wise student enrollment numbers (UG, PG, Ph.D.).
>
> o Both intake capacity and actual enrollment should be reported.
>
> o Includes details of:
>
> ▪ Domestic students (state-wise).
>
> ▪ International students (country-wise).
>
> o Purpose:
>
> ▪ Used in Faculty-Student Ratio (FSR), Graduation Outcomes (GO), and normalization of research/financial parameters.

**Verification**

· **Cross-check with Finance Department Records**

> o Finance department's official ledgers and records must match the audited accounts submitted.
>
> o Prevents over-reporting of income or funding sources.

· **Audited Reports**

> o Must be certified by:
>
> ▪ Statutory auditor.
>
> ▪ Finance controller.
>
> ▪ Governing body/Board of Management.
>
> o Ensures authenticity of financial reporting.
>
> o Also used to validate income from:
>
> ▪ Sponsored projects.
>
> ▪ Consultancy.
>
> ▪ EDP/MDP training programs.
>
> ▪ Tuition and other institutional receipts.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· To ensure financial transparency, accountability, and resource adequacy.

· Provides a reliable denominator for normalizing research income and resource utilization.

· Cross-linked with student strength data to evaluate whether resources are used effectively per student.

**12. Online Education (OE): Completion of Syllabus, Exams & SWAYAM**

**Formula**

· OE = f(OE) (function defined by NIRF based on adoption of online teaching-learning mechanisms).

· Total Marks = 10.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Completion of Syllabus via Online Mode**

> o Extent to which courses are delivered digitally.
>
> o Includes synchronous (live online lectures) and asynchronous (LMS, MOOCs, recorded videos) teaching.
>
> o Institutions that integrate blended learning systematically score higher.

2\. **Online Examinations & Assessments**

> o Conduct of mid-term, end-term, quizzes, and viva using proctored online tools.
>
> o Adoption of AI-proctored, secure platforms gets better scores.
>
> o Reflects digital readiness for crisis situations (like COVID-19).

3\. **SWAYAM Courses (MOOCs)**

> o Recognition of:
>
> ▪ Number of students enrolled in SWAYAM/NPTEL.
>
> ▪ Completion rates of these MOOCs.
>
> ▪ Faculty contribution to developing SWAYAM courses.
>
> o Encourages Indian HEIs to contribute to the national MOOC ecosystem.

**Scoring Logic (Indicative)**

· **Syllabus Completion Online (4 Marks):**

> o 100% syllabus completion via LMS/e-tools → Full 4 marks.
>
> o Partial adoption → proportionally lower.

· **Online Exams (3 Marks):**

> o All exams conducted online (secured & proctored) → Full 3 marks.
>
> o Hybrid mode → partial marks.

· **SWAYAM Courses (3 Marks):**

> o Full student participation + faculty-developed courses → Full 3 marks.
>
> o Only enrollment, no completions → lower marks.

· Maximum OE = 10 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (to be submitted by institution):

> o % of syllabus delivered online.
>
> o % of courses assessed via online exams.
>
> o Number of SWAYAM courses completed by students.
>
> o Number of SWAYAM courses developed by faculty.

· Verification:

> o LMS records, online exam records, SWAYAM portal certifications.

**Why It Matters**

· Promotes digital learning adoption in line with NEP 2020.

· Encourages student flexibility through MOOCs and blended learning.

· Ensures institutions are resilient and adaptable to future disruptions.

· Strengthens India's digital education infrastructure.

**% of syllabus delivered online**

· Refers to the proportion of the total curriculum (UG + PG) that has been delivered using online/digital modes.

· Delivery modes may include:

> ▪ Live online lectures via platforms (Zoom, MS Teams, Google Meet, etc.).
>
> ▪ Asynchronous recorded sessions hosted on LMS (Moodle, Blackboard, Canvas).
>
> ▪ Digital course content through MOOCs, SWAYAM, Coursera, edX.
>
> ▪ Virtual labs, simulation-based teaching, and online project guidance.

· Purpose:

> ▪ To assess the institution's adoption of digital teaching-learning practices.
>
> ▪ To measure alignment with NEP 2020's emphasis on blended learning and technology integration.

· Data requirement:

> ▪ Institution calculates percentage = (Total syllabus delivered online ÷ Total syllabus delivered overall) × 100.
>
> ▪ Verified through LMS logs, timetable records, and faculty reports.

**% of courses assessed via online exams**

· Represents the share of academic courses where examinations/assessments were conducted fully or partly online.

· Assessment modes may include:

> ▪ Online proctored examinations.
>
> ▪ Online quizzes, multiple-choice tests, and assignments on LMS.
>
> ▪ Remote viva, project submissions, and presentations through digital platforms.

· Purpose:

> ▪ To gauge the institution's capability to adopt technology-enabled evaluation systems.
>
> ▪ To ensure transparency, accessibility, and efficiency in assessments.

· Data requirement:

> ▪ Percentage = (Number of courses assessed online ÷ Total courses offered) × 100.
>
> ▪ Evidence from examination cell reports, LMS data, or online proctoring vendor reports.

**Number of SWAYAM courses completed by students**

· Captures how many SWAYAM/NPTEL/other UGC-AICTE-approved MOOC courses have been successfully completed by students.

· Completion = Students must earn certificates from SWAYAM/NPTEL after passing the final proctored exam.

· Purpose:

> ▪ To measure student exposure to national-level MOOCs and self-learning platforms.
>
> ▪ To reflect institutional encouragement for lifelong learning and skill enhancement.

· Data requirement:

> ▪ Count of unique SWAYAM certificates completed by enrolled students during the assessment period.
>
> ▪ Verified through certificate copies or official SWAYAM dashboard records.

**Number of SWAYAM courses developed by faculty**

· Refers to the number of complete courses created and delivered by the institution's faculty members on the SWAYAM platform.

· Faculty must have developed full MOOCs (with AICTE/UGC approval) that are published/hosted on SWAYAM.

· Purpose:

> ▪ To evaluate faculty contribution to the national digital education ecosystem.
>
> ▪ To highlight institutional participation in knowledge dissemination at the national/global level.

· Data requirement:

> ▪ List of courses with course titles, faculty names, hosting semester/year.
>
> ▪ Links to SWAYAM portal course pages and official approval letters.

**Overall Relevance for NIRF**

· These parameters fall under the Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) dimension.

· They showcase digital adoption, blended learning readiness, and contribution to national-level initiatives like SWAYAM.

· Evidence submitted should be authentic, with certificates, LMS reports, or SWAYAM links to avoid data rejection during NIRF verification.

**Learning Management System (LMS) Records**

· What it is:

> ▪ LMS is the digital backbone for online teaching and learning (e.g., Moodle, Blackboard, Canvas, Google Classroom, institution's in-house LMS).
>
> ▪ It records teaching activities, uploaded course content, attendance, assignments, and interaction logs.

· Role in Verification:

> ▪ Provides evidence for % of syllabus delivered online.
>
> ▪ Tracks the number of lectures delivered via online mode, modules uploaded, and student engagement.
>
> ▪ Generates audit trails/logs (date, time, faculty, duration).

· Examples of data extracted:

> ▪ Reports of uploaded lectures per subject.
>
> ▪ Student activity (views, downloads, submissions).
>
> ▪ Faculty dashboard showing completion status of online modules.

**Online Exam Records**

· What it is:

> ▪ Documentation of examinations or assessments conducted via digital platforms.
>
> ▪ Can include institution's in-house exam portal, third-party proctoring services (Mettl, Examity), or LMS-integrated test modules.

· Role in Verification:

> ▪ Validates % of courses assessed via online exams.
>
> ▪ Confirms the scale, number of courses, and students evaluated digitally.
>
> ▪ Provides authenticity through invigilation reports, student login data, and result sheets.

· Examples of data extracted:

> ▪ Online exam schedules and attendance logs.
>
> ▪ Proctoring reports (screenshots, flags, candidate verification).
>
> ▪ Consolidated course-wise exam reports from Examination Cell.

**SWAYAM Portal Certifications**

· What it is:

> ▪ Official certification issued by SWAYAM/NPTEL portal when students or faculty successfully complete a course.
>
> ▪ Certificates are digitally signed and downloadable from the SWAYAM portal.

· Role in Verification:

> ▪ Confirms number of SWAYAM courses completed by students.
>
> ▪ Validates SWAYAM courses developed by faculty (through course links, approval letters, and course pages hosted on SWAYAM).

· Examples of data extracted:

> ▪ Student certificate repository with names, course titles, completion dates.
>
> ▪ Faculty-developed course links with official hosting semester/year.
>
> ▪ Consolidated report from SWAYAM portal (institution dashboard).

**Why Verification is Important in NIRF**

· Ensures transparency and credibility of submitted data.

· Prevents data inflation or unverifiable claims by institutions.

· Acts as primary evidence during random audits or fact-checking by NIRF committees.

**13. Multiple Entry/Exit, Indian Knowledge System (IKS) & Regional Languages (MIR)**

**MIR Metric (5 Marks)**

**Formula**

· MIR = (2 × MEE) + (2 × IKS) + (1 × RL)

> o MEE = Multiple Entry/Exit (upto 2 Marks)
>
> o IKS = Indian Knowledge System (upto 2 Marks)
>
> o RL = Regional Languages (upto 1 Mark)

**Components**

1\. **Multiple Entry/Exit (MEE) – 2 Marks**

> o Based on implementation of NEP 2020's Academic Bank of Credits (ABC).
>
> o Allows learners to:
>
> ▪ Exit after 1st/2nd/3rd year with certification/diploma/degree.
>
> ▪ Re-enter later with credit transfers.
>
> o Institutions with full adoption (credit bank integration, flexible curriculum) → Full 2 Marks.

2\. **Indian Knowledge System (IKS) – 2 Marks**

> o Encourages inclusion of courses that promote:
>
> ▪ Indian culture, heritage, philosophy, arts, languages, yoga, traditional medicine, environmental knowledge systems, ethics.
>
> o Institutions offering dedicated IKS courses in UG/PG curriculum → Full 2 Marks.
>
> o If partially included (electives, foundation courses only) → Partial marks.

3\. **Regional Languages (RL) – 1 Mark**

> o Adoption of regional language instruction as per NEP 2020.
>
> o Courses offered fully/partially in mother tongue/local languages.
>
> o Integration of bilingual textbooks, digital resources, or translation.
>
> o Full implementation → 1 Mark; Partial → proportionally less.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data:

> o Academic regulations showing MEE adoption.
>
> o List of IKS courses with syllabi.
>
> o Details of programs offered in regional languages.

· Verification:

> o Curriculum documents, program regulations, timetables.
>
> o Course completion data, faculty allocations.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution has:

> o MEE fully implemented (2 Marks).
>
> o 3 IKS foundation courses introduced (2 Marks).
>
> o Some courses taught in Hindi & Tamil (1 Mark).

· MIR = 2 + 2 + 1 = 5 / 5 Marks

**Why It Matters**

· Aligns with NEP 2020 → flexibility, holistic learning, cultural grounding.

· Strengthens national identity + global outlook.

· Enhances accessibility for students from rural and regional backgrounds.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Academic Regulations showing MEE adoption**

> o MEE = Multiple Entry and Exit system, as per NEP 2020.
>
> o Institution must provide academic regulations that:
>
> ▪ Allow students to exit after 1/2/3 years with certificate/diploma/degree.
>
> ▪ Define credit transfers, re-entry policies, and alignment with ABC (Academic Bank of Credits).
>
> o Documentation:
>
> ▪ Academic Council regulations.
>
> ▪ UG/PG program regulations mentioning entry-exit points.

· **List of IKS (Indian Knowledge System) Courses with Syllabi**

> o IKS courses related to India's heritage, traditional knowledge, and indigenous practices.
>
> o Examples:
>
> ▪ Yoga studies, Ayurveda, Sanskrit texts, Indian mathematics, classical music, Vedic sciences.
>
> o Institution must list all IKS courses offered, along with course syllabi.
>
> o Documentation:
>
> ▪ Course structure, module descriptions, credits assigned.

· **Details of Programs Offered in Regional Languages**

> o Institution must specify which programs are available in regional/local languages (as per NEP's emphasis on mother-tongue learning).
>
> o Examples:
>
> ▪ B.A. History in Tamil medium.
>
> ▪ B.Sc. Physics in Hindi medium.
>
> ▪ Engineering diploma in Marathi medium.
>
> o Documentation:
>
> ▪ Program handbook, timetables, student enrollment lists.

**Verification**

· **Curriculum Documents**

> o Official program curriculum, subject listings, and credit structures.
>
> o Confirms inclusion of MEE, IKS, and regional-language programs.

· **Program Regulations**

> o Academic Council-approved regulations specifying program structure and exit policies.
>
> o Validates MEE adoption.

· **Timetables**

> o Class timetables showing courses offered in IKS/regional languages.
>
> o Evidence of actual delivery (not just policy-level adoption).

· **Course Completion Data**

> o Reports showing number of students enrolled and completed IKS/regional-language courses.
>
> o Confirms real implementation.

· **Faculty Allocations**

> o Department-wise teaching allocations confirming which faculty handled IKS/regional-language courses.
>
> o Avoids claims of “offered but not delivered.”

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Aligned with NEP 2020 reforms → flexibility, inclusivity, and Indian Knowledge promotion.

· Ensures institutions are implementing MEE, promoting IKS, and teaching in regional languages.

· Adds weight under Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) and Outreach & Inclusivity (OI).

**14. Research and Professional Practice (RP)**

· Overall Weightage: 30% of NIRF Rankings

· Total Marks: 100 (internally), scaled to contribute 30 marks to the overall score.

· Objective: To evaluate the research ecosystem, innovation capacity, and professional engagement of an institution.

**A. Combined Metric for Publications (PU) – 30 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Total number of research publications indexed in Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, and other recognized databases.
>
> o Conference proceedings and journal papers are also considered.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Demonstrates research productivity of faculty and researchers.
>
> o Reflects institution's contribution to national and international knowledge.

· **Impact:**

> o Institutions with strong research output gain visibility in global academia.

**B. Combined Metric for Quality of Publications (QP) – 30 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Citations per paper, h-index, and field-weighted citation impact.
>
> o Journal quality based on metrics like SNIP, SJR, and Impact Factor.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Ensures focus is not just on the number of publications but on their scientific value and influence.
>
> o Rewards institutions that publish in high-quality, peer-reviewed journals.

· **Impact:**

> o Prevents inflation by prioritizing quality over quantity.

**C. IPR and Patents: Published and Granted (IPR) – 15 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Number of patents filed, published, granted, and commercialized by the institution.
>
> o Also considers intellectual property beyond patents (e.g., copyrights, designs).

· **Why it matters:**

> o Encourages a culture of innovation and technology transfer.
>
> o Measures real-world application of research in solving industry and societal problems.

· **Impact:**

> o Institutions with active patenting activity show stronger industry-academia collaboration.

**D. Footprint of Projects and Professional Practice (FPPP) – 15 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Sponsored research projects funded by government or industry.
>
> o Consultancy revenue generated from external engagements.
>
> o Professional development programs offered (executive education, training).

· **Why it matters:**

> o Indicates institutional strength in industry linkages, applied research, and consultancy.
>
> o Demonstrates ability to attract funding and professional trust.

· **Impact:**

> o A high FPPP score reflects strong practical engagement beyond academics.

**E. Combined Metric for Publications & Citations in SDGs (PSDGs) – 10 Marks**

· **What it measures:**

> o Research output aligned with the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).
>
> o Citations of such work in global literature.

· **Why it matters:**

> o Reflects institution's contribution to solving global challenges like health, climate, equality, and sustainable development.
>
> o Encourages socially impactful and globally relevant research.

· **Impact:**

> o Positions Indian institutions within the global sustainability agenda.

**Overall Significance of RP**

· RP contributes 30%, making it equally important as TLR.

· Strong RP performance indicates that an institution is not only teaching well but also creating new knowledge, innovating, and collaborating with industry.

· It also boosts institutional reputation both nationally and internationally.

**NIRF 2024 – Research and Professional Practice (RP) Breakdown**

| **S. No.** | **Sub-Parameter**                                            | **Marks** | **Remarks**                                                                                               |
|------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 2A         | Combined Metric for Publications (PU)                        | 30        | Measures volume of research output across Scopus/Web of Science indexed journals and conferences          |
| 2B         | Combined Metric for Quality of Publications (QP)             | 30        | Evaluates research quality through citations, impact factors, and field-weighted citation indices         |
| 2C         | IPR and Patents: Published and Granted (IPR)                 | 15        | Rewards institutions for patents filed, published, granted, and commercialized                            |
| 2D         | Footprint of Projects and Professional Practice (FPPP)       | 15        | Assesses revenue and outcomes from sponsored research, consultancy, and professional development programs |
| 2E         | Combined Metric for Publications & Citations in SDGs (PSDGs) | 10        | Captures contribution of research aligned to UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and global impact    |
| **Total**  | **–**                                                        | **100**   | **Weighted overall contribution = 30% of NIRF Score**                                                     |

**15. Combined Metric for Publications (PU)**

**Formula**

· PU = 30 × f(P / FRQ)

> o P = Weighted number of publications (as per Scopus, Web of Science, or other approved third-party sources).
>
> o FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max( Nominal Faculty (based on FSR 1:15), Actual Faculty Strength ).
>
> o f(P / FRQ) = Normalization function defined by NIRF to adjust for discipline-specific publication norms.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Research Productivity per Faculty**

> o Adjusts publications count by faculty strength to ensure fairness.
>
> o Prevents larger institutions from scoring disproportionately high purely due to size.

2\. **Weighted Publications**

> o Different types of publications may carry different weights:
>
> ▪ Journal articles (peer-reviewed, indexed in Scopus/WoS).
>
> ▪ Conference papers (only high-quality/Scopus/WoS indexed).
>
> ▪ Books/chapters (depending on publisher reputation).

3\. **Quality Control via Third-Party Data**

> o Data extracted from international bibliometric databases.
>
> o Reduces possibility of inflated/false self-reporting.

**Scoring Example**

· Actual Faculty Strength = 500

· Nominal Faculty Requirement = (Students ÷ 15) = 600

· FRQ = max(500, 600) = 600

· Weighted Publications (P) = 1200 (3-year average)

· Ratio = P / FRQ = 1200 / 600 = 2.0

· If f(2.0) = 1 (normalized to cap), then PU = 30 Marks (full score).

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Source: Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed, etc.

· Time Frame: Publications in the previous 3 years.

· Faculty Strength Data: Submitted by institution for normalization.

**Why It Matters**

· Rewards consistent research output per faculty.

· Ensures quality + quantity balance through bibliometric sources.

· Encourages institutions to focus on research productivity and visibility.

· Builds global competitiveness of Indian HEIs in research rankings.

**Primary Source: Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed, etc.**

· What it means:

> ▪ Publication data must be extracted from recognized, international bibliometric databases.
>
> ▪ Scopus and Web of Science (WoS) are the primary sources accepted by NIRF for bibliometric analysis.
>
> ▪ For medical/biomedical institutions, PubMed is also accepted.

· Purpose:

> ▪ Ensures authenticity and standardization of research publication counts.
>
> ▪ Prevents inclusion of publications from non-indexed, predatory, or unverified journals.

· Data captured:

> o Number of papers published by institution faculty in indexed journals.
>
> o Citation counts, h-index, field-weighted impact.

**Time Frame: Publications in the previous 3 years**

· What it means:

> ▪ Only publications from the preceding three calendar years are considered.
>
> ▪ Example: If NIRF 2025 is being submitted, only publications from 2022, 2023, 2024 will count.

· Purpose:

> ▪ Focuses on recent research performance rather than lifetime output.
>
> ▪ Ensures fair comparison across institutions of different ages.

· Data captured:

> ▪ Institution must provide faculty-wise or department-wise publication lists for the specified years.
>
> ▪ Bibliometric data providers (Scopus/WoS) generate citation and publication reports for the same window.

**Faculty Strength Data: Submitted by institution for normalization**

· What it means:

> ▪ Research output is normalized against faculty strength to ensure fairness.
>
> ▪ Larger institutions with more faculty should not be unfairly advantaged in raw publication counts.

· Purpose:

> ▪ Normalization balances research productivity per faculty.
>
> ▪ A smaller institution with high research intensity can score better than a larger but low-output one.

· Data required from Institution:

> ▪ Total number of faculty (PhD + Master's) as submitted in Faculty Strength Report (FSR).
>
> ▪ Verified faculty list aligned with HR records and NIRF faculty submission.

· Normalization Formula (simplified):

> o Publications per Faculty = (Total Publications in last 3 years) ÷ (Faculty Strength).
>
> o Citation metrics are also normalized similarly.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Ensures research and professional practice (RP) metrics are evidence-based.

· Rewards quality over quantity, emphasizing citations, impact factor, and recent productivity.

· Normalization ensures equity between large public universities and smaller specialized institutions.

**16. Combined Metric for Quality of Publications (QP)**

**Formula**

· QP = 15 × f(CC / FRQ) + 15 × f(TOP25P / P)

**Where:**

· CC = Total Citation Count over the last 3 years.

· P = Publications (as computed in PU metric).

· TOP25P = Number of publications in the top 25% citation percentile, averaged over the last 3 years.

· FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max(Nominal Faculty (from 1:15 FSR), Actual Faculty).

· f(x) = Normalization function applied by NIRF for fairness across disciplines.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Citation Impact (15 Marks)**

> o Evaluates average citations per faculty.
>
> o High citations → greater research influence and recognition.
>
> o Normalized by FRQ to ensure fairness for different institution sizes.

2\. **High-Impact Research in Top 25% Journals (15 Marks)**

> o Rewards institutions with publications in globally recognized, highly cited journals.
>
> o Encourages researchers to aim for quality over quantity.

**Scoring Example**

· Faculty Reference Quantity (FRQ) = 600

· Publications (P) = 1200

· Citation Count (CC) = 24,000 → CC/FRQ = 40

· TOP25P = 300 → ratio TOP25P/P = 300/1200 = 0.25 (25%)

Then:

· First Term = 15 × f(40) → Suppose normalized to 12

· Second Term = 15 × f(0.25) = 15 × 1 = 15

· QP = 12 + 15 = 27 Marks (out of 30)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data:

> o Bibliometric data from Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed, Dimensions.
>
> o Citations must be averaged over the previous 3 years.

· Verification:

> o Extracted directly from third-party databases.
>
> o May require institution's Scopus/WoS ID mapping.

**Why It Matters**

· Rewards quality and impact of research, not just volume.

· Encourages publishing in top journals with higher visibility.

· Aligns Indian institutions with global best practices in research evaluation.

· Ensures focus on long-term citation relevance, not just quantity.

**Primary Data**

· **Bibliometric Data Sources**

> o Accepted sources:
>
> ▪ Scopus (Elsevier) → Broad coverage across disciplines.
>
> ▪ Web of Science (WoS) (Clarivate) → High-quality indexed journals.
>
> ▪ PubMed (NIH) → Medical and life sciences.
>
> ▪ Dimensions (Digital Science) → Citations, patents, clinical trials, policy documents.
>
> o Ensures authenticity and global comparability of research output.

· **Citations Averaged Over Previous 3 Years**

> o Citation impact is not taken as a lifetime count, but averaged for the last 3 years only.
>
> o Formula (simplified):
>
> ▪ Average Citations per Paper = (Total citations to papers published in last 3 years) ÷ (Total papers in last 3 years).
>
> o Purpose:
>
> ▪ Captures recent impact rather than historic/lifetime numbers.
>
> ▪ Prevents older, highly cited papers from inflating scores unfairly.

**Verification**

· **Extracted Directly from Third-Party Databases**

> ▪ NIRF does not rely on institution-reported publication data alone.
>
> ▪ Bibliometric data is pulled directly from Scopus/WoS/Dimensions databases via APIs or licensed datasets.
>
> ▪ Ensures consistency, transparency, and prevents inflation of publication claims.

· **Institution's Scopus/WoS ID Mapping**

> o Each institution is mapped to a Scopus Affiliation ID / WoS Organization ID.
>
> o Helps in accurately attributing publications to the correct institution.
>
> o Required because:
>
> ▪ Faculty may publish under different department names, campuses, or slightly varied institution names.
>
> ▪ Without ID mapping, data retrieval may miss or misattribute publications.
>
> o Institutions must verify and provide their official Affiliation IDs to NIRF.

**Why This Matters for NIRF**

· Forms the backbone of the Research and Professional Practices (RP) parameter.

· Ensures quality, integrity, and fairness in research assessment.

· Prevents data manipulation and highlights institutions contributing to global academic research visibility.

**17. IPR and Patents**

**Formula**

· IPR = IPG + IPP

> o IPG (Patents Granted): IPG = 10 × f(PG)
>
> o PG = Number of patents granted over the last 3 years.
>
> o Max 10 marks.
>
> o IPP (Patents Published): IPP = 5 × f(PP)
>
> o PP = Number of patents published (applications published in gazettes) over the last 3 years.
>
> o Max 5 marks.
>
> o f() = Normalization function used by NIRF to scale counts across institutions and disciplines.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Innovation Pipeline**

> o Patents published reflect inventions disclosed for examination.
>
> o Shows institutional culture of innovation and filing.

2\. **Innovation Quality**

> o Patents granted reflect novelty + inventive step verified by patent authorities.
>
> o Emphasizes quality and commercial value.

3\. **Balance between Pipeline & Outcomes**

> o Weighting (10 for granted, 5 for published) ensures finalized IP gets more credit.

**Scoring Example**

· 3-Year Data:

> o Published Patents (PP) = 40
>
> o Granted Patents (PG) = 12

· If normalization function is proportional with caps:

> o IPP = 5 × f(40) = say 4.5
>
> o IPG = 10 × f(12) = say 8
>
> o IPR = 12.5 (out of 15)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise list of published & granted patents.
>
> o Jurisdiction: India (IPO), international (USPTO, EPO, WIPO).

· Verification:

> o Patent certificates (for granted).
>
> o Publication records (for published).
>
> o IP Cell / TTO records, patent office databases.

**Why It Matters**

· Encourages institutions to foster innovation and IP culture.

· Connects research with industry impact & commercialization.

· Supports India's positioning as an innovation-driven economy.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Year-wise list of published & granted patents**

> o Institution must maintain and submit a year-wise list of:
>
> o Patents Published (filed and published in official gazette but not yet granted).
>
> o Patents Granted (legally approved and assigned patent rights).
>
> o Data must cover the previous 3 years for NIRF submission.
>
> o Details required:
>
> ▪ Patent Title
>
> ▪ Inventor(s) / Faculty / Student
>
> ▪ Filing Year & Publication Year
>
> ▪ Grant Year (if applicable)
>
> ▪ Patent Number / Application Number

· **Jurisdiction: India & International**

> o Must specify where the patent was filed/granted:
>
> ▪ **IPO (Indian Patent Office)** – domestic patents.
>
> ▪ **USPTO (United States Patent and Trademark Office)** – US patents.
>
> ▪ **EPO (European Patent Office)** – European patents.
>
> ▪ **WIPO (World Intellectual Property Organization)** – International PCT filings.
>
> o Purpose:
>
> ▪ Highlights the institution's global innovation footprint.
>
> ▪ NIRF assigns higher weightage to granted patents and international patents.

**Verification**

· **Patent Certificates (for granted)**

> o Verified through official grant certificate issued by the respective patent authority (IPO/USPTO/EPO/WIPO).
>
> o Confirms legal approval of intellectual property rights.

· **Publication Records (for published)**

> o Verified through official publication record (patent gazette, IPO website, WIPO publication).
>
> o Provides proof that the patent is filed and published but not yet granted.

· **IP Cell / TTO Records, Patent Office Databases**

> o Institutional IP Cell / Technology Transfer Office (TTO) maintains patent filing and grant records.
>
> o Cross-verification through:
>
> o IPO online database (India).
>
> o USPTO / EPO / WIPO databases for international patents.
>
> o Ensures authenticity and prevents duplication or inflated claims.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Patent data contributes to Research & Professional Practice (RP) in NIRF.

· Showcases the institution's innovation, industry relevance, and commercialization potential.

· Internationally granted patents are considered high-value indicators of global competitiveness.

**18. Footprint of Projects and Professional Practice (FPPP)**

**Formula**

· FPPP = FPR + FPC + EDP/MDP

> o FPR = 5 × f(RF)
>
> o RF = Average annual research funding earnings (grants received, institute-level) over the last 3 years.
>
> o FPC = 5 × f(CF)
>
> o CF = Average annual consultancy earnings (amount received from industry/clients, institute-level) over the last 3 years.
>
> o EDP/MDP = 5 × f(EP)
>
> o EP = Average annual earnings from Executive Development Programs / Management Development Programs in the last 3 years.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Research Funding Success (FPR – 5 Marks)**

> o Rewards institutions for securing competitive research grants (Govt., industry, international).
>
> o Measures ability to attract funding and sustain research ecosystem.

2\. **Consultancy Engagements (FPC – 5 Marks)**

> o Reflects real-world problem solving for industry and government.
>
> o Indicates knowledge transfer and institution–industry partnerships.

3\. **Executive / Management Development Programs (EDP/MDP – 5 Marks)**

> o Measures revenue from training executives, professionals, or government officers.
>
> o Demonstrates institution's role in continuing education and upskilling workforce.

**Scoring Example**

· Research Funding (RF) = ₹15 Cr average / 3 years → f(RF) normalized to 0.9 → FPR = 5 × 0.9 = 4.5

· Consultancy (CF) = ₹5 Cr average / 3 years → f(CF) normalized to 0.6 → FPC = 5 × 0.6 = 3

· Executive Programs (EP) = ₹2 Cr average / 3 years → f(EP) normalized to 0.5 → EDP = 5 × 0.5 = 2.5

· FPPP = 4.5 + 3 + 2.5 = 10 Marks (out of 15)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise income from funded projects, consultancy, and executive training programs.
>
> o Only amounts actually received (not sanctioned) should be reported.

· Verification:

> o Audited financial statements.
>
> o Grant sanction letters and utilization certificates.
>
> o Consultancy contracts and invoices.
>
> o EDP/MDP program fee receipts.

**Why It Matters**

· Encourages institutions to diversify beyond tuition fees.

· Promotes industry partnerships and professional impact.

· Links academic institutions to economic and societal development.

· Demonstrates capacity for applied research and knowledge commercialization.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Year-wise income from funded projects**

> o Refers to financial support received from:
>
> ▪ National agencies (DST, DBT, CSIR, UGC, AICTE, ICMR, SERB, etc.).
>
> ▪ International funding agencies (World Bank, EU Horizon, Gates Foundation, NIH, etc.).
>
> ▪ Industry-sponsored R&D projects.
>
> o Only amounts actually received in a given year should be reported (not just sanctioned or approved).
>
> o Includes both research grants and infrastructure development grants.

· **Year-wise income from consultancy**

> o Refers to payments received from industry, government, or organizations for consultancy services provided by faculty/institution.
>
> o May include:
>
> ▪ Technical consultancy.
>
> ▪ Advisory projects.
>
> ▪ Testing and certification services.
>
> o Reported amount must reflect actual receipts, not projected or pending amounts.

· **Year-wise income from executive training programs**

> o Income generated from EDPs (Executive Development Programs) and MDPs (Management Development Programs) conducted for industry professionals.
>
> o Includes training fees collected from participants, companies, or sponsoring agencies.
>
> o Evidence required: receipts and bank records of actual payments.

**Verification**

· **Audited financial statements**

> o Must match institution's annual audited accounts.
>
> o Ensures reported numbers are authentic, approved by auditors, and traceable in institutional books.

· **Grant sanction letters and utilization certificates**

> o Sanction letter = Proof of grant approval.
>
> o Utilization certificate = Proof of actual use of funds and disbursement.
>
> o Together, they validate research funding received.

· **Consultancy contracts and invoices**

> o Consultancy agreements signed between institution and client.
>
> o Payment invoices issued and corresponding receipts (bank statements).
>
> o Confirms authenticity of consultancy income.

· **EDP/MDP program fee receipts**

> o Participant fee receipts, sponsoring organization payment slips, or institutional receipts.
>
> o Validates actual income from training programs.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Falls under Research & Professional Practice (RP) parameter.

· Demonstrates financial health, industry collaboration, and applied research capacity of the institution.

· Ensures quality and transparency of reported income.

· Institutions with strong funded projects, consultancy, and training programs score higher in NIRF ranking.

**19. Combined Metric for Publications & Citations in SDGs (PSDGs)**

**Formula**

· PSDGs = 5 × f(PUSDG / FRQ) + 5 × f(CISDG / FRQ)

**Where:**

· PUSDG = Weighted number of publications related to UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) over the past 3 years.

· CISDG = Total citations of SDG-related publications over the past 3 years.

· FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max(Nominal Faculty (based on FSR 1:15), Actual Faculty strength).

· f(x) = Normalization function defined by NIRF, adjusting for size and discipline variations.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Publication Effort in SDG Themes (5 Marks)**

> o Measures research output aligned with UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) (e.g., climate action, health, clean energy, gender equity).
>
> o Encourages institutions to focus research on societal and global challenges.

2\. **Impact of SDG Research through Citations (5 Marks)**

> o Captures visibility and recognition of SDG-related work.
>
> o Rewards institutions whose SDG publications are cited and used globally.

**Scoring Example**

· Faculty Reference Quantity (FRQ) = 500

· PUSDG = 800 → PUSDG/FRQ = 1.6

· CISDG = 12,000 → CISDG/FRQ = 24

· Suppose NIRF normalization caps full marks at PUSDG/FRQ ≥ 1.5 and CISDG/FRQ ≥ 20.

> o Publication Component = 5 × 1 = 5
>
> o Citation Component = 5 × 1 = 5
>
> o PSDGs = 10 Marks (Full Score)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Source: Scopus, Web of Science, Dimensions, or Elsevier's SDG mapping (used in THE Impact Rankings too).

· Time Frame: Last 3 years.

· Verification:

> o Bibliometric mapping to SDG keywords (e.g., renewable energy, public health).
>
> o Institution-specific author IDs to avoid false attribution.

**Why It Matters**

· Aligns Indian higher education with global sustainability agendas.

· Encourages universities to contribute solutions to climate change, poverty, health, equity, and environment.

· Enhances visibility in international rankings (e.g., THE Impact Rankings).

· Promotes interdisciplinary collaboration.

**Primary Source**

· Accepted Databases

> ▪ **Scopus (Elsevier)** – Widely used for SDG bibliometric mapping.
>
> ▪ **Web of Science (WoS) (Clarivate)** – Journal quality and citation tracking.
>
> ▪ **Dimensions (Digital Science)** – Expanded coverage including policy, patents, grants.
>
> ▪ **Elsevier's SDG Mapping Tool** – Same methodology used in THE Impact Rankings, links publications to UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

· Why these sources:

> o Provide standardized and globally comparable data.
>
> o Prevent inclusion of non-indexed or predatory publications.

**Time Frame**

· Last 3 years only.

> o Example: For NIRF 2025 → publications from 2022, 2023, 2024 are considered.

· Reason:

> o Keeps focus on recent contributions to SDG-related research.
>
> o Prevents institutions from inflating numbers with older, unrelated work.

**Verification**

· **Bibliometric Mapping to SDG Keywords**

> o Each publication is matched to SDG targets/keywords (e.g., SDG 3 → “public health,” SDG 7 → “renewable energy,” SDG 13 → “climate change mitigation”).
>
> o Databases like Scopus and Dimensions already provide SDG-tagged outputs.
>
> o Institutions must validate by cross-checking keywords in abstracts, titles, and indexed terms.

· **Institution-Specific Author IDs**

> o To ensure publications are correctly attributed to the reporting institution:
>
> ▪ Scopus Affiliation ID (unique to institution).
>
> ▪ WoS Organization Enhanced ID.
>
> ▪ Dimensions GRID/ROR ID.
>
> o Prevents errors such as:
>
> ▪ Faculty with similar names at different institutions.
>
> ▪ Publications wrongly linked to the wrong campus.
>
> o Institutions must provide these IDs to NIRF for accurate data extraction.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Aligns with NEP 2020 and global benchmarking frameworks (THE Impact Rankings, QS Sustainability).

· Highlights how institutions contribute to United Nations SDGs through research.

· Encourages socially relevant and impactful research beyond academic output.

**20. Graduation Outcomes (GO)**

**NIRF 2024 – Graduation Outcomes (GO) Breakdown**

| **S. No.** | **Sub-Parameter**                                    | **Marks** | **Remarks**                                                                                         |
|------------|------------------------------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 3A         | Metric for University Examinations (GUE)             | 60        | Measures student success in university-level examinations, progression, and performance indicators  |
| 3B         | Metric for Number of Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) | 40        | Captures doctoral research output by assessing the number of Ph.D. students successfully graduating |
| **Total**  | **–**                                                | **100**   | **Weighted overall contribution = 20% of NIRF Score**                                               |

***A. Metric for University Examinations (GUE) – 60 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Success of students in university examinations at the UG and PG levels.
>
> o Indicators include pass percentages, on-time graduations, and academic progression.

· Why it matters:

> o Reflects the academic effectiveness of teaching-learning systems.
>
> o High graduation rates show strong student support, quality teaching, and effective assessment systems.

· Impact:

> o Institutions with consistent examination performance build trust in academic quality and student success rates.

***B. Metric for Number of Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 40 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Number of doctoral candidates successfully graduating in a given period.
>
> o Indicates the capacity of an institution to produce trained researchers.

· Why it matters:

> o Doctoral graduates are central to the research ecosystem of India.
>
> o They contribute to publications, innovation, and industry-academia collaborations.

· Impact:

> o A higher number of Ph.D. graduates signals strong research infrastructure, faculty guidance, and global competitiveness.

**Overall Significance of GO**

· GO contributes 20% to the NIRF ranking score.

· It serves as a direct measure of institutional effectiveness in producing successful graduates at both academic and research levels.

· Balanced focus:

> o GUE (60%) → captures general student academic success.
>
> o GPHD (40%) → emphasizes advanced research training and doctoral output.

**21. Metric for University Examinations (GUE)**

**Formula**

· GUE = 60 × min\[(Ng / 80), 1\]

> o Ng = Percentage of Students (as a fraction of approved intake), averaged over the previous 3 years, passing the respective university examinations in the stipulated time.
>
> o Stipulated time = Normal duration of the program (e.g., 3 years for UG, 2 years for PG).

**What It Captures**

1\. **Student Success in Exams**

> o Measures academic performance and progression.
>
> o High GUE indicates students are completing programs on time.

2\. **Retention & Efficiency**

> o Rewards institutions where students graduate within the prescribed duration.
>
> o Penalizes institutions with delays, dropouts, or poor exam results.

3\. **Quality of Teaching & Learning**

> o Indirectly reflects effectiveness of faculty quality, pedagogy, curriculum delivery, and academic support systems.

**Scoring Logic**

· Ng ≥ 80% → Full 60 Marks.

· Ng \< 80% → Proportionally reduced marks.

· Ng Example Calculation:

> o Approved Intake (3 years average) = 1,000
>
> o Students Graduated on Time = 750
>
> o Ng = (750 / 1000) × 100 = 75%
>
> o GUE = 60 × (75/80) = 56.25 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Approved intake for UG/PG programs.
>
> o Number of students graduated in stipulated time (year-wise for past 3 years).

· Verification:

> o University exam results, graduation lists, convocation reports, annual reports.

**Why It Matters**

· Ensures institutions are not only admitting students but also graduating them successfully.

· Reflects academic efficiency, governance, and student support systems.

· Provides an outcome-based measure of teaching-learning quality.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Approved intake for UG/PG programs**

> o Institution must submit the sanctioned intake capacity for each UG and PG program.
>
> o This refers to the number of seats approved by statutory/regulatory bodies (AICTE, UGC, PCI, COA, INC, MCI/NMC, etc.).
>
> o Data should be provided program-wise and year-wise.
>
> o Important because graduation performance is evaluated relative to intake size (capacity utilization).

· **Number of students graduated in stipulated time (last 3 years)**

> o Institution must report actual number of students graduating from UG/PG programs within the prescribed duration:
>
> o UG (3-year/4-year program depending on discipline).
>
> o PG (2-year or discipline-specific duration).
>
> o Reported year-wise for the last 3 years.
>
> o Focus is on students who completed within the minimum stipulated duration, not those with delays/extensions.

**Verification**

· **University Exam Results**

> o Official result records from the affiliating university or autonomous examination cell.
>
> o Confirms the pass-out numbers for each batch.

· **Graduation Lists**

> o Department-wise or program-wise lists of graduating students.
>
> o Includes roll numbers, registration numbers, or student IDs.

· **Convocation Reports**

> o Official reports from university/college convocations where degrees were awarded.
>
> o Often include the exact number of graduating students.

· **Annual Reports**

> o Institutional annual reports (submitted to governing bodies) include graduation statistics.
>
> o Provide consolidated numbers for verification.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Forms part of the Graduation Outcomes (GO) parameter.

· Reflects student success rates, efficiency of academic delivery, and program credibility.

· Institutions with high graduation rates in stipulated time demonstrate academic quality and effective student support.

**22. Metric for Ph.D. Graduates (GPHD)**

**Formula**

· GPHD = 40 × f(Nphd)

> o Nphd = Average number of Ph.D. students graduated (awarded degree) over the previous 3 years.
>
> o f(Nphd): A function defined by NIRF to normalize scores, ensuring comparability across institutions of different sizes.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Research Output via Human Capital**

> o Reflects how many doctoral scholars successfully completed their programs.
>
> o A key measure of institutional contribution to advanced research and innovation.

2\. **Sustainability of Research Programs**

> o Institutions consistently graduating Ph.D. students demonstrate strong faculty supervision, infrastructure, and funding ecosystems.

3\. **Alignment with National Goals**

> o Supports India's push to increase Ph.D. graduates under NEP 2020.
>
> o Enhances the knowledge economy and research workforce.

**Scoring Logic**

· Higher average Ph.D. graduations → higher GPHD score.

· For example (illustrative only):

> o If average Ph.D. graduates ≥ benchmark (say 100 per year), institution may score full 40 marks.
>
> o If below benchmark, marks are proportionally scaled.

**Example Calculation:**

· Ph.D. Graduates over 3 years = \[60, 70, 80\]

· Nphd = (60 + 70 + 80) / 3 = 70

· If f(Nphd) = (Nphd / 100) capped at 1, then GPHD = 40 × (70/100) = 28 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Number of Ph.D. students graduating in each of the last 3 years.
>
> o Source: Annual Reports, Convocation Reports, University Records.

· Verification:

> o Cross-verified against degree award records, doctoral committee minutes, or official convocation lists.

**Why It Matters**

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Strengthen doctoral research programs.
>
> o Provide timely completion support (mentorship, funding, infrastructure).
>
> o Align with global best practices in research productivity.

· Acts as a key differentiator for research-intensive institutions vs teaching-focused colleges.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Number of Ph.D. students graduating in each of the last 3 years**

> o Institution must report the count of Ph.D. degrees awarded (not admissions, enrollments, or thesis submissions).
>
> o Year-wise data for the last 3 academic years is required.
>
> o Details should include:
>
> ▪ Student Name / Registration Number.
>
> ▪ Department / Discipline.
>
> ▪ Title of Thesis.
>
> ▪ Date of Viva-Voce / Final Defense.
>
> ▪ Date of Degree Award / Convocation.
>
> o Only those whose degrees were officially awarded are counted.

· Source Documents

> o **Annual Reports** – contain consolidated data on research output including doctoral completions.
>
> o **Convocation Reports** – official listing of students receiving Ph.D. degrees.
>
> o **University Records** – maintained by Controller of Examinations / Academic Council.

**Verification**

· **Cross-verification against degree award records**

> o Official Ph.D. award registers maintained by the university.
>
> o Ensures accuracy and prevents inclusion of incomplete theses.

· **Doctoral Committee Minutes**

> o Departmental or institutional doctoral committees approve thesis defense and recommend degree award.
>
> o Used as supporting evidence.

· **Official Convocation Lists**

> o Final and most authentic source, as these lists are published during convocation ceremonies.
>
> o Contain roll numbers, thesis titles, and awarding dates.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Directly feeds into the Graduation Outcomes (GO) metric, specifically GPHD (Number of Ph.D. Students Graduated).

· Strong indicator of research culture, doctoral supervision quality, and institutional contribution to knowledge generation.

· High and consistent Ph.D. output is a major positive factor in ranking.

**23. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)**

**NIRF 2024 – Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) Breakdown**

| **S. No.** | **Sub-Parameter**                                                              | **Marks** | **Remarks**                                                                                                  |
|------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 4A         | Percentage of Students from Other States / Countries (Regional Diversity – RD) | 30        | Captures geographic diversity by measuring the proportion of students from outside the home state or country |
| 4B         | Percentage of Women (Women Diversity – WD)                                     | 30        | Reflects gender diversity in enrollment, faculty, and leadership roles                                       |
| 4C         | Economically and Socially Challenged Students (ESCS)                           | 20        | Measures access and support for disadvantaged groups (scholarships, fee waivers, reservations)               |
| 4D         | Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS)                            | 20        | Evaluates infrastructure, resources, and inclusivity measures for differently-abled students                 |
| **Total**  | **–**                                                                          | **100**   | **Weighted overall contribution = 10% of NIRF Score**                                                        |

***A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Percentage of students enrolled from other states or foreign countries.

· Why it matters:

> o Promotes a nationally and globally diverse learning environment.
>
> o Institutions attracting students from across geographies demonstrate wider appeal and competitiveness.

***B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Percentage of female students, faculty members, and leadership positions.

· Why it matters:

> o Encourages gender equity in higher education.
>
> o Reflects inclusivity in academic participation and opportunities.

***C. Economically and Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Enrollment and support systems for students from disadvantaged categories (SC/ST/OBC, EWS, minority communities).
>
> o Includes scholarships, fee waivers, hostel facilities, and targeted welfare schemes.

· Why it matters:

> o Ensures higher education is accessible and equitable.
>
> o Promotes social justice and compliance with constitutional provisions.

***D. Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Availability of infrastructure and resources for differently-abled students.
>
> o Examples: ramps, lifts, tactile paths, accessible toilets, Braille books, sign language support.

· Why it matters:

> o Promotes a barrier-free environment in higher education.
>
> o Ensures participation and success of students with disabilities.

**Overall Significance of OI**

· OI contributes 10% to the NIRF overall ranking.

· It ensures rankings reward institutions that go beyond academics to promote:

> o Diversity (regional & gender)
>
> o Equity (supporting disadvantaged groups)
>
> o Accessibility (facilities for differently-abled)

**24. Regional Diversity (RD)**

**Formula**

· RD = (25 × fraction of total students enrolled from other states) + (5 × fraction of students from other countries)

**Components**

1\. **Students from Other States – 25 Marks**

> o Fraction of students enrolled from states other than where the institution is located.
>
> o Encourages cross-regional mobility and national inclusivity.
>
> o Institutions that attract diverse student populations score higher.

2\. **Students from Other Countries – 5 Marks**

> o Fraction of foreign students enrolled in UG, PG, and Ph.D. programs.
>
> o Promotes internationalization of higher education.
>
> o Aligns with India's ambition to be a global education hub under NEP 2020.

**Scoring Example**

· Total Students = 5,000

· Students from other Indian states = 1,500 (30%)

· Students from other countries = 250 (5%)

· RD Score = (25 × 0.30) + (5 × 0.05) = 7.5 + 0.25 = 7.75 Marks (out of 30)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o State-wise & country-wise student enrollment data.
>
> o Verified from admission records.

· Verification:

> o Admission registers, student ID records, nationality/residential proof.

**Why It Matters**

· Promotes student diversity in institutions.

· Ensures exposure to multiple cultures, languages, and perspectives.

· Rewards institutions attracting students across India & globally.

· Aligns with India's push towards international rankings and global visibility.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **State-wise Student Enrollment Data**

> o Institution must provide the number of students enrolled state/UT-wise across India.
>
> o Data should cover all active UG, PG, and Ph.D. students.
>
> o Helps evaluate the regional diversity of the institution.
>
> o Example:
>
> ▪ Tamil Nadu – 320 students
>
> ▪ Maharashtra – 210 students
>
> ▪ Delhi – 150 students

· **Country-wise Student Enrollment Data**

> o Institution must report the number of international students enrolled.
>
> o Data must be provided country-wise.
>
> o Example:
>
> ▪ Nepal – 20 students
>
> ▪ Nigeria – 12 students
>
> ▪ Bangladesh – 8 students
>
> o Used to assess internationalization of education.

· **Verification from Admission Records**

> o Data must be consistent with actual admission office records.
>
> o Includes student program details, enrollment numbers, and year of admission.

**Verification**

· **Admission Registers**

> o Official records maintained by the admission cell.
>
> o Provide student details (name, program, year of admission, domicile).

· **Student ID Records**

> o Institution-issued student identity cards, enrollment numbers, or database entries.
>
> o Confirms the authenticity of enrolled students.

· **Nationality/Residential Proof**

> o For Indian students → Domicile certificate, Aadhaar, voter ID, etc.
>
> o For international students → Passport, student visa, FRRO registration.
>
> o Confirms correctness of state-wise/country-wise categorization.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Supports the Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) parameter.

· Demonstrates geographical diversity of student intake.

· International students increase global engagement score.

· Institutions with wider student diversity score higher.

**25. Women Diversity (WD)**

**Formula**

· WD = (15 × (NWS / 50)) + (15 × (NWF / 20))

> o NWS = % of Women Students (in total enrollment).
>
> o NWF = % of Women Faculty (including senior administrative positions such as HoDs, Deans, Directors, Institute Heads).
>
> o Benchmarks:
>
> o 50% women students → full 15 marks.
>
> o 20% women faculty → full 15 marks.

**Components**

1\. **Women Students (15 Marks)**

> o Measures gender inclusivity in student enrollment.
>
> o High scores awarded when institutions approach gender balance (~50%).
>
> o Encourages universities to remove barriers to women's participation in higher education.

2\. **Women Faculty & Leadership (15 Marks)**

> o Evaluates women's representation in faculty positions and decision-making roles.
>
> o Promotes gender equity in academic employment and leadership.
>
> o Rewards institutions where women faculty exceed 20% benchmark.

**Scoring Example**

· Total Students = 4,000

· Women Students = 1,600 → NWS = 40%

· Total Faculty = 400

· Women Faculty = 120 → NWF = 30%

· WD = (15 × (40/50)) + (15 × (30/20))

· WD = (12) + (15) = 27 Marks (out of 30)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data:

> o Student enrollment by gender (UG, PG, Ph.D.)
>
> o Faculty list with gender distribution.

· Verification:

> o Admission registers, HR/faculty database, annual reports.

**Why It Matters**

· Supports SDG 5: Gender Equality.

· Promotes women's empowerment in higher education both as learners and leaders.

· Enhances institutional reputation for inclusivity.

· Ensures diversity in teaching, research, and administration.

**Primary Data**

· **Student Enrollment by Gender (UG, PG, Ph.D.)**

> o Institution must provide the number of male, female, and other gender students enrolled.
>
> o Data must be reported program-wise (UG, PG, Ph.D.) and consolidated year-wise.
>
> o Helps measure gender diversity among students.
>
> o Example:
>
