> ▪ UG: Male – 1,200 \| Female – 800 \| Others – 10
>
> o PG: Male – 400 \| Female – 300 \| Others – 5
>
> o Ph.D.: Male – 150 \| Female – 100 \| Others – 2

· **Faculty List with Gender Distribution**

> o Institution must submit a complete faculty list with gender data.
>
> o Categories: Male, Female, Others.
>
> o Includes regular, full-time faculty only (adjunct/visiting faculty not counted).
>
> o Example:
>
> ▪ Male – 320
>
> ▪ Female – 180
>
> ▪ Others – 5

**Verification**

· **Admission Registers**

> o Official admission data containing student details, including gender.
>
> o Verified against student identity and enrollment records.

· **HR / Faculty Database**

> o Institutional HR records containing faculty profiles with gender information.
>
> o Cross-checked with payroll and official appointments.

· **Annual Reports**

> o Institutional annual reports include gender-based statistics for both students and faculty.
>
> o Acts as consolidated evidence.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Gender distribution contributes to Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) parameter.

· Promotes gender balance and inclusivity in higher education.

· Institutions with a healthy gender ratio (especially higher female participation) score better.

**26. Economically and Socially Challenged Students (ESCS)**

**Formula**

· ESCS = 20 × f(Nesc)

> o Nesc = % of students receiving full tuition fee reimbursement by the institution (scholarships, fellowships, financial aid, fee waivers).
>
> o Function f(Nesc) scales marks according to proportion of supported students.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Equity in Access**

> o Rewards institutions that support economically weaker sections (EWS) and socially disadvantaged groups (SC/ST/OBC, minorities).
>
> o Reflects social justice and inclusivity mandate of higher education.

2\. **Financial Assistance Provided**

> o Full tuition fee support (not partial) is considered.
>
> o Support may come from:
>
> ▪ Government schemes (e.g., Post-Matric Scholarships, EWS fee waivers).
>
> ▪ Institutional schemes (fee concessions, free-ships).
>
> ▪ CSR/Philanthropic funding routed through the institution.

3\. **Impact on Inclusivity**

> o Ensures that talented students are not denied education due to financial constraints.
>
> o Enhances diversity by including students from challenged socio-economic backgrounds.

**Scoring Logic**

· Higher % of ESCS students = higher score.

· Example:

> o Total Students = 5000
>
> o ESCS Students = 1000 (20%)
>
> o ESCS Score = 20 × 0.20 = 4 Marks (out of 20)

· If Nesc = 100% (all students fully reimbursed) → full 20 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise count of total students.
>
> o Number of students receiving full tuition reimbursement.

· Verification Sources:

> o Scholarship registers.
>
> o Fee waiver records from accounts section.
>
> o Government funding utilization certificates.

**Why It Matters**

· Promotes NEP 2020 vision of equitable and inclusive access to higher education.

· Encourages institutions to actively support marginalized and underprivileged students.

· Helps build a socially diverse campus environment.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Year-wise count of total students**

> o Institution must provide the total number of students enrolled for each of the last 3 years.
>
> o Includes UG, PG, and Ph.D. students (full-time only).
>
> o Used as the denominator for calculating scholarship coverage percentage.

· **Number of students receiving full tuition reimbursement**

> o Refers to students who receive 100% fee waiver or full scholarship.
>
> o Covers:
>
> ▪ Government scholarships (Central/State schemes).
>
> ▪ Institutional fee waivers (merit, need-based).
>
> ▪ Industry-sponsored scholarships.
>
> o Must be reported year-wise.
>
> o Only students with full tuition reimbursement are counted (partial aid is excluded for this metric).

**Verification Sources**

· **Scholarship Registers**

> o Maintained by the institution's scholarship/financial aid cell.
>
> o Contains student name, program, scholarship scheme, and amount awarded.

· **Fee Waiver Records (Accounts Section)**

> o Official accounts/finance records showing tuition waived for eligible students.
>
> o Confirms the actual amount waived, not just sanctioned.

· **Government Funding Utilization Certificates**

> o Certificates issued by funding authorities confirming disbursement of scholarships.
>
> o Used for cross-verification with institutional records.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Forms part of Outreach & Inclusivity (OI).

· Reflects institution's efforts in equity and access by supporting students from weaker backgrounds.

· Institutions with higher % of students receiving full scholarships score better.

**27. Physically Challenged Students (PCS)**

**Formula**

· PCS = 20 Marks if full facilities are provided

· Partial marks if facilities are incomplete or limited.

· Score is based on verifiable responses to NIRF's prescribed checklist.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Accessibility of Campus Infrastructure**

> o Ramps, lifts, tactile paths, accessible washrooms, transport facilities.
>
> o Ensures barrier-free mobility for differently abled students.

2\. **Learning Support Systems**

> o Assistive technologies (screen readers, Braille printers, audiobooks).
>
> o Sign language interpreters for lectures.
>
> o Extra time and alternate exam formats.

3\. **Accommodation & Social Inclusion**

> o Reserved seats and scholarships.
>
> o Hostel facilities with accessible rooms.
>
> o Campus clubs, mentorship programs, and peer-support initiatives.

4\. **Policy & Compliance**

> o Alignment with RPwD Act 2016 and UGC Accessibility Guidelines.
>
> o Proactive measures for equal opportunity.

**Scoring Logic**

· Full 20 Marks if:

> o All key facilities (infrastructure + academic support + inclusivity measures) are provided.

· Proportional Score if:

> o Only partial facilities exist (e.g., ramps without ICT assistive tools).

· 0 Marks if:

> o No measures in place.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of physical & learning support facilities.
>
> o Evidence: photographs, policy documents, usage records.

· Verification:

> o Physical inspection, audited compliance reports, accessibility audits.

**Why It Matters**

· Promotes inclusive education by ensuring access for all learners.

· Strengthens social responsibility of institutions.

· Enhances global ranking visibility, as inclusivity is a key metric in QS/Times rankings too.

· Directly supports SDG 4 (Quality Education) and SDG 10 (Reduced Inequalities).

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **List of Physical & Learning Support Facilities**

> o Institution must provide a comprehensive list of facilities available for differently-abled students and inclusive learning.
>
> o Examples include:
>
> ▪ **Physical Access** → ramps, lifts with Braille, wheelchair-accessible washrooms, tactile paths.
>
> ▪ **Assistive Technologies** → screen readers, speech-to-text software, magnifiers.
>
> ▪ **Library Support** → Braille books, audiobooks, e-resources with accessibility features.
>
> ▪ **Exam & Classroom Support** → extra time in exams, scribes, sign language interpreters, ergonomic seating.
>
> ▪ **Residential & Campus Support** → accessible hostels, medical facilities, dedicated help desks.

· Evidence Required

> o Photographs of installed infrastructure (ramps, tactile paths, lifts).
>
> o Policy Documents (institutional policy on inclusivity, accessibility guidelines).
>
> o Usage Records (logs of facility usage, requests from differently-abled students, records of assistive devices issued).

**Verification**

· **Physical Inspection**

> o On-site inspection by accreditation agencies or government-appointed committees.
>
> o Confirms actual availability of reported facilities.

· **Audited Compliance Reports**

> o Third-party or internal audit reports confirming compliance with:
>
> o RPwD Act, 2016 (Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act).
>
> o UGC guidelines for barrier-free access.
>
> o Provides authenticity for institutional claims.

· **Accessibility Audits**

> o Independent accessibility audits conducted by certified agencies.
>
> o Ensure facilities meet universal design standards.
>
> o Reports include compliance scores and recommendations.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Part of the Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) parameter.

· Demonstrates how institutions ensure equity, inclusivity, and support for differently-abled students.

· Institutions with stronger support systems score higher in NIRF.

**28. Perception (PR)**

**NIRF 2024 – Perception (PR) Breakdown**

| **S. No.**                       | **Sub-Parameter**                             | **Marks** | **Remarks**                                                                                               |
|----------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| 5A                               | Peer Perception: Academic Peers and Employers | 100       | Captures reputation of the institution among academics, employers, research organizations, and the public |
| Special Note (Universities only) | –                                             | –         | For universities, PR is split as 70% Peer Perception + 30% Accreditation scores                           |
| **Total**                        | **–**                                         | **100**   | **Weighted overall contribution = 10% of NIRF Score**                                                     |

***A. Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks***

· What it measures:

> o Reputation of the institution among academic peers, employers, research collaborators, and the public.
>
> o Survey-based metric capturing quality of graduates, employability, research strength, and academic standing.

· How it is collected:

> o NIRF conducts surveys with employers, recruiters, academics, and experts.
>
> o Includes peer recognition at national and international levels.

***Special Rule for Universities***

· In the case of universities, PR is not solely based on peer surveys.

· It is divided into:

> o 70% – Peer Perception (Academics & Employers)
>
> o 30% – Accreditation Scores (from NAAC/NBA or equivalent bodies).

· Why this matters:

> o Universities are often multidisciplinary with diverse outcomes, so accreditation provides an additional layer of quality assurance.
>
> o Helps avoid over-reliance on subjective peer opinion.

**Overall Significance of PR**

· PR contributes 10% to the NIRF overall ranking.

· Although its weight is smaller compared to TLR or RP, PR can significantly impact the rank of reputed institutions.

· Strong PR reflects:

> o Trust of employers in student employability.
>
> o Recognition by academic peers of research and teaching quality.
>
> o Validation of accreditation, especially for universities.

**NIRF 2024 – Consolidated Ranking Parameters**

| **Parameter**                          | **Marks** | **Weight** | **Contribution to Overall Score (Out of 100)** |
|----------------------------------------|-----------|------------|------------------------------------------------|
| Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR)   | 100       | 0.30       | 30                                             |
| Research & Professional Practices (RP) | 100       | 0.30       | 30                                             |
| Graduation Outcomes (GO)               | 100       | 0.20       | 20                                             |
| Outreach & Inclusivity (OI)            | 100       | 0.10       | 10                                             |
| Perception (PR)                        | 100       | 0.10       | 10                                             |
| **Total**                              | **500**   | **–**      | **100**                                        |

**29. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – NIRF 2024**

· Total Marks: 100

· Ranking Weight: 0.30 (30% of overall NIRF score)

· Overall Formula:

> TLR = SS(20) + FSR(25) + FQE(20) + FRU(20) + OE(10) + MIR(5)

**Component Metrics of TLR**

| **Code** | **Sub-Parameter**                                                   | **Marks** | **Explanation**                                                                                                                   |
|----------|---------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| SS       | Student Strength including Ph.D. Students                           | 20        | Evaluates student intake across UG, PG, and Ph.D. levels; higher doctoral student strength reflects strong research orientation   |
| FSR      | Faculty–Student Ratio with emphasis on permanent faculty            | 25        | Measures adequacy of teaching staff relative to student numbers, giving higher weight to permanent faculty for academic stability |
| FQE      | Combined metric for Faculty with PhD (or equivalent) and Experience | 20        | Rewards institutions for employing highly qualified and experienced faculty members                                               |
| FRU      | Financial Resources and Their Utilisation                           | 20        | Considers total financial resources, effective utilization of funds for academics, infrastructure, and research                   |
| OE       | Online Education (Syllabus Completion, Exams, SWAYAM)               | 10        | Assesses integration of e-learning, MOOC participation, and digital exam completion                                               |
| MIR      | Multiple Entry/Exit, Indian Knowledge System & Regional Languages   | 5         | Captures adoption of NEP 2020 reforms, inclusion of IKS, and promotion of regional language education                             |

**Significance of TLR**

· TLR is the largest single contributor (30%) to the overall NIRF score.

· It captures not only faculty strength and resources but also the institution's commitment to modernization and inclusivity:

> o Traditional metrics: faculty numbers, PhD qualifications, financial resources.
>
> o Modern reforms: online education, NEP-driven flexibility (MIR), cultural integration.

**A. Student Strength including Ph.D. Students (SS)**

· Maximum Marks: 20

· Formula:

> SS = f(NT,NE) × 15 + f(NP) × 5

**Breakdown of Formula Components**

1\. **f(NT, NE) → Function of sanctioned intake and actual enrollment**

> o NT: Total sanctioned approved intake for all UG and PG programs.
>
> o NE: Total number of students actually enrolled in UG and PG programs.
>
> o This function is designed by NIRF to balance between:
>
> o Institutional capacity (NT)
>
> o Actual student enrollment (NE)
>
> o Institutions with a high enrollment-to-sanctioned capacity ratio score better.
>
> o Weight in SS: 15 marks

2\. **f(NP) → Function of doctoral enrollment**

> o NP: Total number of students enrolled in Ph.D. programs (till the previous academic year).
>
> o More Ph.D. enrollment indicates a research-oriented ecosystem.
>
> o NIRF designs this function to reward institutions with strong doctoral programs.
>
> o Weight in SS: 5 marks

**Data Sources**

· Primary data: Institutions must provide NT, NE, and NP in the prescribed format on the NIRF portal.

· Verification:

> o NT and NE are cross-verified with AISHE, UGC, and AICTE databases.
>
> o NP (doctoral enrollment) is verified through institutional records and Ph.D. registration data.

**Significance of SS in TLR**

· Student strength contributes 20 marks out of 100 in TLR, and thus about 6% of the overall NIRF score.

· A strong SS score reflects:

> o High utilization of sanctioned capacity.
>
> o Balanced mix of UG, PG, and Ph.D. students.
>
> o Healthy doctoral enrollment, strengthening research culture.

**B. Faculty–Student Ratio with emphasis on permanent faculty (FSR)**

· Maximum Marks: 25

· Formula:

> FSR = 25 × \[15 × F/N\]

**Breakdown of Formula Components**

· F: Number of full-time regular faculty members in the institution (previous academic year).

> o Includes: Faculty with Ph.D. or Master's degree.
>
> o Excludes: Faculty with only a Bachelor's degree.
>
> o Contract/Ad-hoc faculty: Counted only if they taught in both semesters of academic year 2022–23.

· N = NT + NP

> o NT: Total sanctioned approved intake for UG and PG programs.
>
> o NP: Total number of enrolled Ph.D. students.
>
> o Represents the student load that needs to be supported.

· Expected Benchmark:

> o Ideal ratio = 1:15 (F/N = 1/15).
>
> o If this benchmark is met, institutions score full 25 marks.

· Penalty for Low Ratios:

> o If F/N \< 1:50, the score is set to zero.
>
> o This prevents institutions with very high student loads and very low faculty strength from gaining any credit.

**Interpretation of the Metric**

· Why FSR Matters:

> o A lower student load per faculty improves academic quality, mentoring, and student engagement.
>
> o Permanent faculty ensures stability, continuity, and stronger research guidance.

· Scoring Implications:

> o Institutions with better than 1:15 ratio (e.g., 1:12 or 1:10) will still get the maximum 25 marks, as it caps at the ideal benchmark.
>
> o Institutions with ratios worse than 1:15 (e.g., 1:20, 1:25) get proportionally lower marks.
>
> o Institutions with extremely poor ratios (worse than 1:50) score zero.

· Data Requirements:

> o Institutions must submit a faculty list in the prescribed format including:
>
> o Faculty name, qualification (Ph.D./Master's), designation.
>
> o Appointment type (permanent/contract/ad-hoc).
>
> o Confirmation of teaching in both semesters.

**Significance of FSR in NIRF**

· Weightage = 25% of TLR → about 7.5% of overall NIRF score.

· It directly influences institutional rank because it reflects teaching quality and resource adequacy.

· Promotes a culture of hiring sufficient permanent, qualified faculty.

**C. Combined Metric for Faculty with Ph.D. and Experience (FQE)**

· Maximum Marks: 20

· Formula:

> FQE = FQ + FE

**1. Faculty with Ph.D. (FQ) – 10 Marks**

· Formula:

> o FQ = 10 × FRA/95, if FRA \< 95%
>
> o FQ = 10, if FRA ≥ 95%

· Where:

> o FRA: Percentage of faculty with Ph.D. (or equivalent qualification) relative to the required faculty strength or actual faculty, whichever is higher.

· Interpretation:

> o If 95% or more faculty hold Ph.D./equivalent → Full 10 marks.
>
> o If less, marks are proportional to % of Ph.D. faculty.
>
> o Ensures institutions prioritize hiring research-qualified faculty.

**2. Faculty Experience (FE) – 10 Marks**

· Defined as:

> FE = 3 × min(3×F1, 1) + 3 × min(3×F2, 1) + 4 × min(3×F3, 1)

· Where:

> o F1: Fraction of faculty with experience up to 8 years.
>
> o F2: Fraction of faculty with experience between 8–15 years.
>
> o F3: Fraction of faculty with experience \> 15 years.

· Rationale:

> o Full marks are awarded if the distribution of experience is balanced (1:1:1 ratio) across junior, mid-level, and senior faculty.
>
> o Prevents skew (e.g., all fresh recruits or all nearing retirement).
>
> o Encourages healthy faculty mix → mentorship, research leadership, and succession planning.

**3. Combined Metric (FQE)**

· Final score is the sum of Ph.D. quality (FQ) and experience distribution (FE).

· Max = 20 Marks.

· Contribution to TLR = 20% of 100 → 6% of overall NIRF score.

**Primary Data Required (Prescribed Format)**

· Faculty List with:

> o Name, Qualification (Ph.D./Equivalent, Master's).
>
> o Appointment Type (Permanent/Contract/Ad-hoc).
>
> o Teaching Experience (Years).
>
> o Department & Designation.

· Used to compute both Ph.D. percentage (FRA) and experience fractions (F1, F2, F3).

**Significance of FQE in NIRF**

· Quality dimension: Rewards institutions with higher % of Ph.D.-qualified faculty.

· Experience dimension: Rewards institutions maintaining balanced faculty mix.

· Ensures both academic qualification and teaching experience are valued in ranking.

**D. Financial Resources and Their Utilisation (FRU)**

· Maximum Marks: 20

· Formula:

> FRU = 5 × f(BC) + 15 × f(BO)

**1. Components of FRU**

***A. BC – Capital Expenditure (5 Marks)***

· Definition:

> o Average annual capital expenditure per student for the last three years.
>
> o Excludes construction of new buildings (to prevent one-time big investments skewing results).

· Examples:

> o Equipment purchases, laboratory upgrades, library resources, ICT investments.

· Significance:

> o Captures how much an institution invests in academic infrastructure.
>
> o Rewards institutions that regularly upgrade facilities.

***B. BO – Operational (Recurring) Expenditure (15 Marks)***

· Definition:

> o Average annual operational expenditure per student for the last three years.
>
> o Excludes hostel maintenance and allied services.

· Examples:

> o Faculty salaries, research support, lab consumables, library subscriptions, student services.

· Significance:

> o Indicates the institution's ability to sustain day-to-day academic quality.
>
> o Operational spending is weighted higher (15 marks) because it reflects continuous support to students and faculty.

**2. Formula Application**

· f(BC) and f(BO): Functions designed by NIRF to normalize expenditure values.

> o Higher per-student spending = higher score (up to full marks).
>
> o Institutions are benchmarked against peer spending levels.

· Weight Distribution:

> o Capital Expenditure (BC) = 5 marks
>
> o Operational Expenditure (BO) = 15 marks

· This ensures balance between infrastructure investment and sustainability of academic operations.

**3. Primary Data Requirement**

· Institutions must submit audited figures in the prescribed NIRF format for each of the last three years:

> o Total capital expenditure (excluding building construction)
>
> o Total operational expenditure (excluding hostels)
>
> o Total student strength (to normalize per student)

· NIRF uses these figures to compute average per-student values before applying the FRU formula.

**4. Significance of FRU in NIRF**

· FRU contributes 20 marks to TLR → about 6% of the overall NIRF score.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Invest consistently in academic resources.
>
> o Maintain financial accountability and sustainability.
>
> o Balance long-term capital growth with short-term academic needs.

**E. Online Education (OE): Online Completion of Syllabus & Exams and SWAYAM**

· Maximum Marks: 10

· Formula:

> OE = f(OE)

· Here, f(OE) is a function designed by NIRF to normalize the score based on adoption and effectiveness of online education practices.

**1. What OE Measures**

· Online Completion of Syllabus:

> o Extent to which institutions adopt online platforms for teaching-learning.
>
> o Includes regular classroom teaching supported with LMS (Learning Management Systems), video lectures, and virtual classrooms.

· Online Examinations:

> o Ability of institutions to conduct assessments and exams online.
>
> o Includes proctored exams, digital evaluation, and secure online testing systems.

· Adoption of SWAYAM & MOOCs:

> o Number of courses offered and completed by students through SWAYAM/NPTEL or other MOOCs.
>
> o Percentage of faculty creating courses on SWAYAM is also considered.

**2. Data Requirements (Prescribed Format)**

Institutions must submit:

· Number of courses completed online by students.

· Percentage of syllabus delivered through online platforms.

· Number of students registered and certified in SWAYAM/NPTEL MOOCs.

· Faculty contributions to MOOCs/SWAYAM (course creators, instructors).

· Online exams conducted (count, coverage, student participation).

**3. Scoring Logic (f(OE))**

· NIRF assigns scores based on adoption level compared to benchmarks.

· Institutions with extensive online adoption (≥70% students using MOOCs, frequent online exams, faculty contributing to SWAYAM) approach full 10 marks.

· Institutions with minimal adoption receive proportionally lower scores.

**4. Significance of OE in NIRF**

· Weightage = 10 Marks (out of 100 TLR) → contributes about 3% of the overall NIRF score.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Blend traditional and digital teaching methods.
>
> o Promote flexible, technology-driven learning ecosystems.
>
> o Align with Digital India and NEP 2020 reforms promoting MOOCs and blended learning.

**F. Combined Metric for Multiple Entry/Exit, Indian Knowledge System, and Regional Languages (MIR)**

· Maximum Marks: 5

· Formula:

> MIR = 2 × MEE + 2 × IKS + 1 × RL

**1. Components of MIR**

***A. Multiple Entry/Exit (MEE) – Weight 2 Marks***

· What it means:

> o Implementation of Multiple Entry and Exit options as recommended in NEP 2020.
>
> o Students can exit after 1, 2, or 3 years with certificate, diploma, or degree respectively.
>
> o Re-entry possible using Academic Bank of Credits (ABC).

· Why it matters:

> o Promotes flexibility in higher education.
>
> o Allows students to learn at their own pace and rejoin without losing progress.

***B. Indian Knowledge System (IKS) – Weight 2 Marks***

· What it means:

> o Offering courses/programs related to Indian Knowledge Systems (e.g., Vedic mathematics, Yoga, Ayurveda, Sanskrit, Indian philosophy, traditional sciences).

· Why it matters:

> o Preserves and promotes cultural heritage.
>
> o Integrates ancient Indian knowledge with modern higher education.

***C. Regional Languages (RL) – Weight 1 Mark***

· What it means:

> o Offering courses in regional languages in line with NEP 2020.
>
> o Examples: B.Tech courses in Hindi/Tamil, humanities subjects in Telugu/Bengali, etc.

· Why it matters:

> o Increases accessibility for students from diverse linguistic backgrounds.
>
> o Promotes inclusivity and reduces language barriers in higher education.

**2. Scoring**

· Institutions implementing all three (MEE + IKS + RL) can score full 5 marks.

· If partially implemented, marks are awarded proportionally (e.g., only MEE + RL → 3 marks).

**3. Data Requirements (Prescribed Format)**

· MEE: Policy documents, Academic Bank of Credits integration records.

· IKS: List of courses/programs introduced under IKS.

· RL: Course lists offered in regional languages with student enrollment data.

**4. Significance of MIR in NIRF**

· Though only 5 marks, MIR highlights alignment with NEP 2020 reforms.

· It encourages institutions to:

> o Be flexible and student-friendly (MEE).
>
> o Preserve and promote Indian culture (IKS).
>
> o Embrace linguistic diversity (RL).

**30. Research and Professional Practice (RP) – NIRF 2024**

· Total Marks: 100

· Ranking Weight: 0.30 (30% of overall NIRF score)

· Overall Formula:

> RP = PU(30) + QP(30) + IPR(15) + FPPP(15) + PSDG(10)

**Component Metrics of RP**

| **Code**  | **Sub-Parameter**                                                             | **Marks** | **Explanation**                                                                                                                      |
|-----------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| PU        | Combined Metric for Publications                                              | 30        | Measures research output volume, based on publications indexed in Scopus, Web of Science, and other global databases                 |
| QP        | Combined Metric for Quality of Publications                                   | 30        | Assesses impact through citations, field-weighted citation index, h-index, SJR, and SNIP values                                      |
| IPR       | IPR and Patents: Published & Granted                                          | 15        | Rewards institutions for patents filed, published, granted, and commercialized                                                       |
| FPPP      | Footprint of Projects, Professional Practice & Executive Development Programs | 15        | Evaluates sponsored projects, consultancy work, industry partnerships, and executive training programs                               |
| PSDG      | Combined Metric for Publications & Citations in SDGs                          | 10        | Captures contribution of institutional research aligned with UN Sustainable Development Goals (e.g., climate change, health, energy) |
| **Total** | **–**                                                                         | **100**   | **Weighted contribution = 30% of NIRF Score**                                                                                        |

**Significance of RP**

· RP accounts for 30% of the NIRF ranking score, making it equally important as TLR.

· It focuses on both quantity and quality of research, as well as innovation, patents, and societal impact.

· Sub-parameters encourage institutions to:

> o Produce more globally recognized publications.
>
> o Focus on quality over quantity.
>
> o Strengthen industry collaboration and consultancy.
>
> o Align with sustainable development priorities.

**A. Combined Metric for Publications (PU)**

· Maximum Marks: 30

· Formula:

> PU = 30 × f(P/FRQ)

**1. Formula Components**

· P (Publications):

> o Weighted number of research publications attributed to the institution.
>
> o Obtained from suitable third-party sources like Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed (for medical), or Indian Citation Index.
>
> o “Weighted” means publications may be adjusted by type, quality, or co-authorship.

· FRQ (Faculty Requirement Quantity):

> o Defined as the maximum of:

1\. The nominal number of faculty members required to maintain FSR = 1:15.

2\. The actual number of faculty available at the institution.

> o Ensures fair normalization: large institutions need proportionately more publications to score high.

· f(P/FRQ):

> o A scaling function designed by NIRF.
>
> o Computes score relative to per-faculty publication productivity.
>
> o Ensures institutions with more faculty are expected to contribute proportionately more publications.

**2. Data Source**

· P (Publications) is taken directly from third-party sources:

> o Scopus, Web of Science (WoS), PubMed, Indian Citation Index.
>
> o Institutions cannot self-report publication numbers (to prevent exaggeration).

· Cross-checking ensures transparency and authenticity.

**3. Rationale for PU**

· Rewards institutions with higher research productivity per faculty.

· Prevents bias where a large institution inflates scores by sheer faculty numbers.

· Benchmarks institutions against a normalized expectation (FSR = 1:15).

**4. Significance in RP**

· PU carries 30 marks → 9% of total NIRF score.

· Forms the core of the Research & Professional Practice (RP) parameter.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Publish consistently in indexed, peer-reviewed journals.
>
> o Strengthen research culture across all departments.
>
> o Track faculty productivity rather than raw numbers.

**B. Combined Metric for Quality of Publications (QP)**

· Maximum Marks: 30

· Formula:

> QP = 15 × f(CC/FRQ) + 15 × f(TOP25P/P)

**1. Formula Components**

· CC (Citation Count):

> o Total number of citations received by the institution's publications over the previous 3 years.
>
> o Higher CC → greater impact and recognition of research.

· P (Publications):

> o Weighted number of publications, as computed for the PU metric.
>
> o Acts as the denominator in the second part of the formula.

· TOP25P (Top 25% Citations):

> o Number of citations belonging to the top 25 percentile globally, averaged over 3 years.
>
> o Highlights high-quality, influential research.

· FRQ (Faculty Requirement Quantity):

> o Same definition as in PU:
>
> o Maximum of (faculty required at FSR = 1:15 OR actual faculty).
>
> o Used to normalize citation count per faculty.

· f(…):

> o Normalization function applied by NIRF to scale values into marks.
>
> o Ensures fairness across institutions of different sizes.

**2. Interpretation of Formula**

· First Term (15 × f(CC/FRQ))

> o Measures average citations per faculty strength (normalized).
>
> o Encourages institutions to focus on quality research output that attracts citations.

· Second Term (15 × f(TOP25P/P))

> o Measures the proportion of highly cited papers among total publications.
>
> o Ensures that institutions are rewarded for globally recognized impactful research rather than just volume.

**3. Data Sources**

· Third-party citation databases:

> o Scopus
>
> o Web of Science (WoS)
>
> o PubMed (for medical research)
>
> o Indian Citation Index

· Institutions cannot self-report citation data — it is directly extracted from trusted sources.

**4. Significance of QP**

· Total Weight = 30 marks → 9% of overall NIRF score.

· Together with PU, QP makes up 60% of RP (Research & Professional Practice).

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Publish in high-quality journals.
>
> o Collaborate internationally to improve citation impact.
>
> o Target research areas of global visibility and relevance.

**NIRF 2024 – PU & QP Formula Table**

| **Metric**                   | **Formula**                            | **Max Marks** | **What It Captures**                                                                                                    | **Data Source**                                                                        |
|------------------------------|----------------------------------------|---------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| PU (Publications)            | PU = 30 × f(P/FRQ)                     | 30            | Volume of research publications normalized per faculty strength (ensures fairness between small and large institutions) | Third-party sources (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, Indian Citation Index)            |
| QP (Quality of Publications) | QP = 15 × f(CC/FRQ) + 15 × f(TOP25P/P) | 30            | Research quality: (a) citation count per faculty, (b) proportion of highly cited papers (top 25%)                       | Third-party citation databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, Indian Citation Index) |

**Quick Insights:**

· PU = Quantity focus (how many publications per faculty).

· QP = Quality focus (citations and high-impact recognition).

· Together, they account for 60 marks out of RP's 100, i.e., 18% of the overall NIRF score.

**C. IPR and Patents: Published & Granted (IPR)**

· Maximum Marks: 15

· Formula:

> IPR = IPG + IPP

**1. Sub-components of IPR**

***A. Patents Granted (IPG) – 10 Marks***

· Formula:

> IPG = 10 × f(PG)

· PG: Number of patents granted in the last three years.

· Significance:

> o Granted patents represent validated innovation.
>
> o Higher weight (10 marks) given since patents granted are more valuable than just filed or published ones.

***B. Patents Published (IPP) – 5 Marks***

· Formula:

> IPP = 5 × f(PP)

· PP: Number of patents published in the last three years.

· Significance:

> o Patent applications published are an early indicator of innovation activity.
>
> o Lower weight (5 marks) since not all published patents lead to grants.

**2. Data Source**

· Third-party patent databases:

> o Indian Patent Office (IPO)
>
> o WIPO (World Intellectual Property Organization)
>
> o USPTO (United States Patent & Trademark Office)
>
> o EPO (European Patent Office)

· Institutions cannot self-report → ensures transparency.

**3. Rationale**

· Encourages institutions to:

> o File and publish patents → fosters a culture of innovation.
>
> o Secure patent grants → indicates practical applicability and recognition of intellectual property.

· Balances innovation pipeline (publications) with successful outcomes (grants).

**4. Significance in RP**

· IPR = 15 marks → 4.5% of overall NIRF score.

· Supports India's research-to-innovation ecosystem.

· Incentivizes institutions to move beyond publications into applied, industry-relevant research outputs.

**NIRF 2024 – IPR Formula Table**

| **Metric**              | **Formula**      | **Max Marks** | **What It Captures**                                                                                              | **Data Source**                                          |
|-------------------------|------------------|---------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| IPG (Patents Granted)   | IPG = 10 × f(PG) | 10            | Recognizes number of patents granted in the last 3 years; validated innovation with real-world applicability      | Third-party patent databases (IPO, WIPO, USPTO, EPO)     |
| IPP (Patents Published) | IPP = 5 × f(PP)  | 5             | Counts number of patents published in the last 3 years; early-stage indicator of innovation activity              | Third-party patent databases (IPO, WIPO, USPTO, EPO)     |
| IPR (Total)             | IPR = IPG + IPP  | 15            | Combined metric rewarding institutions for both innovation pipeline (published) and successful outcomes (granted) | Verified through national & international patent offices |

**Quick Insights:**

· Patents Granted (10 marks) carry more weight than Patents Published (5 marks).

· Data is sourced externally, preventing manipulation.

· Encourages institutions to move from filing to securing patents, strengthening technology transfer.

**D. Footprint of Projects, Professional Practice & Executive Development Programs (FPPP)**

· Maximum Marks: 15

· Formula:

> FPPP = FPR + FPC + EDP/MDP

**1. Sub-components of FPPP**

***A. Research Funding (FPR) – 5 Marks***

· Formula:

> FPR = 5 × f(RF)

· RF (Research Funding):

> o Average annual research funding earnings at the institution level over the last 3 years.
>
> o Includes funding from government agencies (DST, DBT, ICSSR, UGC, AICTE, etc.) and international agencies.

· Significance:

> o Captures institution's ability to attract competitive research grants.
>
> o Reflects research capability and recognition.

***B. Consultancy (FPC) – 5 Marks***

· Formula:

> FPC = 5 × f(CF)

· CF (Consultancy Funding):

> o Average annual consultancy earnings (actual receipts) over the last 3 years.
>
> o Typically from industry partnerships and problem-solving projects.

· Significance:

> o Indicates institution's strength in industry linkages and applied research.
>
> o Encourages knowledge transfer to industry and society.

***C. Executive Development / Management Development Programs (EDP/MDP) – 5 Marks***

· Formula:

> EDP/MDP = 5 × f(EP)

· EP (Executive/Management Program Earnings):

> o Average annual income generated from executive development or management development programs (short-term, skill-based training) in the last 3 years.

· Significance:

> o Reflects institutional engagement in lifelong learning and professional training.
>
> o Promotes direct contribution to capacity building for professionals.

**2. Data Source**

· Primary Data: Institutions must provide audited financial figures for RF, CF, and EP in the prescribed format.

· NIRF may cross-verify with funding agencies, consultancy agreements, and audited accounts.

**3. Significance of FPPP in NIRF**

· FPPP contributes 15 marks (4.5% of overall NIRF score).

· Strengthens the research-to-industry and academia-to-profession pipeline.

· Rewards institutions that:

> o Secure research grants (FPR).
>
> o Deliver consultancy to industries (FPC).
>
> o Conduct executive/management training (EDP/MDP).

**E. Combined Metric for Publications & Citations in SDGs (PSDGs)**

· Maximum Marks: 10

· Formula:

> PSDGs = 5 × f(PUSDG/FRQ) + 5 × f(CISDG/FRQ)

**1. Formula Components**

· PUSDG (Publications in SDGs):

> o Weighted number of research publications aligned with UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).
>
> o Examples:
>
> o SDG 3 (Health): Public health, vaccines, epidemiology.
>
> o SDG 4 (Education): Pedagogical innovation, digital learning.
>
> o SDG 7 (Energy): Renewable energy, clean technologies.
>
> o SDG 13 (Climate Action): Climate change, sustainability research.

· CISDG (Citations in SDGs):

> o Total citation count of SDG-related publications over the previous three years.
>
> o Indicates influence and impact of sustainability-related research.

· FRQ (Faculty Requirement Quantity):

> o Maximum of:
>
> o Nominal faculty required at FSR = 1:15, or
>
> o Actual faculty available.
>
> o Used for normalization, ensuring per-faculty productivity comparison.

· f(…):

> o Normalization function designed by NIRF to scale values fairly.

**2. Interpretation of the Metric**

· First Term (5 × f(PUSDG/FRQ))

> o Captures per-faculty research output specifically aligned with SDGs.

· Second Term (5 × f(CISDG/FRQ))

> o Captures per-faculty citation impact of SDG-related research.

· Balance:

> o Both volume (publications) and impact (citations) are rewarded equally (5 marks each).

**3. Data Source**

· Directly collected from third-party databases:

> o Scopus
>
> o Web of Science (WoS)
>
> o PubMed (for health-related SDGs)
>
> o Other citation databases used globally.

· Institutions cannot self-report these figures.

**4. Significance of PSDGs in NIRF**

· Total weight = 10 marks → contributes 3% of the overall NIRF score.

· Promotes alignment of higher education research with global sustainable development priorities.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Publish in areas with social and environmental impact.
>
> o Strengthen interdisciplinary research (health, energy, climate, education).
>
> o Gain international visibility in addressing grand global challenges.

**31. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – NIRF 2024**

· Total Marks: 100

· Ranking Weight: 0.20 (20% of overall NIRF score)

· Overall Formula:

> GO = GUE(60) + GPHD(40)

**Component Metrics of GO**

| **Code**  | **Sub-Parameter**                             | **Marks** | **Explanation**                                                                                                                            |
|-----------|-----------------------------------------------|-----------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| GUE       | Metric for University Examinations            | 60        | Measures success of students in university-level examinations, including pass percentages, timely graduations, and progression rates       |
| GPHD      | Metric for Number of Ph.D. Students Graduated | 40        | Captures the research strength of the institution by measuring the number of doctoral students successfully graduating in the last 3 years |
| **Total** | **–**                                         | **100**   | **Weighted contribution = 20% of NIRF Score**                                                                                              |

**Significance of GO**

· Weightage = 20% of total NIRF score.

· Ensures institutions are evaluated based on student success outcomes at both:

> o General academic level → GUE (60%)
>
> o Advanced research level → GPHD (40%)

· Promotes accountability in:

> o Ensuring high pass rates and timely graduations.
>
> o Supporting strong doctoral research programs.

**A. Metric for University Examinations (GUE)**

· Maximum Marks: 60

· Formula:

> GUE = 60 × min(Ng/80, 1)

**1. Formula Components**

· Ng (Graduation Success Rate):

> o Defined as the percentage of students (relative to approved intake) who successfully pass the university examinations in the stipulated time for their enrolled program.
>
> o Averaged over the previous three years.
>
> o Example: If approved intake = 100 and 85 students graduate on time, Ng = 85%.

· Scaling Function:

> o If Ng \< 80%, score = proportional fraction of 60.
>
> o If Ng ≥ 80%, the institution gets full 60 marks.
>
> o Ensures that institutions cannot claim additional benefit beyond the 80% benchmark.

**2. Data Source**

· Primary data must be provided by the institution in prescribed format, including:

> o Approved intake for each program (UG/PG).
>
> o Number of students graduating on time in each of the last 3 years.

· NIRF cross-checks data with university records and examination results.

**3. Interpretation**

· Institutions with strong academic processes (teaching, support, mentoring) will have high on-time graduation rates.

· Benchmark is set at 80% pass rate → considered a good indicator of quality.

· Institutions performing below this benchmark lose marks proportionally.

**4. Significance in GO**

· GUE contributes 60 marks out of 100 in GO → 12% of overall NIRF score.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Ensure timely completion of academic programs.
>
> o Focus on student retention, success, and progression.
>
> o Strengthen academic support systems to minimize dropouts.

**B. Metric for Number of Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD)**

· Maximum Marks: 40

· Formula:

> GPHD = 40 × f(Nphd)

**1. Formula Components**

· Nphd (Ph.D. Graduates):

> o Average number of Ph.D. students successfully awarded degrees over the previous 3 academic years.
>
> o Data to be reported in absolute numbers, then normalized through NIRF's scaling function f(Nphd).

· f(Nphd):

> o A normalization function applied by NIRF to map the average number of Ph.D. graduates into a score out of 40.
>
> o Ensures fairness by benchmarking against national distributions of doctoral outputs.

**2. Data Source**

· Institutions must provide primary data in prescribed format:

> o Number of Ph.D. degrees awarded per year.
>
> o Certified via Annual Reports / Convocation Reports.

· NIRF may cross-check with:

> o UGC, AIU, institutional convocation records, or other official documentation.

**3. Interpretation**

· A higher average number of Ph.D. graduates → stronger institutional capacity for research, supervision, and innovation.

· Rewards institutions with:

> o Robust doctoral programs.
>
> o Active research faculty.
>
> o Strong infrastructure for advanced research training.

**4. Significance in GO**

· GPHD contributes 40 marks out of 100 in GO → 8% of overall NIRF score.

· Balances GUE (student graduation success at UG/PG level) with research-oriented outputs (Ph.D. completions).

· Supports India's research ecosystem by recognizing institutions producing highly trained researchers.

**32. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – NIRF 2024**

· Total Marks: 100

· Ranking Weight: 0.10 (10% of overall NIRF score)

· Overall Formula:

> OI = RD(30) + WD(30) + ESCS(20) + PCS(20)

**Component Metrics of OI**

| **Code**  | **Sub-Parameter**                                                         | **Marks** | **Explanation**                                                                                                           |
|-----------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| RD        | Percentage of Students from Other States / Countries (Regional Diversity) | 30        | Captures geographic diversity by measuring the proportion of students enrolled from outside the home state or from abroad |
| WD        | Percentage of Women (Women Diversity)                                     | 30        | Reflects gender diversity across students, faculty, and leadership roles                                                  |
| ESCS      | Economically & Socially Challenged Students                               | 20        | Measures access, enrollment, and institutional support (scholarships, fee waivers) for disadvantaged groups               |
| PCS       | Facilities for Physically Challenged Students                             | 20        | Evaluates infrastructure, learning support, and accessibility for differently-abled students                              |
| **Total** | **–**                                                                     | **100**   | **Weighted contribution = 10% of NIRF Score**                                                                             |

**Significance of OI**

· Ensures rankings capture not just academic and research excellence, but also an institution's social responsibility.

· Encourages higher education institutions to:

> o Build a geographically diverse student body (RD).
>
> o Ensure gender balance (WD).
>
> o Provide equitable opportunities to marginalized groups (ESCS).
>
> o Develop inclusive infrastructure for differently-abled students (PCS).

**A. Percentage of Students from Other States / Countries (Regional Diversity – RD)**

· Maximum Marks: 30

· Formula:

> RD = 25 × (fraction of students from other states) + 5 × (fraction of students from other countries)

**1. Formula Components**

· Fraction of Students from Other States

> o Percentage of total enrolled students whose domicile / qualifying examination is from a state other than where the institution is located.
>
> o Weighted at 25 marks.

· Fraction of Students from Other Countries

> o Percentage of international students enrolled in the institution.
>
> o Weighted at 5 marks.

**2. Data Source**

· Primary data to be provided in the prescribed format, including:

> o Total student enrollment (UG, PG, Ph.D.).
>
> o Number of students from other Indian states.
>
> o Number of students from foreign countries.

· Data may be cross-verified with:

> o Admission records, passport/nationality data (for international students), and enrollment registers.

**3. Interpretation**

· Institutions with higher geographic diversity get higher marks.

· Example:

> o If 30% of students are from outside the home state and 10% are foreign students:
>
> o RD = 25 × 0.30 + 5 × 0.10 = 7.5 + 0.5 = 8.0

· Full marks require a strong mix of national and international students.

**4. Significance in OI**

· RD contributes 30% of the OI score, i.e., 3% of the overall NIRF score.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Attract students beyond their immediate region.
>
> o Build a pan-India and global reputation.
>
> o Create a culturally diverse learning environment.

**B. Percentage of Women (Women Diversity – WD)**

· Maximum Marks: 30

· Formula:

> WD = 15 × (NWS/50) + 15 × (NWF/20)

**1. Formula Components**

· NWS (Women Students):

> o Percentage of female students enrolled in the institution.
>
> o Full 15 marks awarded when 50% enrollment is female.
>
> o If NWS \< 50%, score is proportional.

· NWF (Women Faculty):

> o Percentage of female faculty members, including women in senior administrative roles (e.g., HoDs, Deans, Directors).
>
> o Full 15 marks awarded when 20% or more faculty are women.
>
> o If NWF \< 20%, score is proportional.

**2. Expectations & Benchmarks**

· 50% women students → gender balance in the student body.

· 20% women faculty → adequate gender representation among academic and leadership staff.

· Institutions reaching these thresholds get maximum score.

**3. Data Source**

· Primary data must be reported in prescribed format, including:

> o Total student enrollment (male/female split).
>
> o Total faculty count (male/female split).
>
> o Number of women in leadership roles.

· Verification via admission records, faculty HR lists, and administrative rosters.

**4. Interpretation**

· Encourages gender diversity in both classrooms and faculty positions.

· Recognizes institutions that actively promote women in leadership roles.

· Example Calculation:

> o If 40% students are women (NWS = 40) and 15% faculty are women (NWF = 15):
>
> o WD = 15 × (40/50) + 15 × (15/20) = 12 + 11.25 = 23.25

**5. Significance in OI**

· WD contributes 30 marks out of 100 in OI → 3% of overall NIRF score.

· Promotes gender equity in higher education, in line with national and global commitments.

**C. Economically and Socially Challenged Students (ESCS)**

· Maximum Marks: 20

· Formula:

> ESCS = 20 × f(Nesc)

**1. Formula Components**

· Nesc (Economically & Socially Challenged Students):

> o Percentage of students receiving full tuition fee reimbursement from the institution (or via scholarships routed through the institution).
>
> o Includes students supported through:
>
> o Institutional scholarships.
>
> o State/Central Government fee reimbursement schemes.
>
> o Endowments or private trust scholarships administered by the institution.

· f(Nesc):

> o Normalization function designed by NIRF to map % of beneficiaries into a score out of 20.
>
> o Institutions with higher percentage of ESCS students score closer to the maximum.

**2. Data Source**

· Primary data must be provided in the prescribed format:

> o Total number of enrolled students.
>
> o Number of ESCS students receiving full tuition fee reimbursement.

· Verified using:

> o Institutional scholarship records.
>
> o State/Central government reimbursement scheme documents.
>
> o Audit reports of financial assistance distribution.

**3. Interpretation**

· High ESCS scores reflect institutional commitment to equity and social responsibility.

· Encourages:

> o Admission of students from economically weaker sections.
>
> o Robust scholarship and financial aid mechanisms.

· Example:

> o If 15% of students receive full reimbursement →
>
> o ESCS = 20 × f(0.15)
>
> o Where f(0.15) is scaled relative to benchmarks defined by NIRF.

**4. Significance in OI**

· ESCS contributes 20 marks out of 100 in OI → 2% of overall NIRF score.

· Ensures institutions are not only inclusive in terms of geographic and gender diversity, but also socio-economic inclusivity.

**D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS)**

· Maximum Marks: 20

· Formula/Scoring Rule:

> o Full 20 marks if the institution provides all prescribed facilities.
>
> o Partial marks awarded proportional to the number of facilities available.

**1. Prescribed Facilities (Indicative List)**

To qualify for full score, an institution must provide comprehensive, accessible infrastructure and support such as:

· Infrastructure Accessibility

> o Ramps and elevators in academic/administrative buildings.
>
> o Wheelchair access to classrooms, labs, libraries, hostels.

· Assistive Learning Tools

> o Screen readers, braille materials, audiobooks, magnifiers.
>
> o Special computer labs with accessibility software.

· Dedicated Support Services

> o Specially trained staff for assistance.
>
> o Sign language interpreters, scribes for exams.
>
> o Dedicated grievance redressal/helpdesk for differently-abled students.

· Institutional Policy

> o Reservation in admissions.
>
> o Fee concessions/scholarships.
>
> o Inclusive learning and assessment policies.

**2. Data Source**

· Primary Data:

> o Institutions must provide verifiable responses in the prescribed format.
>
> o Evidence includes:
>
> o Infrastructure photographs.
>
> o Procurement/maintenance records of assistive tools.
>
> o Student beneficiary lists.

· Verification Basis:

> o On-site inspection (if required).
>
> o Annual reports and compliance documents.

**3. Scoring Approach**

· 20 marks: If all prescribed facilities are in place and verifiable.

· Proportional marks: If only partial facilities are available (e.g., ramps but no braille resources).

· 0 marks: If no significant facilities exist.

**4. Significance in OI**

· PCS contributes 20 marks out of 100 in OI → 2% of overall NIRF score.

· Encourages institutions to adopt inclusive practices and ensure equal opportunities for differently-abled students.

**33. Perception (PR) – NIRF 2024**

· Total Marks: 100

· Ranking Weight: 0.10 (10% of overall NIRF score)

· Overall Formula:

> PR = PR(100)

**Component Metric**

| **Code** | **Sub-Parameter**                           | **Marks** | **Explanation**                                                                                                                                       |
|----------|---------------------------------------------|-----------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| PR       | Peer Perception: Employers & Academic Peers | 100       | Measures how the institution is perceived by external stakeholders, including employers, industry leaders, academic peers, and research collaborators |

**1. Sources of Peer Perception**

· Employers / Industry Leaders

> o Feedback on graduate employability.
>
> o Hiring patterns and reputation in the job market.

· Academic Peers

> o Reputation among universities, research institutions, and faculty networks.
>
> o Collaborations, faculty exchanges, and co-authored research.

· Other Stakeholders

> o Professional bodies, accreditation agencies.
>
> o Alumni perceptions in leadership roles.

**2. Data Collection Method**

· Survey-based evaluation:

> o NIRF collects perception scores through structured surveys sent to:
>
> o Employers/Industry representatives.
>
> o Academic experts nationally and internationally.
>
> o Institutions cannot influence or alter responses.

· Transparency safeguard:

> o If any institution engages in canvassing or promotional emails during the survey phase, all votes received will be set to zero (as per NIRF guidelines).

**3. Special Rule for Universities**

· For universities, the PR parameter is split:

> o 70% weight → Peer Perception (Employers & Academic Peers)
>
> o 30% weight → Accreditation status (e.g., NAAC/NBA scores, international accreditations).

**4. Significance in NIRF**

· PR contributes 100 marks = 10% of overall NIRF score.

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Build strong employer networks.
>
> o Foster academic collaborations.
>
> o Maintain quality accreditations.
>
> o Strengthen alumni relations for visibility.

**A. Peer Perception: Employers & Academic Peers (PR)**

· Maximum Marks: 100

· Scoring Basis: Entirely based on survey feedback collected from external stakeholders.

**1. Who Provides Feedback?**

· Employers & Industry Professionals

> o CEOs, HR heads, recruiters, and hiring managers from reputed organizations.
>
> o Represent a wide range of sectors (IT, healthcare, manufacturing, finance, etc.).

· Academic Peers

> o Professors, deans, research leaders from national and international universities.
>
> o Experts familiar with academic collaborations, student quality, and faculty achievements.

· Other Stakeholders (occasionally included):

> o Professional bodies and accreditation agencies.
>
> o Alumni in leadership roles.

**2. Survey Process**

· Survey Coverage:

> o Conducted over a large and diverse sample of respondents.
>
> o Ensures representation from different regions, sectors, and disciplines.

· Survey Design:

> o Respondents asked to indicate their preference and reputation score for graduates of different institutions.
>
> o Uses structured questionnaires with ranking/score options.

· Survey Integrity:

> o Institutions are not allowed to canvass or influence respondents.
>
> o If canvassing emails are detected, all votes for that institution are nullified.

**3. List Preparation**

· Comprehensive respondent list is curated by NIRF:

> o Covers employers, professionals, academics across India and abroad.
>
> o Lists are updated periodically to maintain fairness and prevent bias.

· Dynamic Update:

> o Ensures that newer/emerging institutions are represented.
>
> o Ensures surveyors represent current industry and academic landscapes.

**4. Significance**

· PR contributes 100 marks (10% of overall NIRF score).

· Institutions with strong:

> o Industry reputation (high employability, skill readiness).
>
> o Academic reputation (research collaborations, peer recognition).

· Tend to score high in this parameter.

**34. NIRF 2024 Ranking Framework for the Engineering Discipline**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Measures quality of teaching infrastructure, faculty strength, and resource utilization.

· **A. Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Rewards institutions with strong student enrollment, balanced UG/PG/PhD ratio.
>
> o Encourages doctoral research presence.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

> o Evaluates adequacy of faculty strength relative to student intake.
>
> o Permanent faculty emphasized. Best ratio = 1:15.

· **C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Measures faculty quality via PhD qualification and teaching/research experience.
>
> o Balanced experience distribution (junior/mid/senior) gets maximum credit.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

> o Tracks per-student capital and operational expenditure over 3 years.
>
> o Rewards sustainable spending on labs, libraries, ICT, salaries, and research.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Assesses research productivity, quality, patents, and industry engagement.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 35 Marks**

> o Weighted research publications from Scopus, Web of Science, etc.
>
> o Normalized by faculty strength (per-faculty productivity).

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 40 Marks**

> o Citations, top 25% papers, h-index, FWCI, journal quality indices.
>
> o Rewards global impact and recognition.

· **C. Patents Published & Granted (IPR) – 15 Marks**

> o Encourages applied innovation and technology transfer.
>
> o Granted patents weighted higher than published.

· **D. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 10 Marks**

> o Measures consultancy revenue, sponsored research, and executive training programs.
>
> o Reflects industry linkage and applied problem-solving.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Captures student success in examinations, placements, higher studies, and research.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

> o Percentage of students placed or admitted to higher studies within a year of graduation.
>
> o Reflects employability and career progression.

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 15 Marks**

> o Proportion of students graduating on time (benchmark 80%).

· **C. Median Salary (GMS) – 25 Marks**

> o Median annual salary of placed students.
>
> o Normalized across sectors for fairness.

· **D. Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 20 Marks**

> o Average Ph.D. completions over 3 years.
>
> o Encourages research output and doctoral supervision.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Assesses diversity, equity, and accessibility.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Proportion of students from other states/countries.
>
> o International presence adds extra credit.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Women students (50% benchmark).
>
> o Women faculty & leadership roles (20% benchmark).

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Students receiving full fee reimbursements/scholarships.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Infrastructure (ramps, elevators, ICT tools) + inclusive services.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures reputation capital via surveys.

· **A. Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

> o Survey feedback from employers, industry leaders, academic peers, and accreditation bodies (for universities, accreditation counts for 30%).
>
> o Captures external validation of graduate quality and institutional reputation.

**Overall Impact in NIRF 2024 (Engineering)**

· TLR (30%) + RP (30%) = Core Academic & Research Strength.

· GO (20%) = Student Success & Employability.

· OI (10%) = Diversity & Inclusion.

· PR (10%) = Reputation & Perception.

**Key Differences**

· TLR: Engineering assigns more weight to FSR (30 vs 25) and FRU (30 vs 20), while excluding OE (10) and MIR (5) sub-metrics.

· RP: Engineering gives higher weight to PU (35 vs 30) and QP (40 vs 30), but removes PSDGs (10).

· GO: Engineering framework expands into placements (GPH: 40) and median salary (GMS: 25) in addition to GUE and GPHD, unlike Overall which only includes GUE & GPHD.

· OI & PR: Both remain the same across Overall and Engineering.

**35. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH)**

**Formula**

· GPH = 40 × f(OF)

> o OF (Outcome Fraction) = (Placed % + Higher Studies %) of students graduating in stipulated time over the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function used by NIRF to adjust across disciplines and institution sizes.
>
> o Cap = 100% (cannot exceed full score).

**What It Captures**

1\. **Employability (Placement %)**

> o Measures how many graduates secure jobs through on-campus or verified off-campus placements.
>
> o Reflects industry readiness and institution's placement ecosystem.

2\. **Academic Progression (Higher Studies %)**

> o Tracks students who pursue PG, PhD, or overseas studies immediately after graduation.
>
> o Includes students clearing national/international competitive exams with admission evidence.

3\. **Entrepreneurship / Govt. Exams (sometimes included under Higher Studies)**

> o Students starting registered ventures, joining family businesses with proof, or joining civil services.

**Scoring Example**

· Eligible Graduates = 1000

· Placed = 600 → 60%

· Higher Studies = 200 → 20%

· Outcome Fraction (OF) = 60% + 20% = 80%

· If NIRF's normalization = linear (illustrative), then:

> o GPH = 40 × 0.80 = 32 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise placement records, offer letters, salary packages.
>
> o Higher studies admission proofs, fee receipts, or bonafide letters.
>
> o Opt-out records (students not participating in placement drives).

· Verification Sources:

> o Placement cell reports, employer HR letters, admission letters, official portals.

**Why It Matters**

· Indicates how well the institution prepares students for jobs and advanced education.

· Connects academics with real-world outcomes.

· Encourages institutions to balance industry employability with academic progression.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Year-wise Placement Records**

> o Institution must provide placement statistics for the past 3 years.
>
> o Data required:
>
> ▪ Number of students eligible for placement.
>
> ▪ Number of students placed.
>
> ▪ Offer letters (company-wise, student-wise).
>
> ▪ Salary packages (minimum, maximum, median, average).

· **Higher Studies Admission Proofs**

> o Data on students who opted for higher education after graduation (UG/PG).
>
> o Acceptable proofs:
>
> ▪ Admission/offer letters from universities.
>
> ▪ Fee receipts.
>
> ▪ Bonafide letters from admitting institutions.
>
> o Must be student-wise and year-wise.

· **Opt-out Records**

> o List of students who chose not to participate in campus placements.
>
> o May include:
>
> ▪ Self-employment/entrepreneurship.
>
> ▪ Family business.
>
> ▪ Government/competitive exam preparation.
>
> o Helps avoid inflating “unplaced” numbers.

**Verification Sources**

· **Placement Cell Reports**

> o Annual placement reports generated by Training & Placement Cell.
>
> o Consolidated data with employer details and packages.

· **Employer HR Letters**

> o Company HR confirmation letters verifying recruitment numbers and salary packages.
>
> o Sometimes cross-checked via official employer portals (TCS, Infosys, etc.).

· **Admission Letters (Higher Studies)**

> o Admission confirmation letters from universities (domestic & foreign).
>
> o Used to validate higher studies claims.

· **Official Portals**

> o Government/University databases where students are registered.
>
> o Examples: National Scholarship Portal, study abroad verification, or alumni tracking systems.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Falls under Graduation Outcomes (GO) parameter.

· Shows employability of graduates, industry linkages, and academic progression.

· Ensures institutions provide authentic, verifiable placement & higher studies data.

· Prevents inflation of placement numbers or unverifiable salary claims.

**36. Median Salary (GMS)**

**Formula**

· GMS = 25 × f(Median Salary / Benchmark Salary)

> o Institutions report the median salary (CTC in INR) of placed students for each graduating batch.
>
> o f() = Normalization function defined by NIRF to standardize across disciplines.
>
> o A benchmark median salary (discipline/program specific, updated annually by NIRF) is used as the scaling denominator.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Earning Potential of Graduates**

> o Reflects market valuation of skills acquired at the institution.
>
> o Median is chosen (instead of average) to avoid distortion from a few very high/low outliers.

2\. **Return on Investment for Students**

> o Helps stakeholders judge whether programs deliver competitive compensation.
>
> o Links institutional reputation to graduate employability outcomes.

3\. **Discipline-Specific Relevance**

> o Engineering, Management, Medical, Law, etc. have different salary benchmarks.
>
> o NIRF's normalization ensures fair comparisons across sectors.

**Scoring Example**

· Suppose NIRF benchmark median salary for Engineering = ₹8,00,000 p.a.

· Institution's reported median salary = ₹6,40,000 p.a.

· Ratio = 6.4 ÷ 8.0 = 0.80

· If f(x) is linear up to 1.0, then:

> o GMS = 25 × 0.80 = 20 Marks (out of 25)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Median salary data for the last 3 graduating batches.
>
> o Placement records, offer letters, salary CTC sheets.
>
> o Exclude non-verified offers.

· Verification:

> o Placement cell records, employer HR letters, audited placement reports.
>
> o Should be consistent with GPH (Placement outcomes).

**Why It Matters**

· Provides a monetary measure of employability success.

· Differentiates institutions not just on % placements (quantity) but also on salary levels (quality).

· Encourages industry-aligned programs and skill development.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Median Salary Data for the Last 3 Graduating Batches**

> o Institution must report median salary figures (not average) for each of the last 3 years' graduating batches.
>
> o Median salary = Middle value of the salary package list when arranged in ascending order.
>
> o Why median? → Prevents distortion from extremely high or low packages.

· **Placement Records, Offer Letters, Salary CTC Sheets**

> o Evidence required:
>
> ▪ Company offer letters issued to students.
>
> ▪ Salary/CTC (Cost to Company) sheets issued by employer HR.
>
> ▪ Placement records maintained by the Training & Placement Cell.
>
> o Only final, accepted offers should be considered (not withdrawn or unverified offers).

· **Exclude Non-Verified Offers**

> o Offers not supported by valid documents must be excluded.
>
> o Verifiable evidence = HR letters, appointment orders, employer confirmations.
>
> o Prevents inflating placement salary data.

**Verification**

· **Placement Cell Records**

> o Official reports generated by Training & Placement Office.
>
> o Contains student-wise placement details, company names, and salary packages.

· **Employer HR Letters**

> o Direct confirmation from companies/employers regarding salary and placement numbers.
>
> o Used to verify authenticity of offer letters.

· **Audited Placement Reports**

> o Consolidated placement reports certified by institutional audit or IQAC.
>
> o Ensures consistency with institution's financial and academic records.

· **Consistency with GPH (Placement Outcomes)**

> o Reported median salary must align with GPH metric (Graduation Outcomes → Placement & Higher Studies).
>
> o Prevents mismatches between placement numbers and salary data.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Median salary is a key outcome measure under Graduation Outcomes (GO).

· Reflects employability, industry linkage, and market reputation of graduates.

· Ensures institutions report realistic, verifiable salary data.

**37. NIRF 2024 Framework for the Management Discipline**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Measures quality of teaching-learning environment and institutional resources.

· **A. Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Rewards strong enrollment, particularly balanced PG/doctoral programs in management.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

> o Adequate faculty availability ensures personalized mentoring.
>
> o Permanent faculty emphasized; expected ratio 1:15 for maximum marks.

· **C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Evaluates proportion of faculty with doctoral degrees and balanced teaching/research experience.
>
> o Important in management where applied expertise complements academic depth.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

> o Captures per-student spending on teaching resources, libraries, ICT, infrastructure, and research activities.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Assesses research productivity and professional engagement.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 40 Marks**

> o Weighted publications in Scopus/WoS-indexed journals in business, economics, and social sciences.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 40 Marks**

> o Evaluated using citations, top 25% cited papers, and journal impact indicators.
>
> o Ensures focus on impactful research, not just volume.

· **C. Footprint of Projects, Professional Practice & Executive Development Programs (FPPP) – 20 Marks**

> o Rewards consultancy projects, sponsored research, and income from executive/management development programs (EDPs/MDPs).
>
> o Reflects industry linkage and application of management knowledge.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Captures employability and career success of management graduates.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

> o Measures percentage of graduates placed or pursuing higher studies.
>
> o Critical for MBA/PGDM institutions where placements are a primary performance indicator.

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 20 Marks**

> o Tracks timely graduation rates (80% benchmark for full marks).

· **C. Median Salary (GMS) – 40 Marks**

> o Median annual salary of placed graduates, normalized across institutions and regions.
>
> o A unique highlight in management rankings since salary outcomes directly reflect graduate quality and industry acceptance.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Evaluates inclusivity, diversity, and access.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Measures percentage of students from outside the home state.
>
> o International students also contribute positively.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Rewards women's participation in student enrollment and faculty positions.

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Tracks full tuition fee reimbursements/scholarships offered.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Inclusive infrastructure (ramps, lifts, braille materials, assistive ICT).

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures reputation among stakeholders.

· **A. Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

> o Survey feedback from employers, recruiters, industry leaders, and academic peers.
>
> o Management institutions heavily depend on corporate reputation and alumni success, making perception highly influential.

**Key Insights for Management Rankings**

· Greater focus on employability and salary outcomes compared to other disciplines.

· Higher weight for research (PU + QP = 80 marks) compared to engineering/overall frameworks.

· FPPP strongly aligned with EDP/MDP income, reflecting management schools' strong executive training role.

· OI & PR remain consistent across disciplines, but placements and salaries dominate GO.
