**Key Differences Across Frameworks**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** includes Online Education (OE: 10) & MIR (5) (aligned with NEP 2020 reforms).
>
> o ***Engineering & Management*** give more weight to FSR (30) and FRU (30).

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** includes SDG-related publications (PSDG: 10).
>
> o ***Engineering*** increases emphasis on quality of publications (QP: 40) and publications (PU: 35).
>
> o ***Management*** raises PU & QP to 40 each and gives higher FPPP weight (20) to reflect executive programs.

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** = Only Exams (GUE: 60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Engineering*** = Placements (GPH: 40) + Exams (15) + Median Salary (25) + Ph.D.s (20).
>
> o ***Management*** = Placements (40) + Exams (20) + Median Salary (40) (salary heavily emphasized).

· OI & PR:

> o Uniform across all three categories → 100 marks each.

**38. NIRF 2024 Framework for the Medical Discipline**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Evaluates the availability, quality, and utilization of teaching-learning resources.

· **A. Student Strength including Doctoral Students (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Considers UG, PG, doctoral, and super-specialty student enrollment.
>
> o Rewards institutions with balanced capacity at all levels of medical education.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

> o Adequacy of full-time permanent faculty relative to student intake.
>
> o In medical education, strong FSR is critical for clinical training.

· **C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Captures faculty qualifications (Ph.D./equivalent in medical sciences) and years of teaching/clinical experience.
>
> o Encourages retention of experienced teaching doctors and researchers.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilization (FRU) – 30 Marks**

> o Average per-student capital and recurring expenditure over 3 years.
>
> o Includes labs, hospitals, simulation centers, research facilities, ICT resources.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Measures the institution's research quality, innovation, and clinical practice contributions.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 40 Marks**

> o Weighted publication count from medical databases like PubMed, Scopus, and WoS.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 40 Marks**

> o Citations per paper, h-index, top 25% cited papers, and journal impact.
>
> o Encourages globally recognized medical research.

· **C. IPR & Patents (IPR) – 10 Marks**

> o Rewards medical innovations, drugs, devices, and patents.
>
> o Lower weight compared to Engineering/Management since IP-intensive output is smaller in medicine.

· **D. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 10 Marks**

> o Sponsored clinical research, consultancy in healthcare, and community outreach programs.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Focuses on student success, progression, and specialization outcomes.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 25 Marks**

> o Percentage of graduates employed or pursuing higher studies/residency.

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 25 Marks**

> o On-time graduation success rate in medical programs.

· **C. PG Students Graduated (GPG) – 30 Marks**

> o Average number of PG students (MD/MS) completing degrees in 3 years.

· **D. Super Specialty Students Graduated (GSS) – 20 Marks**

> o DM/MCh super-specialty graduates.
>
> o Highlights advanced training capacity in specialized medicine.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Assesses diversity, equity, and accessibility.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Students from other states and foreign countries.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Women students and women faculty (incl. senior roles).

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Students receiving full fee reimbursement/scholarships.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Accessibility in hospitals, hostels, and classrooms for differently-abled students.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Reflects national and global reputation.

· **Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

> o Based on surveys among employers (healthcare institutions, pharma), academic peers, and research collaborators.
>
> o For medical institutions, reputation strongly depends on clinical outcomes, alumni in healthcare, and research collaborations.

**Key Insights for Medical Rankings**

· TLR & RP (60%) dominate → ensures clinical training and impactful research.

· GO (20%) is customized to include PG and super-specialty graduates, unique to medicine.

· OI (10%) & PR (10%) remain consistent across all disciplines.

· Lower weight for patents (10%) compared to engineering, since medicine is more research/publication-driven than patent-heavy.

**Key Differences**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** includes NEP-linked reforms → Online Education (OE: 10) & MIR (5).
>
> o ***Medical*** shifts more weight to FSR (30) and FRU (30), removing OE & MIR.

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** includes SDG-related publications (PSDG: 10).
>
> o ***Medical*** emphasizes Publications (40) & Quality of Publications (40), reducing IPR & FPPP weights (10 each).

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** only measures University Exams (60) & Ph.D. graduates (40).
>
> o ***Medical*** customizes for health education with Placements (25), Exams (25), PG graduates (30), and Super-specialty graduates (20).

· OI & PR:

> o Both remain identical in Overall and Medical.

**39. PG Students Graduated (GPG)**

**Formula**

· GPG = 30 × f(Npg)

> o Npg = Average number of PG students graduated (awarded PG degrees) in the previous 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to balance institutional size differences.
>
> o Full 30 marks are awarded if Npg ≥ benchmark value set by NIRF (discipline-specific).

**What It Captures**

1\. **Throughput of PG Programs**

> o Measures academic output of postgraduate programs (M.Tech, M.Sc., MBA, MD, MS, etc.).
>
> o Reflects institution's ability to retain and graduate PG students on time.

2\. **Postgraduate Success Rate**

> o Rewards institutions that enroll and graduate a steady stream of PG students.
>
> o Indicates academic strength, faculty engagement, and research environment.

3\. **Quality Assurance**

> o Graduation rates show the effectiveness of academic delivery, mentoring, and support systems.

**Scoring Example**

· PG Students Graduated (past 3 years):

> o 2020 = 450
>
> o 2021 = 480
>
> o 2022 = 500

· Average (Npg) = (450+480+500)/3 = 477

· If benchmark = 500 students, then normalization f(Npg) = 0.95

· GPG = 30 × 0.95 = 28.5 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise PG graduation numbers (from convocation/annual reports).
>
> o Only students graduating on time are to be counted.

· Verification Sources:

> o University results, convocation lists, transcripts.
>
> o Annual/academic reports submitted to UGC/AICTE/NMC/PCI, etc.

**Why It Matters**

· Highlights the institution's role in producing advanced degree holders.

· Shows contribution to the research ecosystem (as many PGs continue into Ph.D. or research careers).

· Reflects institutional credibility in PG education.

**40. Super Specialty Students Graduated (GSS)**

**Formula**

· GSS = 20 × f(Ngss)

> o Ngss = Average number of super-specialty students graduated (awarded degree/diploma) in the previous 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to ensure fairness across different institutional sizes and disciplines.
>
> o Full 20 marks if the average matches/exceeds the NIRF-set benchmark for that discipline (e.g., DM/MCh in Medical Colleges).

**What It Captures**

1\. **Advanced Training Output**

> o Measures the throughput of highly specialized programs such as DM (Doctorate of Medicine), M.Ch. (Master of Chirurgiae), or other equivalent super-specialty programs.

2\. **Institutional Contribution to Specialized Healthcare**

> o Rewards institutions that contribute to critical workforce needs in areas like neurosurgery, cardiology, oncology, nephrology, etc.

3\. **Sustainability of Super-Specialty Programs**

> o Reflects institutional capacity to consistently enroll, train, and graduate super-specialty students.

**Scoring Example**

· GSS Graduates (past 3 years):

> o 2020 = 60
>
> o 2021 = 55
>
> o 2022 = 70

· Average (Ngss) = (60+55+70)/3 = 61.6 ≈ 62

· If benchmark = 80 graduates → normalization = 62/80 = 0.775

· GSS = 20 × 0.775 = 15.5 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise number of super-specialty graduates.
>
> o Only count students who graduated on time (stipulated duration).

· Verification Sources:

> o University examination results, convocation/annual reports, Medical Council/NMC records.
>
> o Clinical board certifications or official degree award records.

**Why It Matters**

· Recognizes institutions excelling in producing highly skilled specialists.

· Addresses national healthcare needs where demand \> supply (e.g., cardiologists, neurosurgeons).

· Boosts institutional standing in medical education & advanced research.

**41. NIRF 2024 Framework for Colleges**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.40)**

This is the most important parameter for colleges (40%), reflecting emphasis on teaching-learning quality.

· **A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Evaluates enrollment size and utilization of sanctioned student intake.
>
> o Balanced representation of UG/PG levels.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

> o Adequacy of faculty relative to student strength, with weightage for permanent faculty.
>
> o Ideal ratio = 1:15 for maximum marks.

· **C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Percentage of faculty with PhDs/equivalent.
>
> o Accounts for distribution across experience levels (junior, mid, senior).

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

> o Expenditure on academics, infrastructure, laboratories, and ICT facilities.
>
> o Efficiency in using financial resources for quality teaching.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.15)**

Relatively lower weight compared to universities/engineering/management, as colleges are primarily teaching-oriented.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 70 Marks**

> o Weighted number of research publications from faculty in peer-reviewed, indexed journals.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 30 Marks**

> o Citation count, top 25% cited papers, and journal quality indices.
>
> o Encourages impactful rather than just high-volume research.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.25)**

Measures student success in graduation, employability, and higher studies.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

> o % of graduates placed in jobs or admitted to PG/doctoral programs.

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 40 Marks**

> o On-time graduation rates (80% benchmark for full score).

· **C. Median Salary (GMS) – 20 Marks**

> o Reflects earning potential of graduates; normalized across regions.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Focuses on diversity and accessibility in college education.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Share of students from outside the home state or abroad.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Women students and faculty representation.

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Students benefiting from full tuition reimbursement or scholarships.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Accessibility (ramps, lifts, assistive technology, scribe support).

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Reflects reputation of the college among external stakeholders.

· **Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

> o Survey feedback from employers, industry leaders, and academic peers.
>
> o For colleges, perception is heavily influenced by placement quality, alumni reputation, and teaching effectiveness.

**Key Insights for College Rankings**

· TLR dominates (40%), reflecting the teaching-centric mission of colleges.

· Research weight is smaller (15%) compared to universities, but still incentivizes faculty publications.

· GO (25%) ensures accountability in student outcomes (graduation, higher studies, salary).

· OI & PR (10% each) ensure inclusivity and reputation are factored in.

**NIRF 2024 – Overall vs Colleges Framework**

**Key Differences**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** includes Online Education (OE: 10) and MIR (5) (aligned with NEP reforms).
>
> o ***Colleges*** put stronger weight on FSR (30) and FRU (30), removing OE & MIR.

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** balances Publications, Quality, Patents, Professional Practice, SDG research.
>
> o ***Colleges*** focus heavily on Publications (70) with limited weight for Quality (30); no patents/IPR.

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** = Exams (60) + Ph.D. graduates (40).
>
> o ***Colleges*** = Placements (40) + Exams (40) + Median Salary (20).

· OI & PR:

> o Identical across both frameworks.

**42. NIRF 2024 Framework for Law Institutions**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.40)**

Law rankings place greater emphasis on teaching quality (40%), reflecting the professional and practice-oriented nature of the discipline.

· **A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Rewards institutions that maintain healthy enrollment at UG (LLB), PG (LLM), and Ph.D. levels.
>
> o Encourages development of doctoral programs in law.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

> o Measures adequacy of permanent law faculty relative to student strength.
>
> o Full marks for a ratio of 1:15 or better.

· **C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Evaluates qualifications (Ph.D. or equivalent legal research degrees) and years of teaching/professional experience.
>
> o Recognizes balance between academic knowledge and practical exposure.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

> o Per-student spending on libraries, moot court infrastructure, legal databases (HeinOnline, SCC Online, LexisNexis), ICT, and faculty salaries.
>
> o Reflects commitment to resource-driven quality education.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.15)**

Lower weight than Engineering/Medical, as law institutions are more teaching-focused, but still vital for academic credibility.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 50 Marks**

> o Weighted publications in law journals (indexed databases, UGC-approved, SCOPUS).
>
> o Includes interdisciplinary law research in governance, human rights, IP, environmental law.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 30 Marks**

> o Citation counts, peer-reviewed journal reputation, top percentile impact.
>
> o Encourages global visibility in legal scholarship.

· **C. Footprint of Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 20 Marks**

> o Consultancy with government, judiciary, or industry.
>
> o Impactful legal aid projects, public policy advisory, and professional training (CLE/MDP programs).

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.25)**

Measures employability, career progression, and academic excellence of law graduates.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

> o % of graduates placed in law firms, corporate legal teams, judiciary, or admitted to higher studies (LLM/Ph.D.).

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 15 Marks**

> o Success rate in on-time graduation at UG/PG level.

· **C. Median Salary (GMS) – 25 Marks**

> o Median salary offered to placed students, normalized for regional differences.

· **D. Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 20 Marks**

> o Rewards institutions with strong doctoral research output in law and allied fields.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Ensures diversity, access, and inclusivity in law education.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Attracting students from across India and abroad.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Gender balance in student body and faculty positions, including leadership roles.

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Students benefiting from scholarships, tuition reimbursements, and affirmative action.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Accessibility provisions like ramps, assistive technology, scribes, and inclusive hostels.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Reflects external reputation of law institutions.

· **A. Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

> o Surveys of law firms, judiciary, legal academia, alumni, and recruiters.
>
> o Captures visibility, graduate quality, and credibility of law schools.

**Key Insights for Law Rankings**

· TLR (40%) dominates, showing that law institutions are judged mainly on teaching-learning quality.

· RP (15%) is lighter compared to technical disciplines but still values legal scholarship and policy consultancy.

· GO (25%) emphasizes placements & salaries, critical for law schools with strong corporate and litigation pipelines.

· OI (10%) and PR (10%) remain standard across disciplines.

**Key Differences**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** includes Online Education (10) and MIR (5).
>
> o ***Law*** increases emphasis on FSR (30) and FRU (30), removing OE & MIR.

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** balances publications, quality, patents, projects, and SDGs.
>
> o ***Law*** simplifies to Publications (50), Quality (30), and Projects/Practice (20) → patents & SDGs not emphasized.

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** = Exams (60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Law*** adds placements (40) + median salary (25) while reducing exams (15) and Ph.D.s (20).

· OI & PR:

> o Identical across both.

**43. NIRF 2024 Framework for Pharmacy Institutions**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Evaluates the quality of academic environment, faculty, and resources.

· **A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Considers UG (B.Pharm), PG (M.Pharm), Ph.D., and doctoral enrollments.
>
> o Balanced intake across levels is rewarded.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

> o Measures adequacy of permanent faculty relative to student intake.
>
> o Ratio of 1:15 is benchmark for maximum marks.

· **C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Percentage of PhD-qualified faculty with varied experience levels (junior, mid-career, senior).
>
> o Encourages institutions to retain a mix of research-oriented and experienced teaching faculty.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

> o Tracks per-student capital and recurring expenditure over 3 years.
>
> o Includes labs, libraries, drug discovery research facilities, instrumentation centers, ICT-enabled classrooms.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Pharmacy institutions are expected to contribute heavily to applied research, patents, and industry collaborations.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 35 Marks**

> o Indexed publications in pharmacology, pharmaceutics, medicinal chemistry, and interdisciplinary sciences.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 40 Marks**

> o Citation impact, h-index, top percentile of cited papers, SCOPUS/Web of Science journal indices.

· **C. IPR & Patents (IPR) – 15 Marks**

> o Patents filed, published, and granted in drug formulations, delivery systems, biotech innovations.
>
> o Granted patents weighted higher.

· **D. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 10 Marks**

> o Sponsored projects, consultancy with pharma industry, and revenue from professional programs.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Measures success of graduates in career progression and research.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

> o % of B.Pharm/M.Pharm/Ph.D. graduates placed in pharma companies, R&D labs, regulatory agencies, or admitted for higher studies.

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 15 Marks**

> o Success rate in completing degrees within stipulated time (benchmark 80%).

· **C. Median Salary (GMS) – 25 Marks**

> o Median annual salary of placed students.

· **D. Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 20 Marks**

> o Average number of Ph.D.s awarded in pharma sciences over 3 years.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Assesses diversity, equity, and inclusivity.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Students enrolled from outside the home state and from other countries.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Proportion of women students and faculty, including leadership roles.

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Scholarships, tuition reimbursements, and support for disadvantaged groups.

· **D. Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Accessibility, assistive technology, inclusive infrastructure.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Reflects reputation of the institution among stakeholders.

· **A. Peer Perception – 100 Marks**

> o Based on surveys of employers (pharma industry, healthcare, regulatory agencies), academic peers, and research collaborators.
>
> o Captures institutional visibility, graduate quality, and brand value.

**Key Insights for Pharmacy Rankings**

· Balanced framework: TLR (30%) + RP (30%) dominate, ensuring both teaching quality and research strength are captured.

· GO (20%) emphasizes placements and research outputs, critical for employability in the pharma industry.

· Patents & IPR (15% weight) highlight the innovation-driven nature of pharmacy research.

· OI & PR (10% each) ensure inclusivity and reputation remain integral.

**NIRF 2024 – Overall vs Pharmacy Framework**

</tr>

Weight = 0.10</td>

**Key Differences**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** includes NEP reforms: Online Education (10) + MIR (5).
>
> o ***Pharmacy*** gives more weight to FSR (30) and FRU (30); excludes OE & MIR.

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** includes SDG-based publications (10).
>
> o ***Pharmacy*** shifts focus to Publications (35) and Quality of Publications (40); keeps IPR (15), but FPPP weight is lower (10).

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** = Exams (60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Pharmacy*** adds Placements (40) + Median Salary (25) in addition to Exams (15) and Ph.D.s (20).

· OI & PR:

> o Identical across both.

**44. NIRF 2024 Framework for Research Institutions**

**1. Metric for Quantitative Research (QNR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Focuses on the volume and measurable impact of research output.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 50 Marks**

> o Weighted count of research publications in reputed indexed journals (Scopus, WoS).
>
> o Rewards institutions producing high volume of publications.

· **B. Citations Index (CI) – 25 Marks**

> o Measures total citations received in the last 3 years.
>
> o Adjusted for faculty strength to ensure fairness across institution size.

· **C. Footprint of Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 25 Marks**

> o Sponsored research projects, consultancy income, industry/government collaborations.
>
> o Reflects financial sustainability and real-world impact of research.

**2. Metric for Qualitative Research (QLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Evaluates the quality, influence, and innovation of research outputs.

· **A. Publications in 1st Quartile JCR (JCRQ1) – 30 Marks**

> o % of papers published in top-quartile (Q1) journals of Journal Citation Reports.
>
> o Captures prestige and quality of research venues.

· **B. Publications in Top 25% (TOP25) – 30 Marks**

> o Fraction of publications among the top 25% most cited globally.
>
> o Rewards highly impactful research.

· **C. IPR & Patents (IPR) – 20 Marks**

> o Patents filed, published, and granted.
>
> o Encourages applied innovation and technology transfer.

· **D. H-Index (HI) – 20 Marks**

> o Measures sustained research productivity & impact at institutional level.

**3. Metric for Students & Faculty Contribution (SFC) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Assesses capacity building and contribution of human resources in research.

· **A. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 40 Marks**

> o Share of highly qualified and experienced research faculty.

· **B. Student Strength (SS) – 30 Marks**

> o Total number of doctoral and postdoctoral students engaged in research.

· **C. Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 30 Marks**

> o Average number of doctoral graduates in last 3 years.
>
> o Reflects pipeline of trained researchers.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Ensures research institutions maintain diversity and accessibility.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Share of students from other states/countries.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Women researchers and faculty representation.

· **C. SC/ST Students (SCTS) – 20 Marks**

> o Enrollment and participation of underrepresented groups.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Infrastructure and support for differently-abled researchers.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures external recognition and reputation.

· **A. Peer Perception – 100 Marks**

> o Based on surveys of academic peers, employers, industry leaders, research collaborators.
>
> o Captures reputation in terms of research excellence, innovation, and contributions to society.

**Key Insights for Research Institution Rankings**

· Research dominates (QNR + QLR = 60%), highlighting the primary mission of research institutes.

· SFC (20%) ensures balance by rewarding faculty strength, Ph.D. training, and student participation.

· OI (10%) ensures inclusivity in advanced research participation.

· PR (10%) reflects national/international reputation, making external validation significant.

**Key Differences**

· Research Institutions:

> o Entirely research-driven → QNR (30%) + QLR (30%) = 60% weight on research output & quality.
>
> o SFC (20%) replaces Teaching & GO, focusing on faculty qualifications, doctoral training, and student contribution.
>
> o OI differs slightly → includes SC/ST student representation instead of ESCS (economic criteria).

· Overall Framework:

> o Balanced focus on teaching (30%), research (30%), graduation outcomes (20%), and inclusivity (10%).
>
> o Includes NEP reforms (OE & MIR) and SDG-linked publications.

**45. Metric for Quantitative Research (QNR), Which Applies to Research Institutions**

**1. Metric for Quantitative Research (QNR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

This parameter evaluates the research volume and measurable outputs of an institution. It emphasizes the scale of scientific productivity, impact, and professional engagements.

**A. Combined Metric for Publications (PU) – 50 Marks**

· What it captures:

> o Total weighted count of research publications in Scopus/Web of Science/SCI-indexed journals.
>
> o Includes collaborative and multi-author research, normalized by faculty strength.

· Why it matters:

> o Publications are the primary evidence of research productivity.
>
> o A higher number of quality publications indicates strong research culture and faculty engagement.

· Data Source:

> o Third-party databases (Scopus, WoS, PubMed, etc.) verified by NIRF.

**B. Combined Metric for Citations Index (CI) – 25 Marks**

· What it captures:

> o The total number of citations received by institutional publications over the last 3 years.
>
> o Normalized against faculty strength (FRQ: maximum of required FSR-based faculty or actual).

· Why it matters:

> o Citations are a proxy for research quality and influence.
>
> o High citations indicate that the institution's research is recognized, used, and extended by peers globally.

· Data Source:

> o Bibliometric databases like Scopus, WoS, Google Scholar, or InCites.

**C. Footprint of Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 25 Marks**

· What it captures:

> o Average annual research funding earned, consultancy income, and professional practice revenue over the past 3 years.
>
> o Includes government-funded projects, industry-sponsored projects, consultancy contracts, and technology services.

· Why it matters:

> o Reflects the institution's ability to attract external funding, build industry partnerships, and apply research in real-world settings.
>
> o Demonstrates institutional credibility and relevance to national development and industry needs.

· Data Source:

> o Primary data provided by institutions (audited accounts, project sanction letters, consultancy receipts).

**Scoring Distribution**

· **Publications (PU): 50 marks** → highest weight, reflecting volume of research.

· **Citations (CI): 25 marks** → captures quality and impact of research.

· **Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP): 25 marks** → balances funding and applied engagement.

**Key Insights**

· QNR = 30% weight in the Research Institutions framework → indicates that quantitative outputs are the strongest determinant of rankings.

· PU (50%) + CI (25%) = 75% of QNR → ensures both volume and impact of publications matter.

· FPPP (25%) ensures institutions are judged not only by publications but also by practical, funded research contributions.

**Explanation of Columns**

· **Year** → Academic year of reporting.

· **Publications (PU)** → Weighted publication count as per Scopus/WoS/SCI databases.

· **Citations (CI)** → Total citation count (normalized).

· **Research Funding** → External project grants received (Govt/Private/International).

· **Consultancy Earnings** → Income from professional/industry consultancy.

· **FPPP Score (25 Marks)** → Derived from average of Research Funding + Consultancy normalized.

· **Total QNR Score (100 Marks)** = PU Score (50) + CI Score (25) + FPPP Score (25).

**46. H-Index (HI)**

**Formula**

· HI = 20 × f(Hinst / Hbench)

> o Hinst = Institution's H-Index (over the last 3 years or cumulative as defined by NIRF).
>
> o Hbench = Benchmark H-Index set by NIRF for maximum score.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF.
>
> o Full 20 Marks if institutional H-Index ≥ benchmark.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Research Productivity + Impact Combined**

> o The H-Index balances number of publications and citations received.
>
> o Example: H = 50 → institution has 50 papers with ≥50 citations each.

2\. **Institutional Research Reputation**

> o Higher H-Index indicates a consistent track record of impactful publications, not just isolated papers.

3\. **Benchmarking Quality**

> o Provides a single, holistic metric widely recognized in global research evaluation.

**Scoring Example**

· Benchmark H-Index (Hbench) = 120.

· Institution H-Index (Hinst) = 90.

· Ratio = 90 ÷ 120 = 0.75.

· HI Score = 20 × 0.75 = 15 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Third Party):

> o Web of Science, Scopus, or Google Scholar (institutional profile).
>
> o Citation databases that compute H-Index automatically.

· Institutional Supporting Data:

> o Faculty publication/citation lists.
>
> o Reports from bibliometric tools like InCites, SciVal.

**Why It Matters**

· H-Index captures sustained, long-term research performance, not just recent bursts.

· Prevents over-reliance on volume metrics (PU) or citation-only metrics (CI).

· Encourages institutions to build consistent, impactful research output.

**Primary Data (Third Party Sources)**

· **Web of Science (WoS)**

> o Provides publication counts, citation counts, and institutional H-index.
>
> o Data extracted via Organization-Enhanced ID.

· **Scopus**

> o Offers comprehensive citation data, h-index, and field-weighted citation impact.
>
> o Data extracted using Scopus Affiliation ID.

· **Google Scholar (Institutional Profile)**

> o Provides citations, h-index, and i10-index at institution level.
>
> o Less standardized than WoS/Scopus, but still used for supplementary validation.

· **Citation Databases Computing H-Index Automatically**

> o Both WoS and Scopus compute H-index based on institutional publication data.
>
> o H-index = Maximum “h” such that h papers have at least h citations each.
>
> o Ensures an objective measure of research quality & impact.

**Institutional Supporting Data**

· **Faculty Publication/Citation Lists**

> o Institution must maintain consolidated faculty-wise data:
>
> ▪ Publications (title, journal, year).
>
> ▪ Citation counts.
>
> ▪ Indexed database source (Scopus/WoS).
>
> o Helps reconcile third-party bibliometric data.

· **Reports from Bibliometric Tools**

> o InCites (WoS) or SciVal (Scopus) generate detailed analytics reports.
>
> o Used to support:
>
> ▪ Subject-area normalized impact.
>
> ▪ International collaboration metrics.
>
> ▪ Citation benchmarks.
>
> o Provides external validation of institutional claims.

**Verification**

· Third-party bibliometric databases (Scopus/WoS) → trusted source for NIRF.

· Institutional ID mapping ensures correct attribution (avoids mixing with similarly named institutions).

· Cross-check with faculty lists & bibliometric tool reports to prevent omissions or over-reporting.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Citation and H-index data are core parts of Research & Professional Practice (RP).

· Reflects quality, visibility, and global recognition of institutional research.

· Ensures institutions are benchmarked against reliable, globally accepted data sources.

**47. Student Strength (SS)**

**1. Formula**

· SS = f(NT, NE) × 20 + f(Np) × 10

**Where:**

· **NT** = Total sanctioned intake in UG & PG programs (as per AICTE/UGC/Statutory approvals).

· **NE** = Total enrolled students in UG & PG programs (actual strength).

· **Np** = Total doctoral (Ph.D.) enrollment till the previous academic year.

· **f()** = Normalization function decided by NIRF for scale and discipline fairness.

**2. Subcomponents**

1\. **UG/PG Strength (20 Marks)**

> o f(NT, NE) reflects how well the institution fills its approved intake capacity.
>
> o Higher student enrollment closer to approved intake = higher score.

2\. **Ph.D. Strength (10 Marks)**

> o f(Np) gives weightage to institutions with strong doctoral programs, showing advanced research culture.

**3. Interpretation**

· Encourages institutions to:

> o Maintain a healthy ratio between sanctioned intake and actual enrollment.
>
> o Strengthen doctoral education, which drives research & innovation.

· A balanced UG, PG, and Ph.D. student distribution is rewarded.

**4. Example Calculation**

· NT = 2,000 (sanctioned intake across UG/PG).

· NE = 1,800 (actual enrollment, i.e., 90% fill rate).

· Np = 300 (Ph.D. students enrolled).

· If f(NT, NE) = 0.9 and f(Np) = 0.75 (illustrative values):

> o UG/PG Score = 0.9 × 20 = 18
>
> o Ph.D. Score = 0.75 × 10 = 7.5

· SS = 18 + 7.5 = 25.5 / 30 Marks

**5. Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Sanctioned intake program-wise (UG + PG).
>
> o Actual enrollment program-wise (UG + PG).
>
> o Ph.D. student list with enrollment year.

· Verification Sources:

> o AICTE/UGC/Statutory approvals.
>
> o University/Institution admission reports.
>
> o Ph.D. enrollment registers.

**6. Why It Matters**

· Encourages institutions to maximize utilization of intake capacity.

· Rewards those with a strong doctoral base to support research excellence.

· Creates a balanced academic ecosystem from UG through Ph.D.

**48. FQE Metric**

**1. Sub-Components**

· ***FQ (20 Marks)*** → Faculty Qualification (Ph.D. or equivalent).

· ***FE (20 Marks)*** → Faculty Experience (distribution across categories).

· FQE = FQ + FE (Max = 40 Marks).

**2. Faculty Qualification (FQ – 20 Marks)**

· Formula:

> o If FRA \< 95% → FQ = 20 × (FRA ÷ 95)
>
> o If FRA ≥ 95% → FQ = 20 (full marks).

· FRA = % of faculty with Ph.D. (or equivalent) relative to whichever is higher:

> o Required Faculty (as per FSR 1:15), OR
>
> o Actual Faculty available.

· Interpretation:

> o Rewards institutions where most faculty are Ph.D. holders.
>
> o At least 95% Ph.D. faculty = full marks.

**3. Faculty Experience (FE – 20 Marks)**

· Categories:

> o F1 = Fraction of faculty with ≤ 8 years' experience.
>
> o F2 = Fraction of faculty with \>8 to 15 years' experience.
>
> o F3 = Fraction of faculty with \>15 years' experience.

· Formula:

> o FE = 6 × min(3F1, 1) + 6 × min(3F2, 1) + 8 × min(3F3, 1)

· Rationale:

> o Ensures a balanced mix of young (F1), mid-career (F2), and senior (F3) faculty.
>
> o Maximum marks if ratio is close to 1:1:1.

**4. Example Calculation**

· Total faculty = 120

> o Ph.D. faculty = 105 (FRA = 87.5%)
>
> o FQ = 20 × (87.5 ÷ 95) = 18.4 Marks

· Faculty Experience:

> o F1 = 40 faculty (40/120 = 0.33)
>
> o F2 = 40 faculty (40/120 = 0.33)
>
> o F3 = 40 faculty (40/120 = 0.33)

· FE = (6 × min(0.99,1)) + (6 × min(0.99,1)) + (8 × min(0.99,1))

> o = 6 + 6 + 8 = 20 Marks

· Final FQE = FQ + FE = 18.4 + 20 = 38.4 / 40

**5. Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Complete faculty list with qualification & years of experience.

· Verification Sources:

> o HR records, Annual Report.
>
> o Faculty appointment and Ph.D. award certificates.

**6. Why It Matters**

· High Ph.D. % = strong academic foundation.

· Balanced experience profile = effective mentoring + research output + academic quality.

· Prevents skew towards either only young or only senior faculty.

**49. Publications in 1st Quartile JCR (JCRQ1)**

**Formula**

· JCRQ1 = 30 × f(Nq1 / FRQ)

> o Nq1 = Number of publications in 1st Quartile (Q1) journals according to Journal Citation Reports (JCR, Clarivate Analytics).
>
> o FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max(required faculty strength as per FSR 1:15, or actual faculty strength).
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF.
>
> o Full 30 Marks if benchmark (set by NIRF) is met/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Publication Quality Benchmark**

> o Q1 journals = top 25% of journals in a discipline ranked by Impact Factor & citation distribution.
>
> o Demonstrates global standard of research quality.

2\. **Faculty Research Excellence**

> o Encourages faculty to publish in high-impact journals, not just high volume.
>
> o Q1 publications generally have higher citations, global visibility, and prestige.

3\. **Global Competitiveness**

> o Institutions with higher JCRQ1 output are seen as globally competitive research leaders.

**Scoring Example**

· FRQ = 250

· Q1 Publications (last 3 years) = 300

· Ratio = 300 ÷ 250 = 1.2

· Benchmark = 1.5

· Normalized = 1.2 ÷ 1.5 = 0.8

· JCRQ1 Score = 30 × 0.8 = 24 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Third Party):

> o Web of Science + JCR (Clarivate Analytics).
>
> o Affiliation-based extraction of Q1 journal publications.

· Institutional Supporting Data:

> o Faculty-wise publication list with DOI, journal name, and JCR quartile classification.
>
> o Verified bibliometric reports (Scopus/Web of Science exports).

**Why It Matters**

· Ensures NIRF doesn't just reward publication counts, but prioritizes quality, influence, and global recognition.

· Strengthens India's research presence in globally competitive journals.

· Provides incentive to move research from Q3/Q4 journals to Q1/Q2 outlets.

**Primary Data (Third Party)**

· **Web of Science (WoS) + Journal Citation Reports (JCR, Clarivate Analytics)**

> o Used as the authoritative source to determine journal quality.
>
> o JCR provides quartile classification (Q1–Q4) based on Impact Factor rankings within subject categories.
>
> o Q1 journals → Top 25% of journals in their field (highest quality).

· **Affiliation-Based Extraction of Q1 Publications**

> o Publications are extracted using the institution's official WoS Affiliation ID.
>
> o Ensures publications are accurately attributed to the reporting institution.
>
> o Only those papers published in Q1 journals are considered for this metric.

**Institutional Supporting Data**

· **Faculty-Wise Publication List**

> o Institution must prepare a detailed faculty-wise list including:
>
> ▪ Faculty Name
>
> ▪ Publication Title
>
> ▪ Journal Name
>
> ▪ Year of Publication
>
> ▪ DOI (Digital Object Identifier)
>
> ▪ JCR Quartile Classification (Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4)

· **Verified Bibliometric Reports**

> o Exported reports from Scopus/WoS with filters applied for Q1 journals.
>
> o May include SciVal or InCites analytics.
>
> o Must be consistent with the JCR quartile classification.

**Verification**

· Cross-check with Clarivate JCR database for quartile confirmation.

· Validate using WoS exports with institution affiliation filters applied.

· Ensure DOI-level verification to prevent inclusion of predatory or non-Q1 journals.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Q1 journal publications indicate research excellence and global visibility.

· Helps distinguish between volume of research vs. quality of research.

· Aligns with global ranking methodologies (e.g., QS, THE).

**50. Publications in Top 25% (TOP25)**

**Formula**

· TOP25 = 30 × f(Ntop25 / FRQ)

> o Ntop25 = Number of publications (past 3 years) in the top 25 percentile of global journals (by Impact Factor / Citation percentile).
>
> o FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max(required faculty strength as per 1:15 FSR, or actual faculty strength).
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to balance across institutions and disciplines.
>
> o Full 30 Marks if benchmark (set by NIRF) is met/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Global Publication Quality Beyond Volume**

> o Measures how many institutional papers appear in top-cited, high-impact journals, ensuring global recognition.
>
> o Goes beyond JCR Q1 alone, as top 25% may cut across subject-specific rankings.

2\. **Research Excellence and Prestige**

> o Demonstrates an institution's ability to produce research that is not only published but recognized among the best globally.

3\. **Benchmarking Research Competitiveness**

> o Rewards HEIs that compete with world-leading institutions in research visibility.

**Scoring Example**

· FRQ = 200 faculty

· Ntop25 = 220 publications in last 3 years within top 25% journals

· Ratio = 220 ÷ 200 = 1.1

· Benchmark = 1.5

· Normalized ratio = 1.1 ÷ 1.5 = 0.733

· TOP25 Score = 30 × 0.733 = 22 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Third Party):

> o Web of Science / Scopus / JCR (Clarivate) with percentile rankings.
>
> o Bibliometric services (e.g., InCites, SciVal).

· Institutional Supporting Data:

> o Faculty publication lists with DOI and journal classification.
>
> o Verified exports from citation databases showing percentile.

**Why It Matters**

· Rewards research quality and impact, not just quantity.

· Pushes Indian HEIs toward global competitiveness in citations and journal impact.

· Encourages faculty to target top-ranked outlets, improving institutional reputation.

**Primary Data (Third Party Sources)**

· **Web of Science (WoS) / Scopus / JCR (Clarivate Analytics)**

> o Provide percentile rankings of journals based on Impact Factor or CiteScore.
>
> o Journals are ranked relative to others in the same subject category.
>
> o Example:
>
> ▪ Top 10% journals → Highest impact, most prestigious.
>
> ▪ Next 10–25% → Strong research quality.

· **Bibliometric Services (InCites, SciVal)**

> o InCites (Clarivate, WoS) and SciVal (Elsevier, Scopus) provide analytics dashboards.
>
> o Generate percentile-based publication reports at institutional and faculty levels.
>
> o Used to cross-verify institutional claims.

**Institutional Supporting Data**

· **Faculty Publication Lists with DOI & Journal Classification**

> o Faculty-wise list of all publications including:
>
> ▪ Title, Journal Name, DOI.
>
> ▪ Journal classification (percentile rank, quartile rank).
>
> o Helps track research distribution across top-ranked journals.

· **Verified Exports from Citation Databases**

> o Institutions must provide exported reports from Scopus/WoS showing journal percentiles.
>
> o Exports must be affiliation-based to avoid false attribution.
>
> o Example evidence:
>
> ▪ CSV/Excel exports from Scopus.
>
> ▪ InCites PDF/Excel reports.
>
> ▪ JCR percentile reports.

**Verification**

· Cross-verified with Clarivate JCR or Elsevier Scopus percentile lists.

· Checked against DOI-level metadata for accuracy.

· Consistency must be maintained with:

> o Research outputs in Q1/Q2 journals.
>
> o Percentile rankings reported by InCites/SciVal.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Goes beyond publication volume to measure research quality and impact.

· Incentivizes publishing in top 10% or top 25% journals globally.

· Aligns with international ranking systems (QS, THE, ARWU).

**51. NIRF 2024 Framework for Skill Universities**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.40)**

For skill universities, teaching and training infrastructure carries the highest weight (40%), as these institutions are practice- and employability-oriented.

· **A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Rewards healthy enrollment across UG, PG, diploma, and vocational courses.
>
> o Reflects institutional capacity to attract and train students.

· **B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 25 Marks**

> o Adequacy of permanent faculty relative to student numbers.
>
> o Importance placed on availability of trainers and faculty mentors for vocational education.

· **C. Faculty Qualification & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

> o Percentage of faculty with advanced qualifications and relevant professional/industry experience.
>
> o Ensures that instructors bring practical expertise into training delivery.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 25 Marks**

> o Per-student capital and recurring expenditure on labs, equipment, training workshops, ICT, and infrastructure.
>
> o Evaluates how effectively funds are utilized to support skills training.

· **E. Vocational Certificate Courses (VCC) – 10 Marks**

> o Special provision for institutions offering short-term vocational training programs, aligned with NEP-2020 and Skill India initiatives.
>
> o Encourages integration of industry-relevant certifications into the curriculum.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.15)**

Research is given lower weight (15%) compared to traditional universities, but practice-driven innovation and patents are highly emphasized.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 20 Marks**

> o Research output in applied sciences, technical skills, and vocational pedagogy.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 20 Marks**

> o Citation metrics, journal reputation, top-cited papers.

· **C. IPR & Patents (IPR) – 40 Marks**

> o Heavy weightage for patents published/granted, reflecting applied innovations and industry-oriented outputs.

· **D. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 20 Marks**

> o Sponsored projects, consultancy, incubation, and professional training initiatives.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.25)**

Measures success in employability, higher studies, and entrepreneurship.

· **A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 35 Marks**

> o % of graduates placed in jobs or admitted to higher studies.

· **B. University Examinations (GUE) – 35 Marks**

> o On-time graduation and completion rate across programs.

· **C. Median Salary (GMS) – 20 Marks**

> o Median salary of placed graduates.

· **D. Placement & Entrepreneurship in Certificate Programs – 10 Marks**

> o New metric unique to skill universities.
>
> o Tracks success of short-term certificate holders in securing jobs or starting businesses.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Ensures skill universities cater to diverse and underrepresented groups.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

> o Students from outside the home state or from abroad.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Representation of women students and faculty.

· **C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Full tuition reimbursements, scholarships, and fee waivers for underprivileged students.

· **D. Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Infrastructure and support for differently-abled students (labs, hostels, ICT).

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures reputation among employers and peers.

· **Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

> o Based on structured surveys with employers, industry partners, and academic peers.
>
> o For skill universities, perception is strongly influenced by industry linkages, placement performance, and innovation-driven training.

**Key Insights for Skill University Rankings**

· TLR dominates (40%), with a unique metric for vocational certificate courses (10%) → highlights skill-based training.

· RP weight is lower (15%), but IPR & patents carry the highest share (40 marks), encouraging applied innovation.

· GO (25%) emphasizes placements, salaries, and entrepreneurship, capturing employability outcomes.

· OI & PR (10% each) align with inclusivity and reputation factors common to other categories.

**Key Differences**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** includes NEP-driven Online Education (10) and MIR (5).
>
> o ***Skill Universities*** emphasize Vocational Certificate Courses (10) and assign higher overall weight (40%).

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** = balanced across publications, quality, patents, projects, and SDG-linked research.
>
> o ***Skill Universities*** = lighter RP weight (15%), but patents/IPR dominate (40 marks), reflecting applied innovation.

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** = Exams (60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Skill Universities*** = expanded to include Placements (35), Exams (35), Median Salary (20), and Entrepreneurship in Certificate Programs (10).

· OI & PR:

> o Identical across both frameworks.

**52. Metric for Vocational Certificate Courses (VCC)**

· **Purpose:**

> o To encourage higher education institutions, especially Skill Universities, to integrate short-term, industry-relevant vocational training into their programs.
>
> o Supports the Skill India Mission and NEP-2020 emphasis on multiple entry/exit and flexible learning pathways.

· **What It Captures:**

> o The number, diversity, and effectiveness of vocational certificate courses offered.
>
> o Tracks enrollment, completion rates, industry recognition, and employability outcomes of these programs.

· **Expected Coverage:**

> o Skill-oriented courses of 3–12 months duration, aligned with:
>
> ▪ National Skills Qualifications Framework (NSQF) levels.
>
> ▪ Sector Skill Councils (SSC) or National Skill Development Corporation (NSDC) approvals.
>
> ▪ Industry-partnered certification (e.g., construction, IT, healthcare, retail, tourism).

· **Assessment Basis:**

> o Number of certificate programs offered in the previous 3 years.
>
> o Enrollment and completion rates of students.
>
> o Industry/sector recognition (DPIIT, SSC, NSDC, UGC approval).
>
> o ***Employability impact*** – percentage of certificate holders securing jobs, apprenticeships, or self-employment.
>
> o Integration with regular degree pathways (credits for upward mobility).

· **Data Source:**

> o Primary data to be provided by the institution in a prescribed format.
>
> o Supported by certificates issued, placement records, MoUs with industry partners, and accreditation from skill councils.

**Scoring Logic (10 Marks)**

· Institutions offering multiple, well-recognized certificate courses with high employability outcomes → closer to full 10 marks.

· Institutions offering limited or low-impact certificate programs → partial marks.

· Institutions with no vocational certificate programs → zero marks.

**Vocational Certificate Courses – Prescribed Format (Sample)**

**Explanation of Columns**

· **Course Name** → Title of the vocational course.

· **Duration (Months)** → Length of course (3–12 months typical).

· **NSQF Level** → Alignment with National Skills Qualification Framework.

· **Enrollments & Completions** → Student participation and success rate.

· **Industry Partner / SSC** → Sector Skill Council, NSDC, or Industry collaborator validating the course.

· **Placement/Entrepreneurship Outcome (%)** → Share of students placed, self-employed, or pursuing higher pathways.

· **Score Contribution** → Weighted contribution toward VCC (10 Marks).

**53. Combined Metric for Placement & Entrepreneurship in Certificate Programs (PECP)**

· **Purpose:**

> o To measure the real-world impact of short-term vocational certificate programs.
>
> o Ensures these programs lead to gainful employment or entrepreneurship, not just certifications.
>
> o Aligned with Skill India Mission and NEP 2020's focus on employability and self-reliance.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Placement Outcomes**

> o % of students completing certificate programs who are placed in jobs, apprenticeships, or internships.
>
> o Includes placement in formal sectors (industry, service, IT, healthcare, manufacturing, etc.).

2\. **Entrepreneurship Outcomes**

> o % of students who start their own ventures, small businesses, or self-employment activities after course completion.
>
> o Includes registered startups, local enterprises, or freelance career paths.

3\. **Integration with National Schemes**

> o Programs aligned with NSQF, SSC, NSDC, or government-approved certifications.
>
> o Recognition under DPIIT Startup India also strengthens scoring.

**Assessment Basis**

· Primary Data (Institution-provided):

> o Course-wise data on enrollments, completions, placements, and entrepreneurship cases.
>
> o Supporting documents: placement records, offer letters, startup registration certificates, MoUs with industry, etc.

· Weight Distribution (within 10 Marks):

> o Placement Success → 5 Marks
>
> o Entrepreneurship Success → 5 Marks

· Scoring Logic:

> o Full marks (10/10) if:
>
> ▪ ≥70% of certificate graduates are employed or self-employed.
>
> o Partial marks based on proportional outcomes.
>
> o Zero marks if no placement/entrepreneurship outcomes are reported.

**Example**

· Total Certificate Program Graduates (last 3 years): 500

· Placed in Jobs: 250 (50%) → contributes proportionally (≈3.5 marks out of 5)

· Entrepreneurship Cases: 100 (20%) → contributes proportionally (≈2.0 marks out of 5)

· PECP Score = 5.5 / 10 marks

**Explanation of Columns**

· **Year** → Academic year of reporting.

· **Certificate Graduates** → Number of students completing certificate programs.

· **Placed** → Number securing jobs, internships, or apprenticeships.

· **Entrepreneurs** → Number starting startups, small businesses, or self-employment.

· **Placement %** → Placed ÷ Graduates × 100.

· **Entrepreneurship %** → Entrepreneurs ÷ Graduates × 100.

· **PECP Score** →

> o Placement → Max 5 marks (scaled proportionally).
>
> o Entrepreneurship → Max 5 marks (scaled proportionally).
>
> o Total = Placement Score + Entrepreneurship Score (Max 10).

**54. NIRF 2024 Framework for Open Universities**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.40)**

For open and distance learning (ODL) institutions, teaching-learning quality and digital delivery infrastructure carry the highest weight.

· **A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

> o Considers UG, PG, and doctoral student enrollment.
>
> o Rewards balanced growth and support for large-scale student populations.

· **B. Faculty per Discipline (FPP) – 20 Marks**

> o Evaluates adequacy of permanent faculty assigned to different disciplines.
>
> o Critical for ODL models, where specialized faculty design courses and learner support.

· **C. Faculty Qualification & Experience (FQE) – 10 Marks**

> o Measures proportion of PhD-qualified and experienced faculty.
>
> o Lower weight compared to other categories, since course development and technology-driven delivery dominate.

· **D. Financial Resources & Utilization (FRU) – 20 Marks**

> o Average per-student expenditure on courseware, ICT systems, study centers, and faculty development.

· **E. Online Education (OE) – 10 Marks**

> o Adoption of e-learning, online examinations, SWAYAM course integration, and digital assessments.

· **F. Learning Materials Development & Revision (MDR) – 10 Marks**

> o Quantity and quality of Self-Learning Materials (SLMs) and audio/video modules developed and revised.
>
> o Recognizes commitment to keeping content updated and learner-centric.

· **G. Multiple Entry/Exit, Indian Knowledge System, Regional Languages (MIR) – 10 Marks**

> o Rewards implementation of NEP-2020 reforms, regional language offerings, and incorporation of Indian knowledge systems.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.15)**

Given lower emphasis compared to conventional universities, but still important for academic credibility.

· **A. Publications (PU) – 50 Marks**

> o Weighted publication count in indexed journals.

· **B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 30 Marks**

> o Citation counts, journal impact factors, and top-cited papers.

· **C. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 20 Marks**

> o Sponsored projects, consultancy, community learning programs, and collaborations with industries or NGOs.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.25)**

Measures student success and progression in a large-scale ODL environment.

· **A. UG Students Graduated (GUG) – 40 Marks**

> o Number of undergraduate students completing their degrees within stipulated time.

· **B. PG Students Graduated (GPG) – 40 Marks**

> o Number of postgraduates successfully graduating.

· **C. Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 20 Marks**

> o Average number of doctoral graduates in last 3 years.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

ODL institutions are judged on how well they reach diverse populations.

· **A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 25 Marks**

> o Balance of rural and urban students.
>
> o Unique focus compared to other categories, given open universities' role in bridging rural access gaps.

· **B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

> o Enrollment of women in various programs.

· **C. Socially Challenged Background (SCB) – 25 Marks**

> o Representation of SC/ST/OBC students.

· **D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

> o Accessible learning centers, digital content, assistive technologies.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures the credibility of open universities among peers and employers.

· **A. Peer Perception (70 Marks)**

> o Survey of employers, academics, and professionals on the reputation of graduates.

· **B. NAAC Accreditation (30 Marks)**

> o Formal accreditation status of the institution.
>
> o Unique to open universities, where quality assurance frameworks (NAAC/AQAR/ODL guidelines) are critical.

**Key Insights for Open University Rankings**

· TLR dominates (40%), with special focus on digital learning, SLMs, and vocational relevance.

· RP is lighter (15%), but still values research and professional practice.

· GO (25%) emphasizes mass graduation outcomes, especially UG/PG students.

· OI (10%) is uniquely designed to include rural–urban balance and SC/ST/OBC representation.

· PR (10%) differs by giving explicit weight to NAAC accreditation (30 marks) alongside peer perception.

**Key Differences**

· TLR:

> o ***Overall*** emphasizes faculty ratio + NEP reforms (OE, MIR).
>
> o ***Open Universities*** expand TLR to 40% weight, adding Faculty per Discipline, Modules Developed/Revised (SLMs, AVs) → reflecting digital learning.

· RP:

> o ***Overall*** gives 30% weight with patents + SDG-linked publications.
>
> o ***Open Universities*** reduce RP to 15%, focusing mainly on publications and practice; patents & SDGs are not emphasized.

· GO:

> o ***Overall*** = Exams + Ph.D. graduates.
>
> o ***Open Universities*** = UG, PG, and Ph.D. graduates → better suited for mass ODL enrollment.

· OI:

> o ***Overall*** = economic inclusivity (ESCS).
>
> o ***Open Universities*** = rural–urban balance + SC/ST/OBC representation, recognizing their mandate to widen access.

· PR:

> o ***Overall*** = 100% peer perception.
>
> o ***Open Universities*** = 70% peer perception + 30% NAAC accreditation (unique).

**55. NIRF 2024 Framework for Innovation Ranking**

**1. Financial Support for Innovation (FSI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.25)**

Measures the financial ecosystem available for nurturing innovation and startups.

· **A. Ventures/Startups Recognized by DPIIT/Startup India (SU) – 20 Marks**

> o Captures the number of startups officially recognized under national innovation initiatives.

· **B. Ventures/Startups Receiving VC Investment (VCI) – 25 Marks**

> o Percentage and scale of startups securing venture capital funding.
>
> o Reflects investor confidence and growth potential.

· **C. FDI Investment in Ventures/Startups (FDII) – 25 Marks**

> o Measures global recognition by tracking FDI inflows into institutional startups.

· **D. Innovation Grants from Government (IG) – 30 Marks**

> o Captures grants from DST, DBT, MSME, AICTE, or other government funding bodies.
>
> o Encourages participation in government-led innovation schemes.

**2. Innovation Achievements (IA) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.15)**

Rewards tangible achievements of innovation activities.

· **A. Innovations at Various Technology Readiness Levels (ITRL) – 40 Marks**

> o Tracks projects/products across TRL stages from concept to commercialization.

· **B. Ventures/Startups with Turnover ≥ ₹50 Lakhs (NVS) – 40 Marks**

> o Reflects successful scaling of incubated ventures.

· **C. Alumni Founders of Forbes/Fortune 500 Companies (AFF) – 20 Marks**

> o Highlights alumni impact at global entrepreneurial platforms.

**3. Research & Innovation Output in SCI (RIO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Focuses on scientific research, patents, and commercialization.

· **A. Publications in SCI Journals (PU) – 25 Marks**

> o Weighted count of indexed SCI publications.

· **B. Quality of Publications (CI) – 25 Marks**

> o Citation impact and recognition of SCI publications.

· **C. IPR & Patents (IPR) – 40 Marks**

> o Patents published, filed, and granted (domestic and international).

· **D. Patent Commercialization & Technology Transfer – 10 Marks**

> o Successful licensing, spin-offs, or technology transfer agreements.

**4. Pre-Incubation & Incubation (PII) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Evaluates institutional incubation capacity and training ecosystem.

· **A. Pre-Incubation (PI) – 30 Marks**

> o Number of pre-incubation programs, mentoring support, idea labs, and seed-stage initiatives.

· **B. Incubation (IIE) – 30 Marks**

> o Established incubators, startup density, and sustained incubation success rates.

· **C. Faculty Development Programs in Innovation (FDP) – 20 Marks**

> o Programs for faculty training in innovation and entrepreneurship pedagogy.

· **D. Credit Courses in Innovation – 20 Marks**

> o Introduction of credit-based entrepreneurship/innovation courses in curriculum.

**5. Adoption of I&E Policy in HEIs (AIE) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.05)**

Assesses how strongly the institution has aligned with national innovation policies.

· **A. Registered on NISP Portal (NISP) – 20 Marks**

> o Checks if the institution has adopted the National Innovation and Startup Policy (NISP).

· **B. Annual Performance of Institution's Innovation Councils (IICs) – 30 Marks**

> o Assessed via MoE's IIC performance scoring.

· **C. Participation in KAPILA Portal – 20 Marks**

> o Student/faculty engagement in IPR awareness and patent filing initiatives.

· **D. Participation in Smart India Hackathon (SIH) – 30 Marks**

> o Rewards student innovation participation in national innovation challenges.

**6. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Reflects external recognition of innovation capabilities.

· **A. Peer Perception – 100 Marks**

> o Surveys of employers, VCs, academic peers, and startup ecosystem leaders.
>
> o Captures institutional reputation in fostering entrepreneurship and innovation.

**Key Insights for Innovation Ranking**

· Research & Innovation Output (30%) + Financial Support (25%) = 55% combined weight → strong emphasis on funding, research, and patents.

· Innovation Achievements (15%) highlight TRL stages, startup turnover, and alumni success.

· Pre-Incubation & Policy Adoption (20%) ensure institutions are structurally ready for entrepreneurship.

· Perception (10%) validates credibility through stakeholder recognition.

**NIRF 2024 – Overall vs Innovation Ranking Framework**
