**Key Differences**

· Overall Framework:

> o Balanced focus on teaching (30%), research (30%), outcomes (20%), inclusivity (10%), perception (10%).
>
> o Includes NEP reforms (OE, MIR) and SDG-linked publications.

· Innovation Ranking:

> o Shifts focus toward financial ecosystem (25%), innovation achievements (15%), research outputs (30%), and incubation ecosystem (10%).
>
> o Adds policy adoption (10%) → NISP, IIC, KAPILA, SIH.
>
> o Still includes perception (10%) but oriented toward industry, VCs, and startup ecosystem peers.

**56. Registered on NISP Portal (NISP)**

**Formula**

· NISP = 20 Marks (Binary Metric)

> o Institutions registered on the NISP portal (National Innovation and Startup Policy) are awarded the full 20 marks.
>
> o Institutions not registered get 0 marks.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Policy Adoption at Institution Level**

> o Confirms that the institution has officially adopted the National Innovation and Startup Policy (2019) framework issued by AICTE & MHRD Innovation Cell.
>
> o Ensures governance-level commitment to fostering innovation and entrepreneurship.

2\. **Institutional Alignment with National Priorities**

> o Registration signifies that the institution has a functional I&E policy, guiding:
>
> ▪ Student & faculty startups.
>
> ▪ IPR management.
>
> ▪ Resource mobilization.
>
> ▪ Incubation and commercialization strategies.

3\. **Foundation for Other Metrics**

> o Acts as the baseline requirement for effective performance in other innovation ranking parameters like FDP, credit courses, incubation, etc.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution A: Registered on NISP portal → Score = 20 Marks.

· Institution B: Not registered → Score = 0 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Confirmation of registration on NISP portal.
>
> o Copy of NISP Policy Document adopted by the institution.

· Verification Sources:

> o Official NISP portal (https://www.mic.gov.in/).
>
> o AICTE/Institutional IIC records.

**Why It Matters**

· Ensures that innovation & entrepreneurship are part of institutional governance.

· Establishes policy consistency across Indian HEIs.

· Strengthens the national innovation ecosystem through structured institutional participation.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Confirmation of Registration on NISP Portal**

> o Institution must provide proof of registration on the official NISP portal hosted by the Ministry of Education's Innovation Cell (MIC).
>
> o Registration confirms the institution's participation in implementing National Innovation and Startup Policy (2019).
>
> o Supporting data:
>
> ▪ Registration certificate / screenshot of registered institution profile.
>
> ▪ NISP portal reference number.

· **Copy of NISP Policy Document Adopted by the Institution**

> o Institution should prepare and adopt an internal NISP Policy document aligned with the national framework.
>
> o Policy should cover:
>
> ▪ Entrepreneurship promotion.
>
> ▪ Startup incubation support.
>
> ▪ Faculty and student IP ownership guidelines.
>
> ▪ Institutional Innovation Council (IIC) functioning.
>
> o Supporting data:
>
> ▪ Approved policy document (Academic Council/Board of Governors resolution).
>
> ▪ Uploaded copy on institutional website (for transparency).

**Verification Sources**

· **Official NISP Portal (https://www.mic.gov.in/)**

> o Used by NIRF assessors to confirm institution's registration status.
>
> o Registration ID and institution profile are cross-checked.

· **AICTE / Institutional IIC Records**

> o Verification through Institution's Innovation Council (IIC) activities and compliance reports.
>
> o AICTE maintains a database of institutions adopting NISP.
>
> o IIC annual performance reports may also be used for validation.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· NISP compliance shows the institution's commitment to innovation, entrepreneurship, and startup culture.

· Encourages alignment with national innovation ecosystem goals.

· Adds weight under Research & Professional Practice (RP) and Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) metrics.

**57. Participation in KAPILA Portal (KAPILA)**

**Formula**

· KAPILA = 20 × f(Nkapila)

> o Nkapila = Number of faculty/students trained or engaged in IPR activities through the KAPILA portal in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF.
>
> o Full 20 Marks awarded when benchmark (set by NIRF/MIC) is reached/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **IPR Awareness & Capacity Building**

> o Ensures institutions are promoting Intellectual Property Rights (IPR) literacy among students and faculty.
>
> o Tracks how many members of the institution are actively participating in patent awareness and training programs.

2\. **National Program Alignment**

> o KAPILA (Kalaam Program for IP Literacy and Awareness) is a GoI initiative under MoE's Innovation Cell.
>
> o Institutions participating show alignment with national IPR development goals.

3\. **Encouraging Patent Filing**

> o Builds an IPR culture so more students/faculty file patents through institutional IPR cells.

**Scoring Example**

· Benchmark participation = 1,000 students/faculty trained.

· Institution reports = 600 trained.

· Ratio = 600 ÷ 1,000 = 0.6.

· KAPILA Score = 20 × 0.6 = 12 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o No. of students/faculty enrolled in KAPILA training sessions.
>
> o Certificates of participation, attendance records.

· Verification Sources:

> o MoE's KAPILA portal (https://kapila.mic.gov.in).
>
> o IIC reports linked to KAPILA participation.

**Why It Matters**

· Promotes awareness and skills in IPR filing & management.

· Strengthens India's innovation pipeline through patent literacy.

· Ensures every HEI develops a culture of IP protection & commercialization.

**58. Annual Performance of Institution's Innovation Councils (IICs)**

**Formula**

· IIC Score = 30 × f(Niic)

> o Niic = Annual performance score of the institution's IIC as provided by the Ministry of Education's Innovation Cell (MIC).
>
> o Institutions are scored based on activities and ratings published in the Annual IIC Scorecard.
>
> o Full 30 Marks if the IIC achieves benchmark performance (5★ rating or equivalent).

**What It Captures**

1\. **Institutional Innovation Governance**

> o Evaluates the functioning and performance of the IIC created under MoE's Innovation Cell.
>
> o Measures the institution's seriousness in monitoring innovation progress.

2\. **Activities Considered**

> o No. of events conducted (seminars, hackathons, idea competitions).
>
> o Faculty & student participation.
>
> o Startups supported through IIC.
>
> o Linkages with national missions (Atal Innovation Mission, Startup India, etc.).
>
> o Regional/National impact of activities.

3\. **Annual Evaluation**

> o IICs are rated 1★ to 5★ based on performance data submitted on the official MIC portal.
>
> o Higher ratings correspond to higher normalized marks under this metric.

**Scoring Example**

· IIC Annual Performance Score (as per MIC portal) = 85/100.

· If benchmark = 90 → ratio = 85 ÷ 90 = 0.944.

· IIC Metric Score = 30 × 0.944 = 28.3 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Copy of IIC annual performance report downloaded from MIC portal.
>
> o Evidence of events, activities, and participation.

· Verification Sources:

> o MoE's Innovation Cell IIC Portal: https://iic.mic.gov.in.
>
> o Annual IIC star rating certificates.

**Why It Matters**

· Encourages structured innovation governance within HEIs.

· Rewards institutions that actively conduct, track, and scale innovation activities.

· Ensures alignment with national innovation missions.

**59. Credit Courses in Innovation (CCI)**

**Formula**

· CCI = 20 × f(Ncci)

> o Ncci = Weighted count of credit-bearing courses in innovation, entrepreneurship, and startup-related domains offered by the institution in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF.
>
> o Full 20 Marks awarded when benchmark (set by NIRF) for credit courses is reached/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Academic Integration of Innovation**

> o Rewards institutions that embed innovation and entrepreneurship into formal curriculum.
>
> o Goes beyond extracurricular activities — making innovation part of mainstream academics.

2\. **Types of Courses Considered**

> o Full credit courses on entrepreneurship, design thinking, startup management, venture creation.
>
> o Minor degree / electives on innovation & entrepreneurship (I&E).
>
> o Integrated courses on IPR, technology commercialization, incubation management.
>
> o NEP 2020-compliant choice-based credit system (CBCS) offerings.

3\. **Outcome Focus**

> o Students completing these courses gain credits toward graduation.
>
> o Ensures formal recognition of innovation learning.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution offered in 3 years:

> o 6 full-credit entrepreneurship courses (3–4 credits each).
>
> o 4 electives in design thinking & IPR.

· Benchmark = 10 courses.

· Ratio = 10 ÷ 10 = 1.0 → full score.

· CCI Score = 20 × 1.0 = 20 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of courses with syllabus, credits, and duration.
>
> o No. of students enrolled & passed.

· Verification Sources:

> o Academic council approvals.
>
> o Course syllabi uploaded to website.
>
> o CBCS/NEP records.
>
> o University exam records.

**Why It Matters**

· Aligns with NEP 2020 focus on innovation & entrepreneurship education.

· Strengthens the academic pipeline for startup creation.

· Builds a structured foundation for future innovators and entrepreneurs.

**60. Faculty Development Programs in Innovation (FDP)**

**Formula**

· FDP = 20 × f(Nfdp)

> o Nfdp = Weighted count of Faculty Development Programs (FDPs) in innovation, entrepreneurship, and related areas conducted by the institution in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to scale across institutions.
>
> o Full 20 Marks when benchmark FDP activity levels (as set by NIRF) are reached/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Faculty Capacity Building**

> o Focuses on how many FDPs the institution organizes for faculty in the domain of innovation, IPR, entrepreneurship, startup mentoring, and technology transfer.
>
> o Ensures that faculty are equipped to mentor students and startups.

2\. **Types of FDP Activities**

> o Workshops on design thinking, innovation pedagogy, and IP management.
>
> o Training on incubation processes, entrepreneurship mentoring.
>
> o Certification programs on entrepreneurship and startup policies (like NISP, IIC, MSME schemes).
>
> o Industry-led FDPs in emerging technology commercialization.

3\. **Quality and Outreach**

> o FDPs with national-level resource persons, DST/AICTE/IIC endorsements, or industry collaborations add more value.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution conducted in 3 years:

> o 5 FDPs with 30 faculty participants each.
>
> o 3 FDPs with 50 faculty participants each.

· Total = 8 FDPs, ~340 faculty trained.

· If benchmark = 10 FDPs → ratio = 8 ÷ 10 = 0.8.

· FDP Score = 20 × 0.8 = 16 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise list of FDPs conducted.
>
> o No. of participants, duration, topic, funding agency.

· Verification Sources:

> o FDP brochures, schedules, attendance records.
>
> o Certificates issued, program reports.
>
> o Funding approval letters (AICTE/UGC/DST).

**Why It Matters**

· Strong FDP culture ensures faculty are innovation mentors, not just teachers.

· Builds institutional capacity for entrepreneurship education.

· Enhances the ecosystem by creating faculty leaders in innovation.

**61. Pre-Incubation (PI)**

**Formula**

· PI = 30 × f(Npi)

> o Npi = Weighted count of pre-incubation activities supported by the institution over the last 3 years.
>
> o Includes idea-stage support, mentoring, seed support, hackathons, and prototype development facilities.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to ensure fair scoring across different institutions.
>
> o Full 30 Marks awarded when benchmark value (set by NIRF) is reached/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Support at Ideation Stage**

> o Tracks the number and quality of student/faculty ideas supported before formal incubation.
>
> o Ensures innovation ranking does not ignore early-stage entrepreneurship pipeline.

2\. **Activities Included**

> o Pre-incubation cells (idea labs, entrepreneurship cells, innovation clubs).
>
> o Hackathons, bootcamps, pitch events.
>
> o Seed/mini grants for early-stage prototyping.
>
> o Mentoring & networking programs.

3\. **Pipeline Development**

> o Shows how effectively the institution converts ideas → prototypes → incubated startups.
>
> o Strong pre-incubation support = more sustainable incubation outcomes.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution conducted:

> o 25 hackathons/ideathons (weight = 0.5 each → 12.5)
>
> o 40 student ideas funded (weight = 1 each → 40)
>
> o 15 prototypes developed in idea labs (weight = 1.5 each → 22.5)

· Weighted total = 75

· If benchmark = 100 → normalized ratio = 0.75

· PI = 30 × 0.75 = 22.5 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Number of pre-incubation programs conducted.
>
> o Number of student/faculty ideas supported.
>
> o Evidence of prototype development at idea stage.

· Verification Sources:

> o Event reports, hackathon participation lists.
>
> o EDC/Innovation Cell records.
>
> o Utilization certificates for idea-stage grants.

**Why It Matters**

· Encourages HEIs to invest in grassroots innovation culture.

· Recognizes student ideation and early-stage entrepreneurship activities.

· Ensures rankings reflect full innovation lifecycle, not just mature startups.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Number of Pre-Incubation Programs Conducted**

> o Institution must report year-wise count of pre-incubation activities that support idea generation and entrepreneurship awareness.
>
> o Examples:
>
> ▪ Bootcamps.
>
> ▪ Hackathons.
>
> ▪ Innovation challenges.
>
> ▪ Ideation workshops.
>
> ▪ Business model competitions.

· **Number of Student/Faculty Ideas Supported**

> o Institution must report the total number of student and faculty innovation ideas formally supported through:
>
> ▪ Innovation/Entrepreneurship Development Cell (EDC/IEDC).
>
> ▪ Institutional Innovation Council (IIC).
>
> ▪ Pre-incubation labs.
>
> o Support could include mentorship, funding, workspace, or technical assistance.

· **Evidence of Prototype Development at Idea Stage**

> o Must provide proof that supported ideas led to prototype development.
>
> o Acceptable evidence:
>
> ▪ Prototype photographs/videos.
>
> ▪ Technical documentation.
>
> ▪ Lab usage records.
>
> ▪ Progress reports from innovation cell.

**Verification Sources**

· **Event Reports & Hackathon Participation Lists**

> o Official reports of events (hackathons, ideation camps) with participant/student lists.
>
> o Used to validate number of programs conducted.

· **EDC/Innovation Cell Records**

> o Documentation maintained by the institution's Entrepreneurship Development Cell (EDC) or IIC.
>
> o Includes idea submissions, mentorship allocation, and program reports.

· **Utilization Certificates for Idea-Stage Grants**

> o For funded ideas, utilization certificates issued by accounts/finance office confirming money spent on prototype development.
>
> o Ensures financial authenticity and transparency.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Encourages institutions to nurture innovation and entrepreneurship culture.

· Measures how effectively institutions support student/faculty ideas before incubation stage.

· Links directly with Innovation & Startup Ecosystem metrics in NIRF.

**62. Incubation (IIE)**

**Formula**

· IIE = 30 × f(Ninc)

> o Ninc = Weighted count of ventures/startups incubated at the institution in the last 3 years.
>
> o Includes student, faculty, and alumni-led startups supported by the institution's incubation center.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to standardize across institutions.
>
> o Full 30 Marks if benchmark incubated ventures are met/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Formal Incubation Output**

> o Tracks startups admitted to official incubation programs, beyond pre-incubation/idea stage.
>
> o Covers incubation support such as workspace, mentorship, access to investors, funding, and networking.

2\. **Types of Startups Considered**

> o Student Startups (enrolled students).
>
> o Faculty Startups (spin-offs, patents commercialized).
>
> o Alumni Startups (linked with the institution's incubator).
>
> o External Startups (sometimes supported but need evidence of institutional role).

3\. **Depth of Incubation**

> o Recognizes institutions with operational incubation centers, TBIs, or Atal Incubation Centers (AICs).
>
> o Rewards HEIs that build startups beyond prototypes, supporting growth, funding, and market entry.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution incubated in 3 years:

> o 15 student startups (weight = 1 each = 15)
>
> o 6 faculty spin-offs (weight = 2 each = 12)
>
> o 4 alumni startups (weight = 1.5 each = 6)

· Weighted total = 33

· Benchmark = 40 incubations

· Ratio = 33 ÷ 40 = 0.825

· IIE = 30 × 0.825 = 24.75 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise list of incubated startups.
>
> o Type of startup (student/faculty/alumni/external).
>
> o Evidence of incubation support (MoUs, agreements, reports).

· Verification Sources:

> o Incubation center records, MoUs with TBIs.
>
> o Annual reports of Atal Incubation Centers, DST/DBT incubators.
>
> o Investor agreements or mentorship logs.

**Why It Matters**

· Moves beyond idea validation (pre-incubation) to formal incubation success.

· Encourages institutions to maintain incubation infrastructure, mentors, and networks.

· Strengthens India's startup ecosystem through academia-driven entrepreneurship.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Year-wise List of Incubated Startups**

> o Institution must maintain and report a year-wise record of all startups incubated.
>
> o Data should include:
>
> ▪ Startup Name.
>
> ▪ Founders (student/faculty/alumni/external).
>
> ▪ Year of incubation.
>
> ▪ Sector/domain (e.g., EdTech, AgriTech, HealthTech).

· **Type of Startup (Student / Faculty / Alumni / External)**

> o Classification based on the founder's relationship with the institution:
>
> ▪ Student Startup → Started by current students.
>
> ▪ Faculty Startup → Initiated by faculty members.
>
> ▪ Alumni Startup → Founded by institution's graduates.
>
> ▪ External Startup → Incubated under institutional incubation cell but founders are outside the institution.

· **Evidence of Incubation Support**

> o Must provide proof of institutional support:
>
> ▪ MoUs/agreements signed with the startup.
>
> ▪ Infrastructure/office space allocation.
>
> ▪ Mentorship assignment records.
>
> ▪ Progress or incubation reports.

**Verification Sources**

· **Incubation Center Records**

> o Maintained by Entrepreneurship/Innovation Cell or Technology Business Incubator (TBI).
>
> o Includes agreements, allocation letters, progress reports.

· **MoUs with TBIs**

> o If institution partners with an external TBI (e.g., Atal Incubation Center, DST/DBT Incubators), MoUs serve as evidence.

· **Annual Reports of Atal Incubation Centers, DST/DBT Incubators**

> o Third-party verification via government-funded incubators.
>
> o Confirms authenticity of incubation claims.

· **Investor Agreements or Mentorship Logs**

> o If startups raised funds or had structured mentorship, those agreements/logs can be submitted as verification.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Startup incubation reflects an institution's entrepreneurship ecosystem strength.

· Encourages institutions to support students/faculty in idea-to-market journeys.

· Strengthens the Innovation & Entrepreneurship metric in NIRF.

**63. Patent Commercialization & Technology Transfer**

**Formula**

· PCTT = 10 × f(Ntt)

> o Ntt = Number of patents licensed / technologies transferred with verified agreements and revenue generation in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to balance across disciplines and institutional sizes.
>
> o Full 10 Marks if benchmark (set by NIRF) is met/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Beyond Filing & Granting**

> o Recognizes actual utilization of patents and technologies by industry or startups.
>
> o Encourages institutions to move beyond patent counts toward real-world impact.

2\. **Revenue from IP Commercialization**

> o Tracks whether patents/technologies generate royalties, licensing fees, or equity.
>
> o Directly links innovation to financial sustainability.

3\. **Industry-Academia Collaboration**

> o Demonstrates how effectively an institution's research serves industry needs.
>
> o Builds institutional reputation as a technology provider.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution commercialized 8 patents in 3 years.

· Generated licensing revenue of ₹2 Cr.

· If benchmark = 10 patents/technologies commercialized:

> o Ratio = 8 ÷ 10 = 0.8
>
> o PCTT = 10 × 0.8 = 8 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise list of patents/technologies transferred.
>
> o Licensee/industry partner details.
>
> o Revenue generated (royalty, lump-sum, equity).

· Verification Sources:

> o Licensing agreements/MoUs with industry.
>
> o IPR cell records.
>
> o Royalty receipts / audited financial statements.

**Why It Matters**

· Ensures patents don't remain dormant but move into industrial application.

· Rewards institutions for converting research into tangible products/services.

· Encourages innovation-to-market ecosystem inside HEIs.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **Year-wise List of Patents/Technologies Transferred**

> o Institution must provide a year-wise record of all patents and technologies that were:
>
> ▪ Licensed to industry partners.
>
> ▪ Transferred for commercial use.
>
> ▪ Assigned to startups or incubated enterprises.
>
> o Each entry should include: Patent/Technology Title, Patent No./Application ID, and Year of Transfer.

· **Licensee/Industry Partner Details**

> o For each transfer, details of the licensee must be provided:
>
> ▪ Company/industry partner/startup name.
>
> ▪ Nature of partnership (exclusive/non-exclusive license, assignment).
>
> ▪ MoU/contract reference.

· **Revenue Generated**

> o Institution must provide evidence of financial/commercial benefit from technology transfer:
>
> ▪ Royalty payments (ongoing revenue).
>
> ▪ Lump-sum licensing fee (one-time).
>
> ▪ Equity shares in startups/partner companies.
>
> o Data should be reported year-wise.

**Verification Sources**

· **Licensing Agreements/MoUs with Industry**

> o Signed legal documents showing terms of technology transfer or licensing.
>
> o Used to confirm that transfer actually took place.

· **IPR Cell Records**

> o Institutional IPR/Technology Transfer Office maintains detailed records of patents, licensing, and commercialization.
>
> o Used as primary institutional evidence.

· **Royalty Receipts / Audited Financial Statements**

> o Proof of revenue generation from technology transfer.
>
> o Cross-verified with institutional audited accounts.
>
> o Ensures claims are backed by financial evidence.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Commercialization of IP reflects industry linkage, innovation strength, and technology transfer capability.

· Institutions generating revenue through IP transfer score higher under Research & Professional Practice (RP).

· Encourages institutions to move beyond publications/patents into real-world impact and commercialization.

**64. IPR & Patents (IPR)**

**Formula**

· IPR = IPP + IPG

> o IPP = 15 × f(Pp) → Patents Published (applications filed & published in patent offices).
>
> o IPG = 25 × f(Pg) → Patents Granted (legally awarded patents in India or abroad).
>
> o Pp = Number of patents published in the last 3 years.
>
> o Pg = Number of patents granted in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF across institutions.
>
> o Maximum = 40 Marks.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Innovation Output Beyond Publications**

> o Recognizes intellectual property generation by institutions.
>
> o Patents show applied research, innovation, and commercialization potential.

2\. **Balance Between Filed & Granted Patents**

> o Publishing (filing) patents shows early innovation activity.
>
> o Granted patents reflect strength, novelty, and legal recognition of innovations.

3\. **Commercial & Societal Impact**

> o Patents granted are strong indicators of commercial viability and innovation readiness.
>
> o Motivates institutions to push research towards technology transfer.

**Scoring Example**

· Patents Published in 3 years = 50

· Patents Granted in 3 years = 15

· If benchmark = 100 published and 20 granted:

> o IPP = 20 × (50 ÷ 100) = 10
>
> o IPG = 20 × (15 ÷ 20) = 15

· IPR = 10 + 15 = 25 Marks (out of 40)

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Third Party + Institution):

> o Published Patents: National (Indian Patent Office) + International (WIPO, USPTO, EPO).
>
> o Granted Patents: Patent office records.

· Verification Sources:

> o IP India database, WIPO Patentscope, USPTO, EPO portals.
>
> o Institutional IPR cell records.
>
> o Patent filing/award certificates.

**Why It Matters**

· Pushes institutions towards applied, industry-relevant research.

· Recognizes both innovation activity (filing) and successful recognition (grants).

· Encourages startups, technology transfer, and commercialization culture in HEIs.

**65. Publications in SCI Journals (PU)**

**Formula**

· PU = 25 × f(Psci / FRQ)

> o Psci = Weighted number of publications indexed in SCI (Science Citation Index) journals in the last 3 years.
>
> o FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max(required faculty as per FSR 1:15, or actual faculty strength).
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to ensure discipline and size neutrality.
>
> o Full 25 Marks if the institution achieves/exceeds the NIRF benchmark ratio.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Research Output Quality**

> o Focuses only on SCI-indexed publications, considered more credible and impactful.
>
> o Distinguishes from general Scopus/UGC-indexed papers by emphasizing global standard journals.

2\. **Faculty Productivity**

> o Normalized by FRQ (faculty strength), ensuring larger institutions don't get undue advantage.
>
> o Measures average publications per faculty in SCI journals.

3\. **Academic & Research Prestige**

> o SCI publications reflect international recognition, peer-reviewed standards, and higher impact factors.
>
> o Strengthens institution's global research visibility.

**Scoring Example**

· Faculty Reference Quantity (FRQ) = 200

· Publications in SCI journals (last 3 years, weighted) = 350

· Ratio = 350 ÷ 200 = 1.75

· If benchmark ratio = 2.0 → normalized score = 1.75 ÷ 2.0 = 0.875

· PU Score = 25 × 0.875 = 21.9 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Third Party):

> o Web of Science (SCI database).
>
> o Institution affiliation-based publication counts.

· Institutional Records (supporting):

> o Faculty-wise publication list with DOI/journal link.
>
> o Annual reports with publication statistics.

**Why It Matters**

· SCI publications are internationally respected, hence this metric ensures global benchmarking.

· Rewards institutions with research-active faculty and strong publication culture.

· Pushes HEIs to focus on quality over quantity in research publications.

**Primary Data (Third Party)**

· **Web of Science (SCI Database)**

> o Clarivate's Science Citation Index (SCI) is the official source for validated publication counts.
>
> o SCI covers high-quality, peer-reviewed journals across disciplines.
>
> o Data is extracted institution-wise using:
>
> ▪ Affiliation ID / Organization-Enhanced ID in Web of Science.
>
> ▪ Ensures accurate attribution of papers to the reporting institution.

· **Institution Affiliation-Based Publication Counts**

> o Publication numbers are not self-declared but retrieved based on institutional affiliation.
>
> o Ensures that:
>
> ▪ Only publications credited to the institution are counted.
>
> ▪ Prevents misattribution (faculty publishing under different or older affiliations).
>
> o Filters applied:
>
> ▪ Publication type: Articles, Reviews, Proceedings.
>
> ▪ Year range: Last 3 years (e.g., 2022–2024 for NIRF 2025).

**Institutional Records (Supporting Data)**

· **Faculty-Wise Publication List**

> o Institution must maintain an internal list of publications with:
>
> ▪ Faculty name.
>
> ▪ Publication title.
>
> ▪ Journal name.
>
> ▪ DOI / Permanent link.
>
> ▪ Source (WoS, Scopus, PubMed).
>
> o Helps reconcile external bibliometric data with institutional records.

· **Annual Reports with Publication Statistics**

> o Consolidated report showing total publications per year.
>
> o Acts as supporting evidence when cross-verified with Web of Science exports.

**Verification**

· **Web of Science Reports (Third Party Verified)**

> o Considered the gold standard for NIRF bibliometric data.
>
> o Direct extraction prevents manipulation of publication counts.

· **Cross-check with Institutional Records**

> o Internal faculty-wise list ensures no missing data.
>
> o Annual reports ensure consistency with declared outputs.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Publication count is a major component of Research & Professional Practice (RP).

· Web of Science ensures authenticity and comparability across institutions.

· Institutional lists provide transparency and accountability.

**66. Quality of Publications (CI)**

**Formula**

· CI = 25 × f(Citations/FRQ)

> o Citations = Total citation count of SCI publications over the last 3 years (sourced from Web of Science or other SCI databases).
>
> o FRQ = Faculty Reference Quantity = max(required faculty strength as per 1:15 FSR, or actual faculty strength).
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to discipline/size differences.
>
> o Full 25 Marks when the normalized ratio meets/exceeds benchmark values.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Research Impact, Not Just Output**

> o Goes beyond publication count (PU) and evaluates how influential those publications are.
>
> o Higher citations = wider recognition, usage, and adoption of research.

2\. **Global Visibility of Research**

> o Rewards institutions whose work is cited internationally in top-tier journals.
>
> o Reflects knowledge contribution to global science & technology.

3\. **Faculty Productivity Normalized**

> o By dividing total citations with FRQ, it measures average citation impact per faculty.

**Scoring Example**

· Faculty Reference Quantity (FRQ) = 200

· Total SCI Citations (3 years) = 18,000

· Ratio = 18,000 ÷ 200 = 90 citations per faculty

· If benchmark = 100 citations/faculty → normalized ratio = 90 ÷ 100 = 0.90

· CI Score = 25 × 0.90 = 22.5 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Third Party):

> o Web of Science (SCI) citation database.
>
> o Citation counts per institution based on affiliation.

· Institutional Records (supporting):

> o Faculty publication lists with citation numbers.
>
> o Departmental research reports.

**Why It Matters**

· Captures true quality of research output by checking citation impact.

· Distinguishes institutions with globally recognized contributions vs. high-volume but low-impact publishing.

· Encourages high-impact, innovative, and collaborative research.

**Primary Data (Third Party)**

· **Web of Science (SCI Citation Database)**

> o The official and trusted source for citation data in NIRF.
>
> o Uses Science Citation Index (SCI), which covers high-quality peer-reviewed journals.
>
> o Institution's citation data is extracted using its affiliation ID / Organization-Enhanced ID.

· **Citation Counts per Institution (Affiliation-Based)**

> o Total citations of all publications linked to the institution in WoS are considered.
>
> o Ensures accurate attribution and avoids double-counting.
>
> o Only citations from last 3 years' publications are typically considered for NIRF.
>
> o Provides normalized citation impact when compared with faculty strength.

**Institutional Records (Supporting Data)**

· **Faculty Publication Lists with Citation Numbers**

> o Institution must maintain faculty-wise publication records including:
>
> ▪ Publication title.
>
> ▪ Journal name.
>
> ▪ Year of publication.
>
> ▪ Citation count (as per WoS/Scopus/Google Scholar).
>
> o Acts as supporting evidence for third-party bibliometric data.

· **Departmental Research Reports**

> o Departments compile annual research reports listing publications and citations.
>
> o Helps verify institutional-level data with department-level contributions.
>
> o Often includes graphs/tables showing growth in citations.

**Verification**

· **WoS/SCI Citation Database (Third Party)**

> o NIRF extracts official citation counts directly from Web of Science.
>
> o Prevents manipulation or inflation of institutional citation claims.

· **Cross-Check with Institutional Records**

> o Internal faculty lists and departmental reports ensure alignment.
>
> o Differences are reconciled via DOI-level checking.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Citation count is a direct indicator of research quality and global impact.

· Used in the Research & Professional Practice (RP) parameter.

· Encourages institutions to publish in high-impact, well-cited journals.

**67. Ventures/Startups Recognized by DPIIT/Startup India (SU)**

**Formula**

· SU = 20 × f(Nsu)

> o Nsu = Number of ventures/startups officially recognized by DPIIT/Startup India (cumulative or in the last 3 years, as per NIRF's data format).
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to ensure comparability across institutions of different sizes.
>
> o Full 20 marks when the institution achieves or exceeds NIRF's benchmark value (discipline/size-specific).

**What It Captures**

1\. **Recognition of Institutional Startups**

> o Measures the number of student/faculty/startup incubated ventures that are officially registered under DPIIT/Startup India portal.
>
> o Confirms that ventures meet eligibility criteria (innovative product/service, scalability, \<10 years old, etc.).

2\. **Institutional Entrepreneurial Ecosystem**

> o Reflects the capacity of the HEI to create job-creators, not just job-seekers.
>
> o Encourages incubation, mentoring, seed funding, and innovation policies.

3\. **Policy Alignment**

> o Aligns with Government of India's Startup India initiative.
>
> o Demonstrates contribution to the Atmanirbhar Bharat and innovation-driven economy.

**Scoring Example**

· Suppose NIRF benchmark = 50 recognized startups.

· Institution reports 35 DPIIT-recognized startups.

· Ratio = 35 ÷ 50 = 0.70

· SU Score = 20 × 0.70 = 14 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o Year-wise list of recognized startups, with Startup India/DPIIT recognition certificates.
>
> o Founders (student/faculty/alumni), sector, date of registration, and incubation support details.

· Verification Sources:

> o DPIIT/Startup India portal (https://www.startupindia.gov.in/).
>
> o Institutional Incubation Cell/EDC records.
>
> o MoUs with accelerators, incubators, or DST-supported TBI/STEP.

**Why It Matters**

· Demonstrates real-world entrepreneurial success emerging from the campus.

· Strengthens industry–academia–government linkages.

· Adds value to regional/national innovation ecosystem.

**68. Ventures/Startups Receiving VC Investment (VCI)**

**Formula**

· VCI = 25 × f(Nvci)

> o Nvci = Number of institutional startups/ventures that received verified venture capital (VC) funding in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to balance institutions of different sizes.
>
> o Full 25 Marks when an institution achieves/exceeds the NIRF benchmark number of VC-backed startups.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Access to Private Capital**

> o Indicates whether startups incubated at the institution are commercially attractive to investors.
>
> o Demonstrates scalability, product-market fit, and growth readiness of student/faculty ventures.

2\. **Validation of Startup Quality**

> o VC investment serves as a third-party validation of the innovation ecosystem's quality.
>
> o Attracting VC funds means startups are beyond prototype stage and are ready for market expansion.

3\. **Institutional Innovation Strength**

> o Encourages institutions to not only incubate startups but also support them towards fundraising through mentorship, demo days, investor networks.

**Scoring Example**

· Suppose NIRF benchmark = 20 startups with VC investment.

· Institution reports 12 startups that raised VC in the last 3 years.

· Ratio = 12 ÷ 20 = 0.60

· VCI Score = 25 × 0.60 = 15 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of startups incubated/linked with institution that raised VC funding.
>
> o Year of funding, startup founders, sector, amount invested.

· Verification Sources:

> o VC funding agreements, investor press releases, official filings (MCA, SEBI).
>
> o Certificates/letters from VC firms.
>
> o Institutional incubation records and MoUs with investors.

**Why It Matters**

· VC-backed startups are more likely to scale, create jobs, and attract global recognition.

· Demonstrates real-world impact of institutional innovation ecosystems.

· Encourages a culture of entrepreneurship + external capital infusion.

**69. FDI Investment in Ventures/Startups (FDII)**

**Formula**

· FDII = 25 × f(Nfdi)

> o Nfdi = Number of institutional ventures/startups that have received verified Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function used by NIRF to balance across institutions of different sizes and disciplines.
>
> o Full 25 Marks when the institution reaches or exceeds NIRF's benchmark for FDI-backed ventures.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Global Investor Confidence**

> o Shows the ability of institutional startups to attract international investors, demonstrating world-class scalability and global appeal.

2\. **Internationalization of Innovation**

> o Reflects how well startups are integrated into global value chains.
>
> o FDI typically comes with technology transfer, partnerships, and global market access.

3\. **Maturity of Innovation Ecosystem**

> o Encourages institutions to help startups build credibility, legal compliance, and global networks needed to raise FDI.

**Scoring Example**

· Benchmark = 10 startups with FDI.

· Institution reports 6 startups with verified FDI inflows.

· Ratio = 6 ÷ 10 = 0.6

· FDII Score = 25 × 0.6 = 15 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of ventures/startups incubated or supported by the institution that received FDI.
>
> o Year of investment, startup founders, sector, amount received.

· Verification Sources:

> o RBI filings under FEMA (Foreign Exchange Management Act).
>
> o MCA filings with FDI entries.
>
> o Official FDI receipts, foreign investment agreements.
>
> o Investor press releases and due diligence reports.

**Why It Matters**

· Attracting FDI is a strong indicator of global competitiveness.

· Demonstrates that institutional startups meet international standards of innovation and governance.

· Strengthens India's positioning in global innovation rankings.

**70. Innovation Grants from Government (IG)**

**Formula**

· IG = 30 × f(Nig)

> o Nig = Total number/value of institutional ventures/startups that received verified Innovation Grants from Central/State Government agencies in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function used by NIRF to balance different institution sizes.
>
> o Full 30 Marks are awarded if the institution's startups achieve or exceed the benchmark grant level (amount/startups count) specified by NIRF.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Public Sector Support for Innovation**

> o Measures the extent to which the institution's startups/ventures have been able to win competitive grants from government schemes.
>
> o Indicates credibility and impact of institutional innovation.

2\. **Government-Industry-Academia Alignment**

> o Encourages startups to leverage flagship programs such as:
>
> ▪ DST NIDHI/PRAYAS
>
> ▪ DBT-BIRAC BIG Grants
>
> ▪ MeitY TIDE Grants
>
> ▪ AICTE IDEA Labs
>
> ▪ MSME Seed Grants
>
> ▪ State Innovation Funds

3\. **Risk-free Seed Funding**

> o Unlike VC/FDI, government grants are non-dilutive funding.
>
> o Promotes early-stage prototyping and technology validation.

**Scoring Example**

· Suppose NIRF benchmark = ₹20 Cr of grants awarded across institutional startups in 3 years.

· Institution reports ₹12 Cr.

· Ratio = 12 ÷ 20 = 0.6

· IG Score = 30 × 0.6 = 18 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of startups/ventures that received grants.
>
> o Agency name, year, grant amount, purpose.

· Verification Sources:

> o Sanction letters from ministries (DST, DBT, MeitY, MSME, AICTE, State Govts.).
>
> o Disbursement proofs, utilization certificates.
>
> o Annual reports of incubators/innovation councils.

**Why It Matters**

· Recognizes startups that successfully compete for public grants.

· Strengthens innovation pipelines at the idea/prototype stage.

· Signals institutional support in helping students/faculty apply for and win government innovation schemes.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **List of Startups/Ventures that Received Grants**

> o Institution must maintain a year-wise list of startups/ventures incubated or supported by the institution that secured external grants.
>
> o Each entry should clearly mention:
>
> ▪ Startup/Venture name.
>
> ▪ Founder details (student, faculty, alumni, or external).
>
> ▪ Year of grant approval.
>
> ▪ Sector/domain (e.g., AgriTech, HealthTech, AI/IoT).

· **Agency Name, Year, Grant Amount, Purpose**

> o Must include details of funding agency such as:
>
> ▪ DST (Department of Science & Technology).
>
> ▪ DBT (Department of Biotechnology).
>
> ▪ MeitY (Ministry of Electronics & IT).
>
> ▪ MSME (Micro, Small & Medium Enterprises).
>
> ▪ AICTE, State Government Schemes, CSR funds, etc.
>
> o Reported data should cover:
>
> ▪ Year of sanction.
>
> ▪ Grant amount (₹).
>
> ▪ Purpose/Project Title (prototype development, commercialization, scaling up, etc.).

**Verification Sources**

· **Sanction Letters from Ministries/Agencies**

> o Official sanction/approval letters issued by funding agencies.
>
> o Confirms legitimacy of the grant.

· **Disbursement Proofs, Utilization Certificates**

> o Bank receipts and financial statements proving grant disbursal.
>
> o Utilization Certificates (UCs) signed by institution accounts confirming fund usage.

· **Annual Reports of Incubators/Innovation Councils**

> o Incubation cells and IIC (Institution's Innovation Council) include grant details in their annual reports.
>
> o Used as supporting evidence for NIRF reporting.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Reflects institutional ecosystem support for entrepreneurship and innovation.

· Measures how effectively institutions attract government and external funding for startups.

· Adds to Research & Professional Practice (RP) and Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) scores.

**71. Innovations at Various Technology Readiness Levels (ITRL)**

**Formula**

· ITRL = 40 × f(Nitr)

> o Nitr = Weighted count of innovations/projects developed at different TRL stages in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF, ensuring fairness across institution size and sector.
>
> o Full 40 Marks are awarded when an institution achieves or exceeds the benchmark number of innovations across TRLs.

**Technology Readiness Levels (TRL Framework)**

The TRL framework is used worldwide (NASA/DoD, now adopted in India for innovation ranking) to track maturity of innovations.

1\. TRL 1–2: Idea, basic principles observed, concept formulated.

2\. TRL 3–4: Proof of concept validated, lab-scale experimentation.

3\. TRL 5–6: Prototyping, validation in simulated/real environment.

4\. TRL 7–8: Pilot production, system prototype demonstrated.

5\. TRL 9: Full deployment, innovation commercially operational.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Depth of Innovation Pipeline**

> o Recognizes innovations/projects across the entire lifecycle, from ideation to commercialization.

2\. **Balanced Ecosystem**

> o Rewards institutions that don't just focus on ideas, but also advance technologies toward market-ready solutions.

3\. **Maturity Tracking**

> o Captures whether institutions are creating commercially viable outputs (TRL 7–9) in addition to academic prototypes.

**Scoring Method (Indicative)**

· Each innovation is assigned a weight based on TRL stage (higher TRL = more weight).

· Example (illustrative weights):

> o TRL 1–3 = 0.5 marks per innovation
>
> o TRL 4–6 = 1.0 mark per innovation
>
> o TRL 7–8 = 2.0 marks per innovation
>
> o TRL 9 = 3.0 marks per innovation

· Total weighted score → normalized → scaled to 40 Marks.

**Scoring Example**

· Institution reports:

> o 10 innovations at TRL 1–3 → 10 × 0.5 = 5
>
> o 8 innovations at TRL 4–6 → 8 × 1.0 = 8
>
> o 4 innovations at TRL 7–8 → 4 × 2.0 = 8
>
> o 2 innovations at TRL 9 → 2 × 3.0 = 6

· Weighted total = 27

· If benchmark = 30, normalized ratio = 27 ÷ 30 = 0.9

· ITRL = 40 × 0.9 = 36 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of innovations, TRL classification, year, sector.
>
> o Evidence of TRL status (lab reports, prototype demos, pilot studies, commercialization proof).

· Verification Sources:

> o Institutional IPR cell, incubator records.
>
> o Technology transfer/licensing agreements.
>
> o Product deployment certificates.

**Why It Matters**

· Captures not just innovation quantity, but quality and maturity.

· Encourages institutions to push more innovations from labs to market.

· Demonstrates impact of academia-driven innovation in the national economy and industry adoption.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **List of Innovations with TRL Classification**

> o Institution must maintain a record of innovations and technologies developed, categorized as per Technology Readiness Levels (TRL 1–9).
>
> o Each entry should include:
>
> ▪ Innovation/Technology Name.
>
> ▪ TRL Stage (from concept → prototype → commercialization).
>
> ▪ Year of development/advancement.
>
> ▪ Sector/Domain (e.g., HealthTech, AgriTech, Energy, AI/ML).

· **Evidence of TRL Status**

> o Institutions must provide documentation corresponding to the TRL stage:
>
> ▪ TRL 3–4: Lab test reports, experimental proof of concept.
>
> ▪ TRL 5–6: Prototype demo reports, pilot testing in relevant environments.
>
> ▪ TRL 7–8: Field deployment, beta testing, validation with industry/government.
>
> ▪ TRL 9: Proof of commercialization, product deployment certificate, sales agreements.

**Verification Sources**

· **Institutional IPR Cell / Incubator Records**

> o Maintain official logs of innovation projects and their TRL classification.
>
> o Provide authentication of innovation ownership.

· **Technology Transfer/Licensing Agreements**

> o Signed agreements with industry or startups for scaling and commercialization.
>
> o Evidence of IP movement beyond lab.

· **Product Deployment Certificates**

> o Proof that the innovation has been deployed or commercialized.
>
> o Can include user certificates, purchase orders, or industry validation reports.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· TRL reporting demonstrates how institutions translate research into real-world technologies.

· Encourages innovation beyond publications → towards prototypes, pilots, and commercialization.

· Forms part of Research & Professional Practice (RP) and Innovation Ecosystem metrics.

**72. Ventures/Startups with Turnover ≥ ₹50 Lakhs (NVS)**

**Formula**

· NVS = 40 × f(Nturn)

> o Nturn = Number of institutional ventures/startups with verified annual turnover ≥ ₹50 lakhs in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to compare across institutions.
>
> o Full 40 Marks when the institution meets/exceeds the NIRF benchmark count.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Commercial Success of Startups**

> o Goes beyond incubation and early-stage funding to measure actual market performance.
>
> o A turnover ≥ ₹50 lakhs indicates revenue traction, product-market fit, and customer base.

2\. **Job Creation & Market Validation**

> o Startups achieving this milestone are usually scaling businesses, generating employment and contributing to the economy.

3\. **Institutional Ecosystem Effectiveness**

> o Rewards HEIs that don't just produce ideas but viable companies.
>
> o Demonstrates strength of mentoring, incubation, and alumni entrepreneurship.

**Scoring Example**

· Suppose NIRF benchmark = 10 such startups.

· Institution reports 7 startups with turnover ≥ ₹50 lakhs.

· Ratio = 7 ÷ 10 = 0.7

· NVS Score = 40 × 0.7 = 28 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of startups incubated/supported by the institution with turnover ≥ ₹50 lakhs.
>
> o Year-wise turnover, founders, sector.

· Verification Sources:

> o Audited financial statements.
>
> o CA-certified turnover certificates.
>
> o MCA filings (Ministry of Corporate Affairs).
>
> o GST returns (if available).

**Why It Matters**

· Recognizes real business outcomes from academic innovation ecosystems.

· Distinguishes institutions where startups achieve financial sustainability and scale.

· Encourages HEIs to focus on quality startups, not just numbers.

**73. Alumni Founders of Forbes/Fortune 500 Companies (AFF)**

**Formula**

· AFF = 20 × f(Naff)

> o Naff = Number of alumni who are recognized founders/co-founders of companies listed in Forbes Global/India 2000 or Fortune 500 (Global/India) rankings.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to standardize across institutions.
>
> o Full 20 Marks if an institution reports alumni founders equal to or above the benchmark set by NIRF.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Long-term Alumni Impact**

> o Measures how an institution's alumni have contributed to creating globally influential enterprises.
>
> o Focuses on legacy, leadership, and large-scale impact beyond academia.

2\. **Brand Prestige**

> o Alumni with Forbes/Fortune 500 companies enhance institutional reputation globally.
>
> o Demonstrates the institution's role in nurturing world-class entrepreneurial leaders.

3\. **Innovation & Economic Contribution**

> o Recognizes alumni-driven companies that have massive turnover, employment, and innovation footprint.

**Scoring Example**

· Benchmark = 5 alumni founders in Forbes/Fortune 500 firms.

· Institution reports 3 verified alumni.

· Ratio = 3 ÷ 5 = 0.6

· AFF Score = 20 × 0.6 = 12 Marks

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of alumni recognized as founders/co-founders of Forbes/Fortune 500 companies.
>
> o Year of recognition, company name, role, graduation details.

· Verification Sources:

> o Forbes Global/India 2000 list, Fortune 500 list (Global/India).
>
> o Official company websites, press releases.
>
> o Alumni association confirmations.
>
> o LinkedIn/Business registries (secondary).

**Why It Matters**

· Highlights the prestige and legacy of an institution in shaping leaders of global enterprises.

· Encourages institutions to strengthen alumni networks, entrepreneurship support, and long-term innovation ecosystems.

· Serves as a powerful branding metric in rankings.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **List of Alumni Recognized as Founders/Co-Founders**

> o Institution must compile a list of notable alumni who are founders, co-founders, or top executives of companies listed in:
>
> ▪ Forbes Global 2000
>
> ▪ Fortune 500 (Global/India)
>
> o Required details:
>
> ▪ Alumni Name.
>
> ▪ Graduation Details (Year, Program, Department).
>
> ▪ Company Name.
>
> ▪ Role (Founder/Co-Founder/CEO/Executive).
>
> ▪ Year of Recognition (when company appeared in Forbes/Fortune list).

**Verification Sources**

· **Forbes Global 2000 / Fortune 500 Lists**

> o Official annual lists (Global and India editions).
>
> o Used to confirm whether the company is listed.

· **Official Company Websites & Press Releases**

> o Validate alumni role (Founder/Co-Founder/CEO).
>
> o Confirms ongoing association.

· **Alumni Association Confirmations**

> o Alumni cell/association maintains records of notable alumni.
>
> o Can provide official letters confirming alumni achievements.

· **LinkedIn / Business Registries (Secondary Sources)**

> o Secondary validation of alumni's current position and role.
>
> o Examples: LinkedIn profiles, Crunchbase, Bloomberg company registries.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· Highlights institution's global alumni impact.

· Strengthens the perception metric under Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) and Research & Professional Practice (RP).

· Demonstrates contribution of the institution to entrepreneurship, leadership, and innovation at global scale.

**74. Participation in Smart India Hackathon (SIH)**

**Formula**

· SIH = 30 × f(Nsih)

> o Nsih = Number of student teams/faculty mentors from the institution that participated in Smart India Hackathon (Software + Hardware editions) in the last 3 years.
>
> o f() = Normalization function applied by NIRF to scale participation across institutions.
>
> o Full 30 Marks when benchmark (set by NIRF/MIC) is achieved/exceeded.

**What It Captures**

1\. **Student Innovation & Problem-Solving Skills**

> o Tracks participation in national-level hackathons solving real-world problems from ministries, industries, and NGOs.
>
> o Ensures students are exposed to applied innovation challenges.

2\. **Institutional Engagement in National Missions**

> o Smart India Hackathon is a flagship innovation program under MoE's Innovation Cell.
>
> o Participation indicates that the institution aligns with national innovation priorities.

3\. **Outputs Considered**

> o No. of teams selected for national rounds.
>
> o No. of winners (cash prizes, projects implemented).
>
> o Industry/government adoption of student solutions.

**Scoring Example**

· Benchmark = 20 teams participating across 3 years.

· Institution reported = 12 teams (8 software + 4 hardware).

· Ratio = 12 ÷ 20 = 0.6.

· SIH Score = 30 × 0.6 = 18 Marks.

**Data Requirements**

· Primary Data (Institution):

> o List of participating teams, domains, and problem statements.
>
> o Year-wise details of participation, awards won, or project adoptions.

· Verification Sources:

> o SIH portal (https://sih.gov.in).
>
> o Certificates, awards, MoE's IIC reports.

**Why It Matters**

· Encourages students to apply knowledge to solve national problems.

· Builds a culture of team-based innovation and entrepreneurship.

· Provides exposure to industries, government agencies, and startups.

**Primary Data (Institution)**

· **List of Participating Teams, Domains, and Problem Statements**

> o Institution must maintain a record of all teams that participated in SIH (Software & Hardware editions).
>
> o Data should include:
>
> ▪ Team Name & Members (student/faculty mentors).
>
> ▪ Domain/Category (e.g., AI/ML, HealthTech, AgriTech, IoT, Cybersecurity).
>
> ▪ Problem Statement (provided by industry/government).
>
> ▪ Year of participation.

· **Year-wise Details of Participation, Awards, or Project Adoptions**

> o Must provide year-wise record for last 3 years.
>
> o Data points:
>
> ▪ Total teams shortlisted/finalists.
>
> ▪ Awards won (cash prize, recognition).
>
> ▪ Problem statements solved and adopted by industry/government.
>
> ▪ Proof of implementation (MoU, pilot project).

**Verification Sources**

· **SIH Portal (https://sih.gov.in)**

> o Official record of institution/team participation.
>
> o Validates number of teams, awards, and project adoption.

· **Certificates & Awards**

> o Certificates issued to student teams by SIH organizing committee.
>
> o Cash prize/award letters as proof.

· **MoE's IIC Reports**

> o Institutional Innovation Council (IIC) maintains reports of SIH activities.
>
> o Yearly IIC reports include details of participation and achievements.

**Why NIRF Collects This**

· SIH is a flagship national innovation initiative under MoE.

· Participation reflects institutional support for innovation, problem-solving, and industry-government collaboration.

· Awards and adoptions demonstrate real-world impact.

· Adds weight under Innovation & Startup Ecosystem metrics in NIRF.

**75. NIRF 2024 Framework for Architecture & Planning Institutions**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.40)**

Since architecture & planning education is resource-intensive, this parameter carries the highest weight (40%).

**A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

· Balanced intake across UG, PG, and Ph.D. programs.

· Reflects institutional capacity to train architects, urban planners, and designers.

**B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

· Adequacy of permanent faculty relative to student strength.

· Ensures studio-based learning is not overcrowded (important for design education).

**C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

· Rewards proportion of PhD-qualified faculty and distribution of experience levels.

· Recognizes blend of academic and professional (industry/practice) expertise.

**D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

· Per-student expenditure on design studios, workshops, laboratories, materials, ICT-enabled classrooms, and model-making facilities.

· Reflects quality of infrastructure supporting practical training.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Architecture & planning institutions are judged not only on research publications but also on professional practice and real-world projects.

**A. Publications (PU) – 60 Marks**

· Research output in urban planning, sustainable design, green buildings, housing, transport, and smart cities.

**B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 20 Marks**

· Citations, journal indexing, and relevance to architecture and planning.

**C. Footprint of Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 20 Marks**

· Consultancy projects, government/industry-sponsored design and planning work, competitions, and urban development initiatives.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Captures success of graduates in employment, higher studies, and professional practice.

**A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

· % of graduates employed in design firms, planning agencies, real estate, infrastructure consultancies, or pursuing PG/Ph.D. studies.

**B. University Examinations (GUE) – 30 Marks**

· On-time graduation rates across UG/PG programs.

**C. Median Salary (GMS) – 30 Marks**

· Median annual salary of placed graduates; benchmarked against the sector.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Evaluates diversity, access, and inclusivity.

**A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

· Enrollment of students from other states and countries.

**B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

· Participation of women students and women faculty (especially in leadership).

**C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

· Scholarships, tuition waivers, and financial support for disadvantaged students.

**D. Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

· Infrastructure and learning support for differently-abled students.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures the reputation of the institution among stakeholders.

**A. Peer Perception (PR) – 100 Marks**

· Based on surveys of employers (design firms, urban development bodies, real estate companies), academic peers, and professionals.

· Strongly influenced by design competitions won, alumni reputation, and consultancy contributions.

**Key Insights for Architecture & Planning Rankings**

· TLR dominates (40%), reflecting the studio- and resource-heavy nature of design education.

· RP has moderate weight (20%), but publications (60 marks) and professional practice (20 marks) make it unique.

· GO (20%) balances placements, salaries, and exam outcomes, with a strong emphasis on employability.

· OI (10%) ensures access and inclusivity.

· PR (10%) captures perception from both academic and professional practice circles.

**NIRF 2024 – Overall vs Architecture & Planning Framework**

**Key Differences**

· **TLR:**

> o ***Overall*** includes NEP reforms (Online Education – 10, MIR – 5).
>
> o ***Architecture & Planning*** places heavier weight (40%), prioritizing FSR (30) and FRU (30) → reflects studio/lab-intensive nature of design education.

· **RP:**

> o ***Overall*** = balanced across publications, quality, patents, projects, SDGs.
>
> o ***Architecture & Planning*** = Publications dominate (60), with quality (20) and projects/consultancy (20) → patents/SDGs not emphasized.

· **GO:**

> o ***Overall*** = Exams (60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Architecture & Planning*** = Placements (40) + Exams (30) + Median Salary (30) → practical career focus.

· **OI & PR:**

> o Identical in both frameworks.

**76. NIRF 2024 Framework for Agriculture & Allied Sectors Institutions**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

This parameter evaluates the teaching capacity, infrastructure, and resources available in agriculture-focused institutions.

**A. Student Strength (SS) – 20 Marks**

· Considers UG (B.Sc. Agri, Dairy, Horticulture, Fisheries, Forestry), PG, and Ph.D. enrollments.

· Balanced enrollment across disciplines is rewarded.

**B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

· Ensures adequate faculty presence for hands-on, field-based education.

· Optimum benchmark: 1:15.

**C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

· Rewards percentage of PhD-qualified faculty with relevant experience.

· Reflects strength in both research-based and field-extension teaching.

**D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 30 Marks**

· Evaluates per-student expenditure on labs, research farms, veterinary clinics, fisheries hatcheries, dairy units, greenhouses, and ICT-enabled learning.

· Shows institutional investment in practice-oriented agriculture training.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Agriculture institutions are research-driven, focusing on food security, biotechnology, sustainability, and rural development.

**A. Publications (PU) – 35 Marks**

· Weighted number of research publications in agriculture, life sciences, veterinary, fisheries, forestry, etc.

**B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 40 Marks**

· Citations, impact factor, and global visibility of research.

· Encourages evidence-based agricultural innovation.

**C. IPR & Patents (IPR) – 15 Marks**

· Patents in plant varieties, biotech products, agri-tech innovations.

**D. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 10 Marks**

· Sponsored projects, consultancy with agro-industries, rural extension programs, and government collaborations.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Captures how well graduates succeed in careers and higher studies.

**A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 40 Marks**

· % of students placed in agri-business firms, research labs, food industries, extension services, or admitted for PG/Ph.D. programs.

**B. University Examinations (GUE) – 15 Marks**

· Timely graduation rate.

**C. Median Salary (GMS) – 25 Marks**

· Median annual package of placed graduates.

**D. Ph.D. Students Graduated (GPHD) – 20 Marks**

· Average Ph.D. graduates over 3 years in agriculture and allied fields.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Ensures agriculture institutions serve diverse and inclusive student populations.

**A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

· Students from other states/countries enrolled.

**B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

· Share of women students and women faculty (including senior roles).

**C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

· Full fee reimbursements, scholarships, stipends to underprivileged students.

**D. Facilities for Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

· Infrastructure and assistive technologies for differently-abled students.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Reflects national reputation and credibility.

**A. Peer Perception – 100 Marks**

· Based on surveys of employers (agribusiness, biotech, food industry, government agencies), research peers, and collaborators.

· Captures recognition of graduate quality and institutional visibility.

**Key Insights for Agriculture & Allied Sectors Rankings**

· TLR (30%) and RP (30%) dominate, showing equal importance of teaching excellence and agricultural research.

· GO (20%) emphasizes placements, salaries, and Ph.D. training, critical for employability and research careers.

· OI (10%) ensures inclusivity in line with agriculture's role in rural upliftment.

· PR (10%) adds credibility, reflecting how well graduates and institutions are perceived nationally.

**NIRF 2024 – Overall vs Agriculture & Allied Sectors Framework**

**Key Differences**

· **TLR:**

> o ***Overall*** includes Online Education (10) and MIR (5) to reflect NEP-2020 reforms.
>
> o ***Agriculture*** gives more weight to FSR (30) and FRU (30), focusing on fieldwork, labs, and practice-based training.

· **RP:**

> o ***Overall*** balances across publications, quality, patents, projects, and SDGs.
>
> o ***Agriculture*** strengthens publications (35) and quality (40) while keeping IPR (15); projects (10) carry less weight, SDGs not separately tracked.

· **GO:**

> o ***Overall*** = University Exams (60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Agriculture*** = Placements (40) + Median Salary (25) + Exams (15) + Ph.D.s (20) → reflects employability and rural/agri-sector careers.

· **OI & PR:**

> o Both frameworks use the same sub-parameters and weights.

**77. NIRF 2024 Framework for Dental Institutions**

**1. Teaching, Learning & Resources (TLR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Dental education is clinical and infrastructure-intensive, hence TLR plays a significant role.

**A. Student Strength (SS) – 15 Marks**

· Enrollment across UG (BDS), PG (MDS), and doctoral levels.

· Slightly lower weight than other medical disciplines, as capacity is often restricted by regulatory norms.

**B. Faculty–Student Ratio (FSR) – 30 Marks**

· Critical in clinical fields where supervision and patient interaction need close monitoring.

· Optimum benchmark: 1:15.

**C. Faculty with PhD & Experience (FQE) – 20 Marks**

· Rewards institutions with higher percentage of PhD/MDS-qualified faculty with substantial teaching and clinical experience.

**D. Financial Resources & Utilisation (FRU) – 35 Marks**

· Higher weight compared to other categories.

· Reflects heavy investment in dental chairs, laboratories, clinics, radiology units, prosthodontic equipment, cadaver labs, and ICT resources.

**2. Research & Professional Practice (RP) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.30)**

Focuses on research strength and practical clinical outreach.

**A. Publications (PU) – 40 Marks**

· Weighted publication count in dental sciences, maxillofacial surgery, prosthodontics, orthodontics, oral pathology, etc.

**B. Quality of Publications (QP) – 40 Marks**

· Citations, journal indexing (Scopus, PubMed), h-index, and global impact.

**C. Projects & Professional Practice (FPPP) – 20 Marks**

· Funded projects, consultancy, outreach dental camps, public health dentistry initiatives.

**3. Graduation Outcomes (GO) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.20)**

Captures how effectively graduates succeed in placements, exams, and higher studies.

**A. Placement & Higher Studies (GPH) – 35 Marks**

· % of BDS/MDS graduates securing employment in clinics, hospitals, or higher education admissions.

**B. University Examinations (GUE) – 30 Marks**

· On-time graduation rate across programs.

**C. PG Graduates (GPG) – 35 Marks**

· Average number of postgraduate students graduating (MDS) over 3 years.

· High weight reflects the importance of advanced specialization in dentistry.

**4. Outreach & Inclusivity (OI) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Measures diversity, inclusivity, and equity in admissions and access.

**A. Regional Diversity (RD) – 30 Marks**

· Students enrolled from other states/countries.

**B. Women Diversity (WD) – 30 Marks**

· Women student and faculty representation (notable as dentistry sees high women participation).

**C. Economically & Socially Challenged Students (ESCS) – 20 Marks**

· Fee waivers, scholarships, and support for disadvantaged students.

**D. Physically Challenged Students (PCS) – 20 Marks**

· Accessibility infrastructure, special learning provisions.

**5. Perception (PR) – 100 Marks (Weight: 0.10)**

Assesses institutional reputation among peers and employers.

**A. Peer Perception – 100 Marks**

· Based on surveys of employers (hospitals, clinics, public health agencies), academic peers, and professional associations.

· Recognizes graduate quality, clinical reputation, and alumni performance.

**Key Insights for Dental Rankings**

· TLR (30%): Higher weight on FSR (30) and FRU (35), recognizing the need for clinical resources and faculty-intensive training.

· RP (30%): Balanced between volume and quality of research with additional emphasis on outreach and consultancy in oral health.

· GO (20%): Focus on placements, higher studies, and PG specialization (reflecting dentistry's professional pathways).

· OI (10%): Aligns with inclusivity standards, especially given high women participation.

· PR (10%): Captures external recognition in the clinical and academic ecosystem.

**NIRF 2024 – Overall vs Dental Framework**

>
> o ***Dental*** framework excludes OE/MIR, instead raising FRU (35) and slightly lowering SS (15), reflecting heavy investment in clinical infrastructure.

· **RP:**

> o ***Overall*** balances across publications, quality, patents, projects, and SDGs.
>
> o ***Dental*** framework gives stronger weight to Publications (40) and Quality (40), with Projects (20), while patents/SDGs are not emphasized.

· **GO:**

> o ***Overall*** = Exams (60) + Ph.D.s (40).
>
> o ***Dental*** = Placements (35) + Exams (30) + PG Graduates (35) → highlights employability and MDS specialization.
